_________________________________________________
PART 6: RESOLVING ISSUES AND
PRESERVING RIGHTS
_________________________________________________
INFORMATION NOTE
What this Part is about: This Part is designed to resolve issues and
questions arising in the course of a Court action. It includes rules describing
how applications are made to the Court and responded to by others, rules
for questioning on affidavits and questioning witnesses before a hearing,
and rules for preserving, protecting and obtaining evidence inside and
outside Alberta.
The Part also
- describes resources and rules available to assist the Court (experts
and referees), and
- includes special rules for replevin and interpleader proceedings.
Words and phrases used in this Part that have defined meanings in
the Appendix [Definitions] include: applications judge; Chief Justice; civil
enforcement agency; claim; Court; court clerk; defendant; enactment;
expert; file; foreclosure action; foreclosure order; health care professional;
judge; judgment; judgment creditor; judgment debtor; judicial centre;
land; lawyer; order; party; plaintiff; pleading; prescribed form; procedural
order; property; record; redemption order; referee; remedy; rules; secured
property.

6-3
PART 6: RESOLVING ISSUES AND PRESERVING RIGHTS R.6.1
DIVISION 1
APPLICATIONS TO THE COURT
What This Division Applies To
6.1 This Division
(a) applies to every application ﬁled in the Court unless a rule or an
enactment otherwise provides or the Court otherwise orders or
permits;
(b) does not apply to originating applications unless another rule
otherwise provides, the parties otherwise agree or the Court
otherwise orders.
[Alta. Reg.124/10;23/21]

Related Provisions
3.8 (originating applications) ; 12.44 (applications in family matters)

Note that only Division 1 does not apply to originating applications. The rest of Part 6 does apply.
Division 2 of Part 3 covers the same topics for originating applications. The 2021 amendment to R.
6.1(b) allows another Rule of Court to make Division 1 apply to certain Originating Applications.
Division 1 of Part 6 does apply to some family law matters: R.12.42.
If a petition for bankruptcy has some errors or gaps in it or its evidence, the fact that this is
bankruptcy under the Bankruptcy & Insolvency Act should not stymie a motion to amend the
petition proceedings to ﬁx these problems.¹
Applying Part 6 to Surrogate litigation seems complex, but it applies fairly broadly, and is
discussed at length.²
Application to the Court to Exercise Its Authority
6.2 When the Court has authority under these rules, a person may make an
application to the Court that the Court exercise its authority.

Related Provisions
1.3 (authority of the court) ; 1.4 (procedural orders generally) ; 1.5 (curing irregularities) ; 1.7

(rule by analogy) ; 3.6 (venue for applications) ; 3.65 (amending pleadings) ; 3.68 (defective
pleadings); 6.46(3) (adopting report of referee) ; 13.5 (varying time) ; 13.38 (fiats)

Footnote
1 ATB Finl. v. Coredent P’ship.2020 ABQB 507, JCE 1803 23824 (Sep 1) (¶’s 24-109).
2 Re Gow Est.2021 ABQB 305, JCStP ES14 05515 (Apr 19) (¶’s 17-50).

6-4
R.6.2 PART 6: RESOLVING ISSUES AND PRESERVING RIGHTS
A. Applications (Motions) Generally
1. References
This annotation gives some brief, basic, practical guidance. For a much fuller guide to tactics
and techniques on applications (motions), see Côté, Systematic Advocacy, Chap.9 (Juriliber
2017).
On jurisdiction of an Applications Judge, see R.6.9(2) n. On injunctions before trial, see R.
6.4n.
2. Giving Notice
(a) Delays and Problems
Applications in King’s Bench are now often about important, complex or substantive matters.
Summary judgment is an example. Written materials, proper preparation, and predictability
of timing, all matter. Having judges idle because of late adjournments or lack of readiness, is
undesirable. Full, complete preparation before selecting a date has advantages and disadvantages.
Now full preparation, or preparation before selecting a date, or becoming prepared within a very
short time after booking (on pain of losing the date booked), is the general procedure. Sometimes
the deadline is impossible to meet, so one must prepare readiness before booking. But if there is
a short deadline for applying, then the whole thing becomes impossible without a time extension,
which (if even possible) requires another application. In theory that booking preparation policy
has advantages, but when dates available to book are over a year away, that can build a lot
of dead time into an application, or even into the lawsuit. And if one of the parties is given to
procrastination, or is deliberately stalling, the delays can be very bad. It is doubtful that there is
any easy way to combat such sloth or stalling. Formerly one could cross-examine on afﬁdavits
and prepare written argument while waiting for the scheduled date, but that is usually not possible
now. Deadlines run from the ﬁling of the application (or even before), not backwards from the date
booked for hearing the application.
To make an application, one must book a date. It will likely be over a year away for a special
application; there is a long backlog. If the application is (or must be) returnable before an
applications judge, especially if it is for a special application, very often the dates are all booked
up, so there will be some wait before one can even select a date. (Sometimes a wait for dates
to become available also happens for applications returnable before a Justice. ) Dates are often
unavailable because it has not proved possible to reduce the backlog which built up during Covid.
Some categories of special application (judicial review or an appeal from an Applications Judge)
must be booked for a full day. Counsel are not supposed to book a date for an application until all
preliminary steps (such as cross-examination) are complete, and the parties are ready to ﬁle their
written briefs. Counsel learn of available dates by emailing the relevant clerk coordinator, if the
application is to be a “special”. But applications heard in ordinary morning chambers in Edmonton
and Calgary have a different calendar of available dates, and are filed by the FDS system, not
by email. From July 1, 2024, all “Family and Divorce” documents added to [the King’s Bench
Family and Divorce Filing Digital Service’s] list of Accepted Documents must be filed through that
Service. A May 7, 2024 Announcement on the Court of King’s Bench website states that and gives
a link to that list. Ones not on the list will be rejected. The Announcement gives a long list of things
not accepted. A formal Practice Note will follow. Red Deer civil chambers will also be included.

6-5
PART 6: RESOLVING ISSUES AND PRESERVING RIGHTS R.6.2
After a date is booked for the application, within a certain fairly short deadline, one must file
all the necessary papers; failing that the application will be struck off the list. There is no chance
to explain late filing, or to get a waiver of any kind for any reason.
The booking entry and the filing request are made to different people and addresses.
The filing must include all papers which will be needed to hear the application. Most of those
papers must be filed in two different places. One of those has never been announced to the public
or the profession, nor put into any part of the King’s Bench website, so far as can be discovered.
(The Clerk may write to the applicant’s solicitor and tell about that. ) This requirement to send
copies of the relevant papers to the Justice (via a court coordinator or the Justice’s assistant) is
very helpful. That is because the Justices do not have direct access to the filing software, and often
the Justice does not otherwise get from the clerks all the relevant documents for an application
which that Justice is to hear. Now King’s Bench has implemented a Court Case Management
Service (CCM) to which the Justices do have access, so parties may upload filed documents
directly to the relevant matter. But CCM does not work in reverse, and parties in the Court of
King’s Bench do not have access to all documents which are on the Court of King’s Bench file (the
way they do with Court of Appeal matters).
After an application is booked, the written briefs must be filed very soon after.
All the above can lead to difﬁculties or impossibilities in some situations, such as
a. where the applicant wants to resist the application because he or she is directly affected,
but was not named as a respondent or party;
b. where the booking was not under the proper style of cause;
c. where the current party was not the one with the duty to draft the order in question and
has no copy of it, nor knowledge whether it was formally entered, nor knowledge whether
it was ﬁled along with any papers for the application;
d. where some materials which must be ﬁled are not accessible to the current party, e. g.
transcripts of cross-examination on an afﬁdavit conducted by another party;
e. where no one has told this party of the unpublished need to ﬁle the materials a second
time to make them available for the judge who will hear the application.
So when a person wishes to respond to someone else’s booked application, before the same
judge at the same time, and that person needs some leave or amendment of the style of cause
or addition of a party, there seems to be no way to achieve that, especially if a deadline for ﬁling
is near.
Filing requests should be addressed to the right ofﬁcial, because the ﬁling work is specialized,
but learning which precise one to send them to can be difﬁcult. Sometimes each of the two or
three alternate persons says that he or she is not the right one.
This may be done in good faith because of rigid parameters for each of them. There may be
situations where there are cases not provided for. For example, it is doubtful that there is a way
to schedule an appeal from an Applications Judge (formerly Master). Yet scheduling is necessary
because there seems to be an unwritten directive that a full-day special is to be booked for such
appeals.

6-6
R.6.2 PART 6: RESOLVING ISSUES AND PRESERVING RIGHTS
(b) Slips
Even if the parties agree to dispense with a written (Notice of) Application, and just show up
together in chambers, ﬁling a formal notice is preferable, as it gives a good deal of information (as
per R.6.3(2) ).¹
Due process and knowing the case which one must meet are important. That requires a
proper written application and notice.²
An application must state the grounds or irregularity to be
relied on (citing this book). And if a key point’s evidence is unclear, the court may adjourn and
get better evidence (also citing this book).³
Not citing the old Statute of Elizabeth would ordinarily
be a deﬁciency, but not where was early notice that that statute would be relied upon, and it was
discussed during oral argument, and written argument. Unusual relief (such as restricted court
access) should be asked for well before the hearing date.⁴
If only one party moves for summary judgment (or dismissal), and fails, the other party who has
not moved is not entitled to get a reciprocal ﬁnal judgment in its favor. Still less to do so because
of a defence not raised by the parties. The Pyrrha decision in 2016 is distinguishable because
there was a pleading and two written submissions asking for the cross remedy, no objection, and
a rebuttal brief.⁵
Lack of a written application for a cross-application may not be fatal, if the party
has raised the request in its pleading and in its written chambers brief (opposing the opponent’s
application), and if the opponent makes no procedural objection, and argues the merits only
against this oral application.⁶
A chambers decision may be upset and reversed if unusual procedures adopted meant that an
important issue was not properly argued, and key facts were missing, and the relief sought orally
differed from that in the written application.⁷
Mere failure to meet a case management judge’s deadline for bringing an application (to
dismiss for want of prosecution) is not always a bar to hearing the matter on its merits, especially
if there are good reasons to have missed the deadline. Whether to extend the deadline should be
considered.⁸
An order barring further applications should not be made without warning, and a chance to
oppose it. The same is true of other nondisclosure or other orders made without notice.⁹

this was also sprung suddenly on the chambers judge. Urgency does not override procedural justice. Notice
is a fundamental principle of an adversarial system: Wiebe v. Weinrich Constr.2020 ABCA 396, Edm 1903
0139 AC (Nov 9). See also A. M. F. v. G. H. P.2022 ABKB 758, JCMH FL08 01594 (Nov 16) (¶’s 25-30, 33-

6-7
PART 6: RESOLVING ISSUES AND PRESERVING RIGHTS R.6.2
If the contents and title of a written application or notice sufﬁce, accidentally putting the wrong
form number in the upper corner works no harm and should be ignored, if no prejudice resulted.¹
When there is no existing proceeding in the Court of King’s Bench, such as an appeal from an
arbitrator or the Alberta Court of Justice, one needs to ﬁle an Originating Application, and a mere
Application (notice of motion), or a Notice to Attend Family Docket Court, is not enough. Indeed
the Notice to Attend creates nothing and cannot be cured?²
The court should not hear a constitutional law question, such as whether provincial or federal
law applies, unless both Attorneys-General have received notice of the issues so the court can
get a full record, and the governments have a chance to rebut.³
Serving a respondent with the written application is not enough; the supporting afﬁdavits must
all be served on all interested parties, so they can know and meet the case against them.⁴
3. When and Where Heard
On actual appearances before an applications judge or justice, see R.3.25 n.
Applications are heard in "chambers", which usually means a courtroom open to the public.
Applications were traditionally called "motions", and the verb is "to move" or "to apply". Counsel and
the Justice or Applications Judge do not gown for chambers, but casual dress is not appropriate
either. On when to gown, see further the 2023 bulletin from the Court of King's Bench.
As of May 17 2022, counsel had the option of appearing live in King’s Bench courtrooms, for
applications. Having one counsel personally present and the other counsel physically elsewhere,
exacerbates the drawbacks of that.⁵
Doubtless as a further consequence of the repeal of Covid
restrictions, effective September 6 2022 regular Court of King’s Bench chambers would again
hear applications for consent orders, for ﬁats, and for adjournment of regular applications (civil,
family or Applications Judge chambers), though adjournments of applications over 5 days away
could be submitted via an online form. Urgent ex parte (no-notice) applications would continue to
be heard in Urgent Matters Chambers.⁶
A May 17 2022 posting in the "News & Announcements" part of the Court of Queen's Bench
website (now King's Bench), says that effective May 24, 2022 restrictions about social distancing
and capacity limits in King's Bench courtrooms are rescinded. It also says that King's Bench
matters likely to attract or involve increased volume of people would remain virtual, e. g. lists and
family docket. All matters already scheduled as remote would remain so. Then on July 4, 2022, all
three Alberta courts ended the mandatory nature of masking and social distancing.⁷

6-8
R.6.2 PART 6: RESOLVING ISSUES AND PRESERVING RIGHTS
As of the spring of 2023, the default mode for appearances in Edmonton and Calgary, is
in person. There are guidelines for remote appearances where hearings and being conducted
online.
Changes to procedure are usually announced on some part of the Court of King's Bench
website and counsel involved in applications should keep watching it.
Court of Appeal applications with one judge sitting are held in Edmonton and Calgary every
Wednesday and Thursday, usually at 9: 30 a. m. (In July and August there is one judge for all
Alberta, on a given week. ) Panels of three judges hear applications once every month except July
and August (gowning is required).
In King's Bench, the ﬁrst question is in which courtroom an application is returnable. In
Edmonton and Calgary, justices and applications judges sit daily to hear applications. Judges’
“chambers” there in normal non-epidemic times are split into four sections: family law matters,
commercial and other lists (which are not daily) ; (and a fourth “emergency” section). Now one
courtroom may be for applications without notice, and agreed late adjournments. It is customary
to limit applications in morning “chambers” to matters which can be argued and decided inside

Footnote
20 minutes.¹
In the "News & Announcements" part of the Court of King's Bench website is a link entitled
"Commercial". It leads to a list several pages long about commercial announcements; in date
order, from March 2020 to April 2022. In there is a long list of matters eligible for the commercial
list, but it says that is "subject to change".
At a time and place where there is only one courtroom for applications, then usually ﬁrst come
consent adjournments and applications without notice. Then the matters on the list, in the order
in which they appear there. Disputed adjournments will likely be heard as part of the cases on the
list, again as they appear there.²
Failure to appear may result in a default order or dismissal, the
remedy for which is R.9.15.³
Ordinarily an application must be heard in the city or town where the ﬁle is and the action is
being run.⁴
Where the court has directed that a special date be booked and used, and parties do book
it, that gives no license to expand or ignore the issues which the court directed be set down
for hearing then. Parties cannot prioritize their own litigation strategies, by raising additional
issues, nor try to avoid issues which the court had directed be heard. Parties should either ﬁle
the application which they were directed to ﬁle, or tell the court at once if they wish to abandon
those issues.⁵
If any kind of written argument is provided to the court, it needs careful checking and
proofreading. One must not recite or assume facts unless they are clearly admitted, or proven by
evidence on ﬁle (or already found by the court in this lawsuit). Having a computer program draft
a submission is very dangerous; the program will not include such restrictions, still less know
Alberta law or practice. Some computers’ drafts even include imaginary legal citations, which
could make the counsel submitting the material look very dishonest. You only have one reputation
to lose.⁶

6-9
PART 6: RESOLVING ISSUES AND PRESERVING RIGHTS R.6.2
4. Length and Type of Application
Morning family law chambers are crowded, busy, and short of time, so issues may not be fully
considered, and plain error can occur.¹
King's Bench Family Law Practice Note 2 is not jurisdictional, but it is valuable and reasonable.
It says that absent urgency, changes of custody or substantial charges to parenting, or over

Footnote
6 months retroactive support, should not be heard in regular family chambers.²
Issues from a
custodial parent’s taking the child to live in another province ordinarily need a more comprehensive
hearing than morning chambers offer.³
A complex matter should not be heard in morning chambers, especially as it often leads to
partial or tentative orders, whereas an afternoon special would decide it all.⁴
Longer matters should be set down as special applications in the afternoon, so the justice or
applications judge can read the required written material ahead of time.⁵
Briefs are optional before
Calgary applications judges motions court, but if ﬁled, the applicant’s brief must be ﬁled 14 days
before, and the respondent’s brief 7 days before, the scheduled special hearing date.⁶
There is a
discussion of the present backlog in the lists for such afternoon “specials”.⁷
The opportunity for lengthy argument in morning chambers is small, as someone bringing
an application there should realize.⁸
An oral surreply (in chambers) is unusual and the judge
has no duty to allow one.⁹
One chambers judge understood the applicant’s arguments, and on
appeal the Court of Appeal heard full argument.¹⁰
A long motion should not be brought in morning
chambers. If counsel knows the motion will take over 20 minutes, it should initially be returnable
as an afternoon “special” and not put into morning motions court. Breach of that procedure may

Oct.6, 2023, found on the Alberta Courts’ website. For practical advice on drafting written arguments, see
Côté, Systematic Advocacy, chaps.9 and 19 (Juriliber Ltd.2017).

Huitt v. Huitt, supra.

10 Ayoungman v. Ayoungman 2017 ABCA 333, Calg 1701 0109 AC (Oct 10).

6-10
R.6.2 PART 6: RESOLVING ISSUES AND PRESERVING RIGHTS
result in loss of costs.¹
If it is an emergency, the applicant must ask an Associate Chief Justice for
an accelerated special chambers hearing, which is usually possible.²
Where a matter came on
morning chambers but was too long, the chambers judge split it. Most was adjourned to a later
date for special chambers, but a request for an injunction was heard as though ex parte, and an
interim injunction given.³
Usually an appeal from an Applications Judge should not be heard in
justices’ morning chambers, but one was where it was urgent and giving a stay would effectively
end the appeal.⁴
There is no rule that a bulky lawsuit cannot be dismissed under R.4.33 in morning King’s
Bench chambers, or needs a special hearing, if the facts and issues for R.4.33 are not complex
or bulky. That is especially true if the judge reviews the afﬁdavits before the hearing. The decision
not to adjourn is discretionary.⁵
A chambers judge continued a restraining order, because he was pressed for time and the
parties had already talked for some time. But restraining the liberty of a party requires giving
enough time. There were strong conﬂicts in the evidence, calling for inquiry. One should not give a
restraining order as a matter of routine, nor out of an abundance of caution.⁶
Even if the order so
made is said to be "without prejudice", or without ﬁnding fault or facts, where it is shown that there
is no urgency nor threat of serious harm.⁷
Issues about how to word a formal order previously pronounced, or about amending the order,
should go back before the same justice. If he or she is hard to access, and the matter is truly
urgent, then the Chief Justice of the King's Bench or supervising judge should be contacted.⁸
Ordinarily an application to strike parts of an afﬁdavit should be heard as part of the substantive
motion, not as a preliminary motion. This is to have all the necessary information available, to avoid
possibly having two judges decide (which could lead to a conﬂict of decisions), and to follow the
analogy of trying a separate issue before trial, which the Court of Appeal says is usually harmful.⁹
If two parties advance inconsistent interpretations of the same Court of Appeal decision,
that is not improper conduct, and does not warrant enhanced costs. Parties can structure their
arguments any way that they decide. Often two parties give the court differing interpretations
of what are the truth. Outright lies are forbidden, but not other differences. The topics of the
impugned passages were what the law is.¹⁰
B. How to Argue Applications
A Court of King’s Bench notice on that Court’s website ended access and masking requirements
which had been in place during the Covid pandemic.¹¹

6-11
PART 6: RESOLVING ISSUES AND PRESERVING RIGHTS R.6.2
Judges in regular morning chambers have no chance to read any of the materials beforehand,
and receive no written argument which they can read later. There is no time for the judge to
take much in the way of notes. And though the judge is supposed to have access to the relevant
documents (once on the physical court ﬁle of papers, now electronically), there is many a slip
between cup and lip in either method.
At the outset of every application, counsel should tell the chambers judge the exact nature of
the application. And if it is one of the type usually heard as a special application on a reserved
date in an afternoon, tell why it cannot wait for an afternoon special chambers applications.¹
When your matter is called,
1. you should promptly rise, say that it is your application, come forward to the counsel
table, and name yourself and your client.
2. If any other counsel or parties are present, say who they are and who they appear for.
3. If any parties are not represented, say whether they were served, whether you anticipate
that anyone will appear for them, and whether they would likely be affected by the motion.
4. In one sentence, tell what kind of lawsuit it is, and what party your client is.
5. In another sentence, describe generally what your application is about.
6. If you know whether it will be consented to, opposed, or neither, say so.
7. Give those 6 introductory remarks slowly enough to let the justice or applications judge
become oriented.
8. Then brieﬂy make your arguments.
Tell the judge whether the facts involved are uncontradicted, or contradicted. The judge probably
will have had no chance to read anything.²
Stick strictly to facts in the afﬁdavits (or the history on
the court record).³
Keep your argument to 10 minutes or less. If that is not quite possible, ask the
judge at the outset whether he/she would prefer that your matter go to the end of the list, or be
adjourned to a special ﬁxed date some afternoon (“special chambers”) ; the latter choice would let
a judge read the application and afﬁdavits and written argument before oral argument.
An outline of counsel's upcoming oral argument can be useful for the judge about to hear an
application, but if it is detailed, and gives additional authorities, it should not be sprung on the
opponent at the opening of oral argument.⁴
A party has no right in morning chambers to read a
long statement, especially if the judge borrows the text and reads it (silently). Denying that creates
no bias.⁵

Footnote
1 M. R. S. v. K. J. S.2014 ABCA 95, Calg 1401 0003 AC (Mar 6).
2 If the motion is in the morning chambers in the Court of King's Bench. Even if the ﬁle is in the courtroom, it
may well be disorganized, incomplete, and not up to date (especially in the Judicial Centre of Edmonton).
So many applications do not go ahead on the return date, that there is little point to the judge reading them
beforehand. And which motion will be heard by which judge (if the motion does go ahead) is not ﬁrm until
the last moment. So electronic ﬁling may not ensure that the Justice has read ahead of time. In afternoon
King's Bench chambers (“special chambers”) and in the Court of Appeal, the judge probably will have read
the papers.

R.6.2 PART 6: RESOLVING ISSUES AND PRESERVING RIGHTS
Address a judge as “Justice ____”, the ﬁrst time. Few judges would be offended by being
addressed as “Justice Green” (or whatever their name is), or even as “Justice” with no name. After
that, an occasional “Sir” or “Ma’m” does no harm. An Applications Judge is to be addressed as
“Judge” or “Your Honor”. One is no longer to say “My Lord” or “My Lady”.
Refer to opposing counsel as “my learned friend”, not by name, unless it is necessary to
distinguish among several counsel. Avoid personal sniping at them,¹
and do not respond to
sniping. On civility in the courtroom, see the Fuerst book.²
Misstating facts to the justice or Applications Judge is serious misconduct. See R.6.3n.
There is a suggestion that in chambers the court cannot deny either party a chance to be
heard, even if no prejudice results.³
But in practice after the party who is about to lose has been
heard in full, often the opponent is not called on. If he or she insisted, doubtless the court would
hear him or her.

A. Introduction and References
On the mechanics of the application (motion) process, including booking and ﬁling, see R.
6.2n. On family law applications, see R.12.44. On costs, see R.10.29. For more general law, see
Halsbury’s.¹
On how R.6.3 reﬂects natural justice and so must be interpreted generously, see
Part E below.
On combining the Court of King's Bench Practice Notes about chambers, and their contents,
see R.6.2n. A.3.
This topic used to be called “motions”; the corresponding verb was “to move”. Now it is
“applications” and “to apply”.
Since March 2020, preparing and ﬁling an application has become more complex. If
counsel ﬁles, it (December 20, 2020 notice) must be done electronically, and those Covid-era
ﬁling requirements remain, as of May 24, 2022 (says a May 17, 2022 posting in the “News &
Announcements” part of the Court of King’s Bench website). But an April 20, 2022 notice there
about new email ﬁling procedures naming conventions is long and complex. There are July 8,

Footnote
2020 guidelines (notice to the profession) about email or digitally-ﬁled documents, found in the
“News & Announcements” part of the Court of King’s Bench website. Important changes are in a
May 7 2024 Announcement on the Court of King’s Bench website. They are described above in R.
6.2 n. A.2. A formal revised Practice Note is to follow. In the Court of King’s Bench, backlogs are
long and available return dates far away.
The Court of King’s Bench website has one location listing schedules and hence available
dates, but after ﬁling an application, getting a conﬁrmation takes some weeks, and the date
available when the application was drafted may turn out to have been ﬁlled and so the application
drafted will not work. And agreed adjournments may disappear though duly notiﬁed to the court.

PART 6: RESOLVING ISSUES AND PRESERVING RIGHTS R.6.3(3)
The new booking and ﬁling procedures for applications raise serious dangers, especially
when a time limit is close. See long notes about that, in R.6.2n. A.2. However, as of spring 2023,
scheduling ordinary morning chambers is prompt and workable. And “desk” applications can be
ﬁled using the Digital Filing Service.
There is no public notice about hard copy, but the Clerks have been instructed to tell counsel
or parties on each individual upcoming application, to ﬁle with the orderlies (or security desk in
smaller judicial centres) an extra hard copy of the papers relevant to the upcoming application, for
use of the judge. That is probably because of big backlogs in getting ﬁlings onto individual ﬁles.
This practice continues (as of April 2023). See further n. D.2.
It is reviewable error for a chambers judge not to decide an issue squarely before him or her.¹
B. Notice
See also R.6.2n. A.2(b) above, and Part D.4 below.
This Rule sets the advance notice period for all applications in King's Bench or the Court of
Appeal, except where otherwise stated. On the notice times required for applications, see R.6.2
n. B. A major exception is originating applications.²
Another is an application to vary a divorce
judgment.³
Because of R.13.3, to compute the notice time one omits the day of service, and only counts
days after the application is served.⁴
The remedy for notice which is too short is an adjournment;
the notice is not a nullity.⁵
In case of urgency, the time can be shortened under R.13.5, or
dispensed with under Rr.6.4 and 11.29.
The previous Rule, that an application could not be brought until the time to defend has
passed, has been repealed.
If legislation or a Rule gives a time limit for an application to be “made”, time continues to
run until the return date, but it stops then even if the court adjourns the application. Filing the
application does not stop the time,⁶
and ordinarily the court cannot extend it.⁷
On how to compute
time when there is a deadline for bringing a motion, such as seeking leave to appeal within 30
days, see the Civil Procedure Encyclopedia.⁸
The Rules require written Applications to give notice, even in family law matters, and no Clerk
or local Bar can dispense with that.⁹
One application can seek striking out and summary judgment, in the alternative, even if one
needs evidence and the other forbids evidence.¹⁰
At the end of a long motion, one party also asked

to have the other side’s statement of claim struck out for want of a reasonable cause of action.
That was mentioned in the written brief for chambers, but not in any written application. Though
the other side did not object, the judge declined to hear striking out.¹
Rule 6.3(2) requires that an
application state the grounds or irregularity relied on. One application for summary judgment did
not, though the afﬁdavit in support did state that. However the actual argument before the judge
was very different. Nor was the application served upon a company which one counsel argued
was the real owner of the lawsuit.²
So judgment given was vacated.
It is important to obey court orders for schedules or deadlines for applications and for afﬁdavits
for them. One can apply for more time; but ﬁling or serving late and then seeking a retroactive
blessing, without showing reason for the delay, is not proper or sufﬁcient.³
It is pointless to apply or appeal for an interlocutory order when it will last a very brief time, if
the deﬁnitive trial is close, or has even passed.⁴
There is a comment on booking problems for applications in Edmonton.⁵
If a petition for bankruptcy under the Bankruptcy Act has some errors or gaps in it or its
evidence, the fact that this is bankruptcy should not stymie a motion to amend the petition
proceedings to ﬁx these problems.⁶
If a party moves to strike portions of the opponent’s afﬁdavit, R.6.3 and Form FL-18 require
giving the grounds for the application or the irregularities complained of. Merely mentioning certain
“concerns” is not enough, especially if it does not cover all the objections. To prepare properly for
hearing the application, formal documents are needed. Failure to do this is frequent, as is a vague
reference to a large statute with no section number given.⁷
If only one party moves for summary judgment (or dismissal), and fails, the other party who
has not moved is not entitled to get a reciprocal judgment in its favor.⁸
It is arguable that a "comeback" application to conﬁrm or upset a previous ex parte order is
under R.6.3(1), not R.6.6.⁹

PART 6: RESOLVING ISSUES AND PRESERVING RIGHTS R.6.3(3)
2. Describing in Argument the Evidence
Chambers works on afﬁdavits, and so counsel must not state “facts” neither agreed to, nor in
an afﬁdavit.¹
Indeed that is expressly forbidden by the Law Society's Code of Conduct.²
But the
justice or applications judge may rely on an admission by counsel, especially on a procedural
topic.³
Misstatements to the court are always misconduct. If they are deliberate, then that is very
grave misconduct. That is even worse if coupled with failure to give opposing counsel notice,
and going to different justices or Applications Judges, still more if a false appearance of notice
is created.⁴
Even careless misrepresentation short of fraud can lead to setting aside an order?⁵
In a case where such misstatements happened a number of times, even though opposing
counsel was not very cooperative, some previous costs were rescinded, and solicitor-client costs
were imposed, payable jointly by the client and by counsel.⁶
For applications or hearings in Commercial Court Management, it is possible for counsel to
“associate” any materials in support of a hearing “which counsel can upload. . . directly to the
digital hearing card for the presiding justice. . . ”. Apparently counsel must do that for matters heard
March 6, 2023 or later. Such information no longer need be given to the court coordinator.⁷
3. When Evidence Adduced or Filed
The Court of King's Bench justice gets material for an application via the Court of King's Bench
"SharePoint" electronic platform, but counsel cannot see it, and do not know when important
records have been omitted or delayed. Counsel can email an address to send things to be
uploaded to Sharepoint, but counsel have no direct access. Many commercial matters use private
software called CaseLines which allows direct uploading, with reported positive results from the
Court.
Rules 6.3 and 6.6 are about orderly exchange of information, not primarily about "case
splitting", and though the court can stop a long sequence of rebuttal afﬁdavits back and forth,
each side is to get a fair chance to present its case and any abuse is usually to be dealt with by
an adjournment, not costs. Case splitting is usually a topic for trial, not interlocutory applications.⁸
In support of an application for interlocutory relief, there is no reason for the chambers judge
to conﬁne himself or herself to evidence ﬁled or adduced after the date on which the statement of
claim was issued, so long as it is relevant.⁹

R.6.3(3) PART 6: RESOLVING ISSUES AND PRESERVING RIGHTS
It is proper to appear in chambers relying on an unﬁled afﬁdavit, then ﬁle it a few days later.¹
But that only addresses filing; this may be unfair and improper if the opponent has no advance
notice.²
Where a statute requires an afﬁdavit verifying an application (motion), the afﬁdavit is essential,
but there is no objection to swearing that afﬁdavit some months before the application is ﬁled
or deemed to be ﬁled. If the facts were known for a long time, swearing earlier is natural. And
technical formalities are to be avoided.³
On tight times and leave to adduce late cross-examination evidence already before the court
or closely related to such evidence, see the Vaillancourt decision.⁴
4. Conﬂicting Evidence
Disputed facts and evidence on a material point almost always used to require live evidence,
not a paper contest.⁵
Conﬂicting afﬁdavits on material points do not allow the court to make a ﬁnal
decision, and some sort of a hearing with live evidence should be given.⁶
If credibility is in issue,
some kind of trial is necessary.⁷
So the general rule is that serious conﬂicts of evidence between
competing afﬁdavits cannot be resolved in chambers without live evidence.⁸
But now some exceptions are developing where:
a. the conﬂict of evidence is not on an important point, nor essential facts. A clash of
afﬁdavits does not bar deciding in chambers, if the conﬂicts are on topics only marginally relevant
to the pending application,⁹
or are academic. See also Rr.6.2n. and R.6.9 n. C.
b. the conﬂict does not involve credibility,¹⁰
c. the chambers judge can reliably ﬁnd the facts, e. g. by corroboration from undoubted
documents,¹¹
d. the conﬂict is only on opinions, not actual facts,
e. one party has made admissions against interest, e. g. by agreeing with part of the
opposing evidence,
f. the issues before the judge can be resolved on the basis of the parts of the afﬁdavits
which do not clash,

Footnote
1 Thompson v. Procrane 2016 ABCA 71, [2016] AJ #237 (one JA Mar 14). On problems getting a transcript

6-19
PART 6: RESOLVING ISSUES AND PRESERVING RIGHTS R.6.3(3)
g. who has the onus of proof will resolve the question,
h. a mere self-serving afﬁdavit will not sufﬁce, absent detailed facts and supporting
evidence, or
i. argumentative or merely conclusory portions of afﬁdavits may not sufﬁce.¹
In chambers a judge is not barred from deciding a factual question just because some
conﬂicting evidence existed. Not all evidence has to get the same weight.
Disputed facts do not necessarily bar summary judgment. Inferences from undisputed evidence
and the parties’ conduct and corroborating evidence, are allowed for summary judgment. Some
conﬂict is not always a bar, especially a bare denial or self-serving afﬁdavit supported by nothing
else, nor a piece of evidence ﬂying in the face of the balance of the evidence. What is a genuine
issue requiring trial must balance weight and reliability of evidence, importance of the issue, and
likelihood that a trial would offer better evidence (among other things).²
Indeed the “presumption”
against using conﬂicting afﬁdavits to give summary judgment “is primarily concerned with” issues
of credibility, especially where the afﬁdavits are relevant and admissible.³
The applicant sought imputation of income to the respondent. The chambers justice found the
evidence insufﬁcient to impute income and dismissed the application entirely. There had been
conﬂicting evidence on material points, and no cross-examination. Some of the evidence left
unanswered, obvious, relevant questions. Dismissal of the application was an error. The judge ﬁrst
should have decided whether she could make the necessary fact ﬁndings there in chambers. If
she could, then she should have found them. If she could not. then she should have remitted the
application to a suitable procedure in the Court of King’s Bench to determine the facts. Dismissal
was improper.⁴
And in one case neither party objected to an earlier order that the matter be heard in special
chambers, on afﬁdavit evidence alone nor otherwise objected. Any such objection should have
been prompt. The evidence was good enough, and some key facts were not disputed. The
chambers judge made use of that and of other factors.⁵
Where an application was complex and multi-faceted and driven by credibility issues, a
judge was clearly right to decline to hear it in ordinary chambers and to direct a hearing with live
evidence, and an appeal from that was hopeless.⁶
5. Notice of Evidence
The written application (notice of motion) must recite any afﬁdavits or other evidence which will
be relied on.⁷
On failure to give timely notice, see Part B above.
Where the parties did not argue (on a summary judgment application) an important issue,
the Justice adjourned the application) to allow more discovery, evidence, and written argument.⁸

R.6.3(3) PART 6: RESOLVING ISSUES AND PRESERVING RIGHTS
6. Allowing Further Evidence
If evidence of an important point is not clear enough, the justice or applications judge may
adjourn to get better evidence.¹
Afﬁdavits were ﬁled after the deadline previously set, but the court received them because they
had a narrow subject, probably created no surprise, and there was enough time to cross-examine.
There would be some disruption and disquiet to the opponent, so the opponent got $1, 000 costs
though its opposition to the late material failed here. Court orders should be respected.²
7. Type and Source of Evidence
On afﬁdavits, see R.13.18n. On evidence or documents from another lawsuit, see R.6.11 and
the seriously limited effects of such evidence.³
On an interim application, the rules of evidence apply, unless some legislation or express
Rule contradicts them.⁴
Whether or not the party has counsel.⁵
All evidence has to be useful and
reliable (two overlapping qualities), and prejudicial effect should not outweigh probative value.
Such prejudice can be harm to a party’s interest, undermining an accurate result, or complicating
frustrating or degrading the process.⁶
So undue consumption of time, unfair surprise, creating side
issues, or confusing the trier of fact, can be prejudicial effect helping to exclude evidence.⁷
A judge
faced with an inadmissible afﬁdavit it need not strike it; he or she can just ignore it.⁸
Merely appending papers to one's written argument does not make them admissible evidence.
At a minimum they would have to be sworn to, their authors available for cross-examination and
credentials (expertise) proven if any opinions were given. Otherwise this is all pure hearsay
(maybe multiple hearsay).⁹
Even if R.13.18 subrules (1) (b) and (2) are met, a court is not bound to accept information in
an afﬁdavit on information and belief.¹⁰
But it is unreasonable for a judge hearing an interlocutory
application to say that hearsay will get no weight, especially if it has corroboration from another
source and is uncontradicted.¹¹
Opinion evidence cannot be entered outside of the formal regime for the admission of expert
opinion evidence under the Rules of Court.¹²

PART 6: RESOLVING ISSUES AND PRESERVING RIGHTS R.6.3(3)
A party need not provide any afﬁdavit in support of his application if the other side has given
an afﬁdavit and been cross-examined on it, and the evidence there would be relevant to support
the motion.¹
All answers on cross-examination on afﬁdavit are before the court, and any party can
rely on them; no reading in is necessary.²
Or counsel can rely upon an afﬁdavit which he or she
previously ﬁled in that lawsuit, so long as he or she gives written notice that that afﬁdavit will be
relied upon for this application.³
The court allowed use of some school records. All concerned except the mother of the child
were happy with that use. The counsellor formerly hired by the mother wished to use them to
defend himself against attacks by the mother in defamation litigation and in complaints to the
counsellor's professional association. The Master looked at the documents before making this
ruling.⁴
If a party is cross-examined on an afﬁdavit ﬁled for an application, the counsel for the
questioning party must ﬁle the transcript with the court; ﬁling some excerpts from it is not enough,
as all parties can rely upon the transcript. The Clerk's ofﬁce records ﬁlings long after the actual
ﬁling date!⁵
8. Judicial Notice
Judicial notice is conﬁned to facts clearly non-controversial or beyond reasonable dispute,
either by the judge’s knowledge, or from readily accessible indisputable sources. The rule must
be strict, as no testing by cross-examination is possible.⁶
The court will not take judicial notice of
government intent to amend legislation.⁷
For an advance-funding application, a judge took judicial
notice that Legal Aid Alberta would not fund, that a fundraising campaign or cloud funding would
likely be uneconomical, that the public is interested in harm to children in government care, and
that aboriginal children are disproportionately taken into care.⁸
The judge can take notice of misconduct which is itself part of the court record, or which the
judge personally saw occur in open court.⁹
On whether counsel can state facts in argument with no sworn evidence, see R.6.11n. A.

R.6.3(3) PART 6: RESOLVING ISSUES AND PRESERVING RIGHTS
9. Miscellaneous
Unless legislation says otherwise, a party bringing an application bears the onus of proving
that application.¹
So a motion under R.4.33 requires proof beyond a reasonable doubt of no
signiﬁcant advance of the action in the period before the applicant moved.
Must a party applying on notice disclose all facts against him or her?²
There is a review of the case law on whether illegally-obtained evidence is admissible in
chambers.³
That evidence was obtained illegally or surreptitiously is not usually a ground to
exclude it in a civil lawsuit.⁴
There is an extended discussion of the “documents in possession” rule as a ground for making
records admissible in evidence.⁵
D. Pleadings and Details
1. Pleadings
Usually one cannot make an application (motion) unless an action has been commenced, by
statement of claim, or originating application.⁶
And the topic of the application must be pleaded
in the lawsuit so commenced.⁷
Rule 3.2(3) governs special statutory applications.⁸
Courts are
usually passive, not investigative, and wait for parties to bring evidence and arguments to the
court.⁹
For many reasons, courts should not act on their own motion.¹⁰
2. Written Application
The current process for ﬁling and scheduling Special Chambers matters where counsel wish
to make oral argument is causing issues because of the triggering deadlines that apply. The
Special Chambers Booking Request form indicates that the Court expects that parties will not
book a Special Application until afﬁdavit exchange and question is completed. The booking form
also requires that other methods of dispute resolution have been considered and the parties are
prepared to ﬁle a brief and proceed to the hearing. The website sets out the booking procedure
and the “triggering” deadlines for ﬁling materials. These deadlines can be varied by court order,
but there are varying approaches among the Justices and Applications Judges to doing so.
The "News & Announcements" part of the Court of King's Bench website contains several
notices to the profession bearing on ﬁling documents in that court. One is on “mandatory email
ﬁling” (December 17, 2020), and another is “guidelines for email or digitally-ﬁled documents” (July
8, 2020).

PART 6: RESOLVING ISSUES AND PRESERVING RIGHTS R.6.3(3)
Getting the relevant papers to a King's Bench justice in time for hearing the motion has been
a problem in some judicial districts for well over 30 years. Why that should be is not entirely clear,
and interim measures for electronic ﬁling have not seen any signiﬁcant reduction of the problem.
Closing Clerk’s ofﬁce counters to the public have added another element.
What may be an interim solution to the problem has come to light in May 2022. There does
not seem to be anything about this in the King's Bench website, but Clerks have been writing to
counsel who have scheduled special chambers applications coming up. The letter looks like a
standard form. It sets out (without quotation marks) some new requirements. These are said to
be mandatory; the possible penalty for breach is having the application struck. Given long waiting
lists for such applications, that is a serious penalty. This applies to “any Justice Special Chambers
hearing, including Family Law Special Chambers. ”
As revealed by the form letter, the requirements are
(a) organize and tab “accordingly”, and label with the action number, the style of cause, and
the date and time of hearing “all materials that are to be relied upon” for the particular special
chambers hearing.
(b) put with this a completed copy of a checklist (which is not reproduced in the form letter, but
is said to be “attached” to the Clerk’s letter)
(c) deliver this “courtesy hard copy” and the checklist to the appropriate desk in the relevant
court house. In Calgary, that is the Court Orderlies on 24th ﬂoor north, and in Edmonton on the
6th ﬂoor of the Law Courts. In other judicial centres, that is the security desk.
(d) do this by the earlier of any existing deadlines from the Court or the Rules of Court, or by
the “end of day Thursday of the week prior to the hearing”.
As of April 27, 2023 there is a pilot project in Calgary which will allow for digital document
delivery to the judiciary for previously booked half day civil specials. Lawyers will be able to upload
electronic materials directly to the Justice assigned to the matter.
Counsel often sign an application, but the Rules and Form 27 do not require that; it was not
usual over 45 years ago. Of course the application must name who issued it.¹
Written Applications are needed before justice hears a matter, and neither the Clerk nor the
local Bar can dispense with that.²
See also R.6.2 n. A.2(b).
Some years ago, a written application (notice of motion) needed to contain little explanation,
but now “brief grounds” are required by R.6.3(2).³
Despite many lawyers' sloppy practice, R.6.3(2)
(d) requires more than a vague reference to some lengthy statute; one must state the precise
sections relied on (unless maybe the applicant explains that in a written brief).⁴
A reference to
one section in an Act implies the following ancillary sections, and may imply the applicable or
analogous Rules of Court.⁵
One need not cite cases or common-law rules in the application. Some

(notice of motion), especially one for contempt, needs more detail than a mere reference to (say)

3. Curing Flaws
A judge criticized ignoring Family Law Practice Note 2, relying on assertion of fact without
evidence, illegible or tiny print, bulky repetitive and unhighlighted materials, and merely citing
previously-ﬁled materials without giving a copy.⁴
The fact that a party has no lawyer is no excuse for ignorance of important Rules and
procedures and time limits.⁵
Disobeying R.6.3(2) is curable, and the remedy may well be an adjournment with costs
of the adjournment in any event.⁶
On curing errors in an application, see the Civil Procedure
Encyclopedia,⁷
and also R.1.5.
If an application lacks grounds, the respondent should move for particulars; but if an application
refers to afﬁdavits served with it which give full particulars, that sufﬁces.⁸
A separate preliminary cross-application to strike out parts of one’s opponent’s application is
usually inefﬁcient and inappropriate. One should instead make one’s objections on the return of
his or her (substantive) application.⁹
When there has been a formal order that the court will consider issues in a lawsuit in certain
steps, a judge can strike out part of pleadings that are not relevant to that particular step.¹⁰
4. Surprise
See also R.6.2 n. A.2(b) above. On who should get notice, see Part E below.
A judge should not give an order sought by neither party in the written applications.¹¹
Basing
an important judgment on an issue not argued by either side (though pleaded) would be unfair,
so it may be necessary to adjourn the application for more argument and even more discovery

PART 6: RESOLVING ISSUES AND PRESERVING RIGHTS R.6.3(3)
and evidence.¹
A party applying does not get more than what is in his or her written application
(notice of motion), and cannot get orders against parties against whom the application seeks no
relief, even if those parties turn up on the hearing of the application.²
It is error to award more
maintenance than the application calls for, without amendment or consent.³
It is unfair to give an
interlocutory injunction wider than the application seeks.⁴
An implied term was found in a contract,
though neither party had argued that (in writing or orally), so a new hearing was ordered.⁵
But
sometimes arguing the merits and not objecting waives that ﬂaw.⁶
It is also error for a chambers judge of his or her own motion to exclude afﬁdavit evidence for
an interlocutory motion without any objection by a party.⁷
In a special chambers application the judge should not give a ﬁnal order (on support and
matrimonial property), without clear warning to the parties that that would be done. Especially if
one of the parties is self-represented, and may not realize that would eliminate a trial or a chance
to lead live evidence.⁸
The key principle is a party's right to know the case he or she has to meet and have a chance
to combat harmful evidence and get and adduce favorable evidence.⁹
A boilerplate reference in a
written application to “such further, and other relief…” is not enough.¹⁰
For the same reason, each
party affected by an application should be served with all the afﬁdavits relied on by the applicant to
support the application.¹¹
It is no excuse that the respondent would have lost anyway, as the issue
is natural justice and fairness.¹²
An application for further particulars or to strike out a pleading for failure to provide them, is
not the same as striking out a pleading as being frivolous and vexatious, nor an application for
summary judgment; and so an application for one should not be turned into the other without
notice.¹³

A “desk” application made without notice, or without proof of notice, or before time to defend
can have expired, is improper, unless it is shown to be the type of application which may properly
be made ex parte (without notice).¹
And if it is rejected on that ground, it is improper just to make
another desk application, with only trivial changes, without warning the court that the previous
similar application was rejected.²
See further R.6.4 and notes.
If one side to a suit wants to make an application to, or get advice and directions from, the
judge, it is proper to write to the judge’s assistant (copying to the opposing lawyer) to seek an
appointment. No more formal notice is necessary.³
If the judge tells each party to send him a letter
giving the history of the case, and that is done with a cross-copy to the opponent, that is not an
improper ex parte communication.⁴
A topic underlying an order was res judicata because of an earlier decision about the topic,
preceded then by an appropriate application, so that topic did not have to be listed as a topic in
a later application.⁵
Some conditions on adjourning an application are foreseeable, but not important strong
injunctions such as a bar on communication. The latter should not be imposed without express
notice.⁶
The court should not hear a constitutional law question, such as whether provincial or federal
law applies, unless both Attorneys-General have received notice of the issues so the court can get
a full record, and the governments have a chance to rebut a case.⁷
5. Miscellaneous
Altering an application after ﬁling with the Clerk, but before service, was criticized.⁸
A notice of constitutional question under s.24 of the Judicature Act must give some particulars
and identify the legislation being attacked. Semble if the notice does not give enough particulars,
the Attorney General may demand more particulars.⁹
“Fair practice” Regulations made under the Insurance Act allow for advance payment orders
against insurance companies. Such an application should be made by Originating Application and
not in any existing torts action, to which presumably the insurer would not be a party. That means
that information from one of these court ﬁles will not be on or inadvertently disclosed in, the other
ﬁle. The court is not to take judicial notice of information on one ﬁle when hearing an application
under the other ﬁle.¹⁰
A proposed agenda of a case management meeting is enough notice of intent to make an
application on a topic there listed, and King's Bench Practice Direction #1 allowed the court to act
of its own motion.¹¹

PART 6: RESOLVING ISSUES AND PRESERVING RIGHTS R.6.3(3)
E. Service and Parties
See also R.6.4 n. B.2, and n. B on notice to only some (not all) of those affected. On notice to
Attorneys-General, see subpart D.4 above.
Rule 6.3 embodies natural justice and so should be interpreted purposively and generously,
not technically. It gives protection both to parties and to non-parties who are affected and so are
entitled to be heard.¹
In an action, the court cannot give orders against persons who have never been parties to
the lawsuit, or who are no longer parties, compelling them to do things.²
But someone who may
be affected by an application may become a party to that application, even if he or she is not the
party to the suit.³
For service, one must distinguish between giving an address for service (and the Rules about
that), and limited retainer of a solicitor for one step only; the two topics are different, and one does
not govern the other.⁴
Rule 6.3(3) calls for service of the written application (notice of motion) not only on all parties,
but also on every other person “affected”. All parties to the lawsuit are entitled to notice of each
application, because an order (e. g. removing a party) may have many indirect or unforeseen
effects on all parties, including delay of the suit.⁵
If one defendant applies for summary dismissal
of the suit, all co-defendants have standing to argue on that application, as all could be affected,
e. g. as to contribution among co-defendants.⁶
Any party to a suit can be heard on the question
of whether his opponent is using an improper representative such as an unadmitted (uncalled)
barrister.⁷
An application for contempt needs personal notice to the respondent, and personal service.⁸
If constitutional questions are raised, any order may be refused or voidable unless prior notice
was given to both Attorneys General.⁹
British Columbia Rules do not deﬁne “party” as including

an intervener, and so a motion for leave to appeal, or notice of appeal, in the Supreme Court of
Canada, need not be served on someone who intervened in the Court of Appeal. The Supreme
Court of Canada refused to rehear an appeal because of that “omission”.¹
Would the reference in
Alberta’s R.6.3 to “other affected parties” change that?²
If an appeal from a chambers order alleges non-service of the application, evidence of non-

parties. And if the appellant appeared in the ﬁrst court and argued the merits, the ground is moot.³

ready, or wants a lawyer.⁵
And if the party is not comfortable speaking English, he or she should
be given a chance to get an interpreter.⁶
Must the application document or the afﬁdavits be ﬁled before service?⁷
F. Varying Provisional Orders
An interlocutory order can sometimes be reheard. But not where doing so would be an abuse
of process, as where waiver of privilege had already been decided, and there was no good reason
to bring it up and try to reargue it again.⁸
There is no room for self-help in family law, and counsel must not tell their clients to disobey
court orders. Applications to vary can be made every business day.⁹
A chambers judge was wrong to dismiss an entire cross-application about support without
addressing the appellant’s allegation that the circumstances had changed after a previous order.¹⁰
There is no general requirement for permission before bringing an application, even an
application to set aside an attachment order, and a provision in an order permitting a further
application on something else, has no particular effect.¹¹

6-29
PART 6: RESOLVING ISSUES AND PRESERVING RIGHTS R.6.3(3)
G. Adjournment
1. Stalling
Stalling is a big problem, especially on applications. Most of this Part G is about the law. For
fuller practical advice about how to prevent stalling and keep things moving, see Côté, Systematic
Advocacy, Chap.9, Part I (Juriliber 2017).
On new dangers of stalling, see also R.6.6n. C.
Some counsel and self-represented parties stall an opposing application with repeated
adjournments, which is a curse of our system.¹
But it is a dangerous game to play, because it is
not hard to expose, can lead to costs,²
even personal costs, and can lead to other remedies, such
as case management.³
Besides, it takes away the staller’s credibility, suggesting that he or she
knows that his or her legal position is hopeless. There is no right to an adjournment, and one may
be refused if delay could cause harm, and the party seeking the adjournment merely wishes to
raise irrelevant points, and does not suggest any doubt or conﬂicting evidence on the only relevant
point.⁴
If after many hours of oral argument on an application a new event occurs, that may not be
a proper ground for adjourning the application to a later date for more evidence, especially if
the new event was known some days before the request to adjourn and before the end of the
argument of the party now seeking the adjournment. Still more so if the new event has very limited
relevance or weight. The Palmer tests (for new evidence on appeal) apply too.⁵

Footnote

Dogan etc. v. AMEC Americas, supra (¶’s 31-40).
2 One side unexpectedly asked for (and got) an adjournment of a contempt hearing. $500.00 costs were
awarded the other party who had travelled from British Columbia to watch. Though not strictly necessary to
be present, it was reasonable, so travel expenses were thrown-away costs: Turkawski v.738675 Alta. (#2)
2001 ABQB 488, 290 AR 334.
3 Striking out an afﬁdavit ﬁled for an opposed motion is an extreme measure and should not be resorted to
as a penalty for delay and obstruction without a considerable pattern of such delay: Murphy v. Cahill 2012
ABQB 793, [2012] AR Uned 843 (¶’s 15-23). Especially where some of the delay involved complaints by
other parties which the court did not give effect to: ibid.

to deny an adjournment and hear a motion with only one clear day’s notice of some parts. The actual notice
was two to four days, and one afﬁdavit used had been known for a year. The appellant could not point toany

absence of counsel for appellants who declined to attend, though told of the application. Temporary stay
of execution of costs was granted until return date of scheduled motion for stay pending appeal: Popke v.
Bolt (Re Serdahely Est. ) 2006 ABCA 68 (one JA Feb 23). The Court of Appeal accords a fair degree of
deference to judges deciding whether to give such adjournments: Young W. Oil & Gas v. Erewhon 2001
ABCA 115, 281 AR 152; Conway v. Zinkhofer 2006 ABCA 74, [2006] AJ #209; Black v. Pendelton 2007
ABCA 191; especially case management judges: Tarapaski v. Tarapaski, supra. If the client sends a totally
unprepared just-retained lawyer (from a new law ﬁrm) to a contempt penalty hearing, and does not come
himself, and the new lawyer says he knows nothing and wants an adjournment to look at the ﬁle, and does

166 (¶’s 32-44, 49).

Larocque v. Yahoo! 2023 SKCA 62 (May 25) (¶’s 39 ff. ).

Being out of town may not be a sufﬁcient excuse, and in any event unavailability must be
communicated to the court and opposite party before the hearing, not after it is over and decided.¹
2. References
On notice of proposed conditions of an adjournment, see Part D, supra. On adjourning
because the matter is too long for morning chambers, see end of Part B, supra.
On interlocutory applications brought too late to have practical effect, see Part B, supra.
3. Tests for Adjournments
Much law is cited about contested adjournments of motions.²
A judge has a “broad discretion”
whether to grant one, though it must not be based on reasons not founded in the law.³
One
can complain on appeal about refusal of an adjournment only if one shows that withholding the
adjournment could reasonably be expected to have affected the result?⁴
The tests for granting or refusing requests to adjourn an application in chambers are reviewed.
Eleven countervailing considerations are given, some neutral, but others usually tending in one
direction or the other.⁵
It is very common for applications to be adjourned, sometimes repeatedly. The party applying
for relief from the court must be prepared to proceed,⁶
but ordinarily the ﬁrst adjournment is not
hard for the respondent to get. Indeed, reasonable accommodation should be given to opposing
counsel and times should not be shortened, nor a brief adjournment to get instructions from a
temporarily-absent client, be refused.⁷
A party who books a special application and wants to
adjourn it (e. g. to cross-examine) cannot simply not ﬁle its brief, to achieve that. If the party does,
he or she needs consent or leave to return the case to the list?⁸

6.8n.

6-31
PART 6: RESOLVING ISSUES AND PRESERVING RIGHTS R.6.3(3)
Requests for an adjournment must be frank. To ask for an adjournment to a speciﬁc day
impliedly represents that one will be prepared to proceed on that date.¹
It is improper to withhold
until just before the long-set date of a hearing, a contentious adjournment request whose alleged
need has been known for some time.²
The practice of not attending court, but faxing a request for adjournment the day before, was
criticized and did not work here.³
A last-minute request for an adjournment to cross-examine will be denied if no good purpose
for it is shown, and there was ample time beforehand to cross-examine.⁴
The request to adjourn should be notiﬁed beforehand but a formal written cross-application to
adjourn is not needed.⁵
There is no absolute right to counsel of one’s choice, especially when it is used to support a
sudden last-minute demand to adjourn a pending hearing just before court opens, still more with
no real explanation.⁶
Four months' continuing failure to get new counsel is too long and not ground
(on the eve of the return date) to postpone an opponent's motion.⁷
An adjournment may be a condition imposed by the chambers justice who ﬁnds that an
important pleaded issue is not dealt with by either counsel’s argument, so more argument and
maybe more evidence and discovery are needed.⁸
4. Non-Prosecution of Application
See Practice Note 1 (v.3, p.1-3) (consolidation of Practice Note No.1 and 2).
The tests for extending time to appeal are not a good analogy to restoring an application
struck out for nonprosecution, and maybe Rr.4.31 and 4.33 would be a better analogy. Practice
Note 2 shows that striking out is not a ﬁnal order. The train is put onto a sidetrack, but not derailed.
Suggested relevant factors are why the delay occurred, for how long, and any prejudice to the
respondent. But whether the application has a reasonable prospect of success is not a suitable
criterion.⁹
5. Running Out of Time
A chambers judge continued a restraining order, because he was pressed for time and the
parties had already talked for some time. But restraining the liberty of a party requires giving
enough time. There were strong conﬂicts in the evidence, calling for inquiry. One should not give
a restraining order as a matter of routine, nor out of an abundance of caution. The order did

6. Consent Adjournments
How to notify the Court of King’s Bench of these has changed from time to time over the years,
and many such agreements do not reach the judges in time. See R.6.2n. A.3 for a description
of the late 2022 return to roughly the pre-Covid ways of notifying of adjournments. As of spring
2023, the Court of King’s Bench can be notiﬁed of consent adjournments electronically, but only
if done more than 5 days in advance. Any consent requests for regular Chambers adjournments
in Edmonton or Calgary 5 days or less before the scheduled date must be spoken to on the Court
record in regular Chambers, in person.
H. Costs
Where a party has to apply, but then the other side consents or does what has been asked for,
and so it is not necessary to pursue the application which was ﬁled, the party who applied should
get some costs. In one case they were 50% of the amount in Schedule C.²
The chambers judge gave each party what he or she asked for. However, the original proposal
of the husband would have caused prejudice to the wife, and he could have avoided the costs of
the application if he had agreed to what the wife proposed before the hearing. Therefore he got no
costs of the application and had to pay costs of the application.³
There is an analogy here to the
Rules on formal offers to settle.
On cross-applications, the appellant gave an undertaking that the costs award against her
could be set off against a matrimonial property equalization payment. That undertaking should
have been given earlier, as the applications could then have been avoided. So the respondent got
costs (about the set-off) of a single application.⁴
On costs for misconduct, see also Part C.
I. Miscellaneous
Legal authorities cited to the Court of King’s Bench must give a citation following the latest
edition of the McGill Style Guide.⁵
That does not apply to the Court of Appeal.
A lay litigant engaged in an application before the court should not walk out partway through,
even if he does not like what he sees or hears, because there may be later stages, such as
arguments over costs, where it would be to his or her interest to remain.⁶
The Court of Appeal criticized a judge other than the existing case management judge for
hearing an important contested motion where there was no urgency, and there were many
indications that the court had to be cautious with the parties and their procedure.⁷
If the judge

PART 6: RESOLVING ISSUES AND PRESERVING RIGHTS R.6.4
who normally should hear an application has a conﬂict of interest, he or she may still order that a
different judge hear the application.¹
The judge’s reading all the papers and written argument ﬁled or notiﬁed for the application,
and coming to oral argument with a tentative view, is neither bias, nor improper.²
If a party to an application sends his or her lawyer to argue, the party need not attend personally,
and the judge should not draw an adverse inference from the party’s absence.³
Rule 6.3 expressly allows amendment of parties’ names after entry of judgment (and so does
R.3.74(2) (b), (3) ).⁴
An application for an order to enforce a settlement after breaches of it found by an arbitrator,
does not open up validity of the settlement, and a separate application to do that would be
necessary.⁵
Prejudice to the respondent is a relevant factor in any application (if it is alleged), and must be
balanced with any other relevant factors.⁶
Applications Without Notice
6.4 Despite any other rule to the contrary, notice of an application is not required
to be served on a party if an enactment so provides or permits or the Court is
satisﬁed that
(a) no notice is necessary, or
(b) serving notice of the application might cause undue prejudice to the
applicant.⁷

A. Introduction
The traditional term for an application (motion) made without notice is “ex parte”. Bench and
Bar often still use that term.⁸
On setting aside ex parte orders, see R.9.15n. On the duty then to reveal all signiﬁcant facts,
see R.9.15 n. B.4.

Footnote

R.6.4 PART 6: RESOLVING ISSUES AND PRESERVING RIGHTS
Applying ex parte, claiming to be a (British Columbia) lawyer and not disclosing opposing
counsel on the record, is serious misconduct and contempt.¹
A judge told a litigant that to get a
stay pending appeal, he should apply on notice. He applied ex parte and got a stay. That was
improper conduct, even though the party appealing was self-represented.²
This annotation deals only with the laws of civil procedure. A separate topic is the ethical
obligation imposed on a lawyer by the Law Society’s Code of Professional Conduct, to give notice
where there is counsel on the other side.
B. When is Notice Needed?
1. Cross-References
See also R.6.3 and all its notes, especially nn. D.4 and E. Exacerbation of misrepresentations
to the justice or Applications Judge by not giving necessary notice: see R.6.3n. C. On the inability
of a chambers judge to go outside the notice given, see R.6.3n. D.4.
2. Need for Notice
Failure to give notice is a breach of natural justice and so is very serious. See R.6.3 n. ,
especially n. E.
The other party should ordinarily get proper detailed notice of any application,³
even if the
order proposed is a change in details of an earlier order, and the judge applied to is a case
management judge.⁴
A party must give all existing parties notice of an application to add more
people as parties, and cannot apply ex parte.⁵
The same is true of settling the contents of an
order reﬂecting a settlement agreement whose contents may be disputed.⁶
Applications without
notice are extraordinary, because notice is a fundamental principle when affecting rights.⁷
A clause in a receivership order that any employees rehired will not be successor employees,
will not bind them because they got no notice; it is a poor practice to put in orders clauses
purporting to bind absent persons.⁸
And it is a false economy, as the order produces no certainty
and is liable to be set aside at any time on the request of one of those not served.⁹
Getting an ex
parte order on insufﬁcient evidence is probably pointless, because the opponent will simply move
to have it set aside.¹⁰
See Rr.6.12 and 9.15.

6-35
PART 6: RESOLVING ISSUES AND PRESERVING RIGHTS R.6.4
3. Impact of Lack of Notice
Lack of notice is a breach of natural justice: see R.6.3 n. E.
Where there is no basis for moving ex parte, and the respondent attends and objects to the
lack of notice and seeks an adjournment, he or she is entitled to the adjournment, and should
not be subject to conditions such as interim relief, especially absent any evidence of improper
conduct by the respondent or danger that assets would disappear.¹
Succeeding on an ex parte application for extension is not an assurance or representation
or advice by the court to the plaintiff, and ex parte orders are always subject to recall on hearing
more evidence or argument. The plaintiff is not obliged to apply ex parte.²
An order about answering undertakings can be reopened despite the functus officio
doctrine, if it was given ex parte. In one case, the application was not served on the respondent,
and sending 7 months before, a draft unﬁled application (never ﬁled in court) was not enough,
especially as it came by email at 4: 30 p. m. the day before the proposed application. The Master
did not know it was ex parte, and counsel told him it was not ex parte, and that the other side
knew the applicant’s counsel was in court that day. Ex parte applications can be heard only in
limited circumstances, and with full disclosure. This was not an urgent matter.³
4. What is Lack of Notice
On incomplete or inaccurate notice, see R.6.3 and notes.
A motion is ex parte if there was no advance notice, even if the opposing lawyer merely happens
to be present in court and makes such impromptu remarks as he can without instructions.⁴
Or if
the motion was supposed to be to set procedure only, but substance is decided without warning.⁵
Or if the party does not attend because her lawyer had not told her that he was ceasing to act,
and he did not attend either?⁶
But if due notice is given by service of a written application, and the
respondent’s counsel attends, the motion is not ex parte, even if that counsel lacks instructions
and offers no argument.⁷
Nor is it ex parte if one party does not show up after proper service.⁸
Service need not be personal; it can be upon an address for service, or a solicitor of record.⁹
One must not get a “consent” order without consent from, or notice to, all the parties who will
be affected.¹⁰

R.6.4 PART 6: RESOLVING ISSUES AND PRESERVING RIGHTS
“Approved as to the order granted”, on a formal order, is ambiguous: is it consent, or approval
of the form of what the judge has earlier entered?¹
A letter containing argument (not facts) sent to a judge by one side, while costs were being
considered, was improper but did no harm.²
5. Grounds Not to Give Notice
(a) General
Applications ex parte are allowed only if delay or a chance to thwart the motion, resulting
from notice, would be too damaging.³
The Court of Appeal criticized applying ex parte on slight
grounds.⁴
A court should not dispense with notice just for the asking. There should be clear
evidence of what harm would be done either by giving notice (such as grounds to believe the
other side would act improperly and irrevocably, between notice received and motion heard), or
by waiting until notice could be given (where irrevocable harm would be done within a couple of
hours).⁵
A very good reason to apply ex parte is needed, if the other side is represented.⁶
(b) Duration of Ex Parte Order
Worse still, lawyers often get ex parte orders which last long after the supposed urgency is
gone. There is no excuse for an injunction given ex parte and lasting until trial; the other side’s
right to move to set it aside is small comfort. Such an interim injunction should last about three
days, long enough to serve a written application for a more durable injunction.⁷
Conversely, there
should not be an ex parte order (over abducting a child) returning the child to the U. S. A. at once,
with no chance to appeal or apply.⁸
A later application to be on notice, to consider whether to
afﬁrm or upset the previous ex parte order, is proper, and may be called a “come back” hearing.⁹
Even worse is getting ex parte permanent relief.¹⁰
An ex parte injunction or search order is for use in a sudden emergency, so where there
is evidence that the applicant has been preparing the application for a long time, indeed has
scheduled it once and then adjourned it, then there is no excuse for any brevity or sloppiness or
nondisclosure.¹¹
