PART 6: RESOLVING ISSUES AND PRESERVING RIGHTS R.6.4
Someone seeking an injunction may lose the right to get it if the party is guilty of delay, if only
because the delay may be evidence that the risk is not great enough to warrant an injunction. The
usual reason for not giving the defendant notice of the application, is that the matter is too urgent
for that. And the delay is bad if it means that the orders are not executed until well into December,
giving little chance for those affected to get any relief before the Christmas vacation.¹
Any interlocutory injunction may be made conditional on the party who got it promptly
prosecuting the main suit (or arbitration).²
A plaintiff getting an interlocutory injunction has a duty
to prosecute his appeal promptly, and if he does nothing for 3 years, the suit may be dismissed.³
(c) Setting Aside Ex Parte Order
If there is an opposed motion to continue (or to rescind) an earlier order given ex parte, any
new evidence should be looked at, and the hearing should be full and de novo.⁴
On a motion to
set aside a previous ex parte order for non-disclosure, now that the court has more evidence from
the defendant, the onus still rests on the plaintiff to continue the injunction.⁵
A motion on notice to set aside an earlier motion with incomplete notice, is not ex parte and
the decision on the second motion cannot be later set aside on ground of misrepresentations at it.⁶
Hearing an application ex parte later, becomes harmless if the judge thereafter rehears the
matter on the merits on notice, hearing both sides.⁷
The natural father should have been given
notice of an application for adoption, but he did not get it. A different judge set it aside. However,
a ﬁve-day trial took place and that argument was not raised then. It is too late on appeal from the
trial to claim that a different judge could not set aside the ﬁrst adoption order.⁸
If someone who
should have got notice stands by and knows that an order is being made, and does not complain
for years, it is too late for him to move to have it set aside.⁹
The court can approve a judicial sale
by listing, despite the absence of a rival offeror, if the latter person sends word that he does not
desire notice and will not attend.¹⁰
After an order is given ex parte, often the court will schedule, or either side will apply for,
review, conﬁrmation, or upsetting that ex parte order. That new hearing may be called a “come
back” hearing. That is a valuable and necessary control, and though some control of the evidence

to be given there may be appropriate, barring any particular evidence there is dangerous and
often wrong. Costs or adjournments are a better regulatory measure, and the whole context
matters, including corroboration or one side's failing to give any evidence at all, or who now has
a chance or knowledge to call evidence which was impossible before, and afﬁdavits based on
information and belief are often proper and get weight.¹
On the merits, an ex parte adoption was later found to have been in the child’s best interests,
and quashing the adoption and ordering a new hearing would delay and prejudice the child. So
the appeal was dismissed. But given the misconduct (lack of notice, etc. ), the Director had to pay
the costs to the appellant which had been ordered below (which were not upset), and also full
indemnity costs of the appeal.²
An unfounded ex parte order was got by one spouse to oust the other spouse from the family
home, and bar contact with the children, despite the absence of previous violence or criminal
activity. It may be expected to produce some panic or consulting several lawyers, or need for
adjournments, or other ﬂurry of activity by the respondent, and maybe more than one written
application. They are not grounds for solicitor-client costs against the party restrained.³
There are extended discussions of why appeal courts usually will not have appeals from
orders made without notice, and why it is far better to apply on notice to the court below to upset
or vary that order.⁴
(d) Miscellaneous
A statutory right to apply ex parte and in camera may override the usual right of the opposing
party to be heard, even if he happens to be present.⁵
Rarely should the defendant be involved in a court application to approve a fee agreement
between a plaintiff under disability and his lawyer, such as a contingency agreement.⁶
So maybe
notice to the opponent is necessary, but he or she would have to show special circumstances in
order to make submissions or adduce evidence?
C. Injunctions Before Trial
1. Introduction
For convenience, all interlocutory injunctions are discussed together here (in Parts C to I),
not merely those given without notice (ex parte). Those without notice are especially discussed
in Part C.3.
On injunctions after trial, see R.9.1n. On preservation orders, see R.6.25. On how long an ex
parte injunction should last, see Part B above.
An injunction is a remedy, not a cause of action.⁷
The rules for perpetual injunctions after trial enforcing covenants are somewhat different,
though they overlap somewhat with those for interlocutory injunctions.⁸

PART 6: RESOLVING ISSUES AND PRESERVING RIGHTS R.6.4
2. Interlocutory Injunctions
There are general textbooks.¹
Many injunctions are given before trial, e. g. to hold the status quo. They are called interlocutory
injunctions. They are normally negative (barring some act), but occasionally mandatory (ordering
some act).²
Often the boundary between negative and mandatory injunctions is not clear in
practice, and the test is substance, not mere wording.³
Interlocutory injunctions usually run until
the end of trial, unless otherwise rescinded. However, injunctions may have to be more temporary
if they are given without notice,⁴
or are based on a covenant which expressly expires before a
trial is likely.⁵
But some injunctions are much more temporary and are called interim injunctions.⁶
They are commonly given to hold the status quo until a proper hearing of the injunction motion.
Even if a contract is clear, an injunction is an equitable remedy which can be withheld if the
bad effects of it on one party would be too large, oppressive, or against public policy. One has to
see whether the injunction would be permanent or interlocutory. An injunction is not necessarily

Performance (Canada L. B. looseleaf) ; Halsbury’s Laws of Canada: Equitable Remedies (electronic
publication LexisNexis 2020).
2 Whether an injunction is truly mandatory is not always easy to tell, as many can be phrased either way.
Terminology is not the test; substance is. Factual status quo and the anticipated effect on the injunction are
more important: ACCEL Can. Hldg. v. Pembina P. L. Corp.2018 ABQB 1039, JCC 1801 17415 (¶’s 18-28).
The implications of a prohibitory injunction may be expense to change a name or advertising or rent new
space, but that does not make it mandatory: Corus Radio v. Harvard Broadcasting 2019 ABQB 880, JCC
1901 12762 (Nov 18) (¶’s 24-25). Some criteria for giving or withholding a mandatory interlocutory injunction
are approved, including the question of whether granting the interlocutory injunction will make it unlikely
that the plaintiff will ever bring the matter onto trial, and whether any due process is being short-circuited by
taking this route: Modry v. Alta. Health Serv.2015 ABCA 265, 606 AR 372. The prima facie case must be
strong, and so one that will probably prevail at trial: id. at ¶ 37; Avmax Aircraft Leasing v. Air X Charter
2022 ABCA 252, Calg 2101 0264 AC (Jul 20) (¶’s 67-70). Geary v. Edm. Remand Centre 2004 ABQB

v. B. N. S.2005 ABQB 704; Horvath v. Syncrude Can.2006 ABQB 185; Aram Sys. v. Novatel 2006 ABQB

20). See also subpart D.1 below.

R.6.4 PART 6: RESOLVING ISSUES AND PRESERVING RIGHTS
permanent just because later it cannot be undone, though some interlocutory injunctions in effect
become permanent.¹
Clients can misunderstand interlocutory orders. It is important that the parties understand
that an interlocutory injunction gives only temporary relief and temporary or tentative conclusions
about the merits; the result at trial could be the opposite.²
Original dispositions of interim and
interlocutory injunction applications do not create issue estoppel which would be a bar at trial. Any
remarks on that subject by the chambers judge would be tentative in nature, and not binding once
the trial started.³
A judge enforcing a settlement and undoing breaches found by an arbitrator,
cannot go into the validity of the settlement.⁴
The Supreme Court afﬁrms the worth and legitimacy of interlocutory injunctions, even where
they last until trial and they give much the same relief as would a trial, especially where no
evidence of actual unfairness or undue inconvenience is given.⁵
A quia timet injunction is one given when the defendant has not yet caused harm or done an
illegal act, but appears likely to do so soon.⁶
An interlocutory quia timet injunction before actual
harm requires very likely harm and imminent real threat.⁷
Restraining orders in family law are types of injunctions, and there is some description of
injunctions and the inherent power of the court, especially to give equitable relief. A slightly
different test for such an injunction in the family law context is suggested. The discussion of the
tests and objective (whether subjective and so forth) is extensive.⁸
If the proposed order is about
a child, the interests to consider are the child’s, not a parent’s.⁹
The law about injunctions is largely judge-made rules of equity; but where family assaults or
the like are involved, see Alberta’s Protection Against Family Violence Act.¹⁰
There is some other
legislation on special topics.¹¹
Temporary injunctions can also be granted to prevent family violence
and ancillary conduct. They may also be given by Justice of the Alberta Court of Justice and some
justices of the peace.¹²
For injunctions under the Protection Against Family Violence Act, see Rr.
12.28 and 12.33.

6-41
PART 6: RESOLVING ISSUES AND PRESERVING RIGHTS R.6.4
Interlocutory injunctions pose many dangers.¹
This whole Part C gives many examples of that.
If an interlocutory injunction is given pending hearing of an application, but the application is
withdrawn afterwards, there can be no breach of the injunction (which has automatically ended),
nor contempt thereby.²
Injunctive relief is not ordinarily or readily available in criminal matters.³
The evils of combining in one proceeding contempt and a new injunction,⁴
or an attachment
order and a Mareva injunction, are discussed.⁵
3. Injunctions Without Notice
Interlocutory injunctions can sometimes be given ex parte⁶
(subject to some restrictions in
some labour legislation).⁷
See also R.6.25n.
If an injunction is given ex parte, the party getting it must disclose to the judge all material
facts, and if he or she did not, that may be a sufﬁcient ground to dissolve the injunction.⁸
If an ex
parte injunction is reviewed (on notice) on other grounds, that is done de novo, and there is no
presumption in favor of maintaining the injunction.⁹
Similarly on a motion to set it aside, the onus
is on the party seeking to preserve the injunction.¹⁰
Delay in moving to set aside an injunction is
not fatal if there is an explanation and no prejudice.¹¹
On duration of an ex parte injunction, see Part B.5(b) above.
If an injunction is given ex parte, and then later a new party is added and gets a similar
injunction or notice, the rules about duty to disclose all relevant information or restricting how
much evidence the original applicant can give on a come back hearing, do not apply.¹²

6-42
R.6.4 PART 6: RESOLVING ISSUES AND PRESERVING RIGHTS
D. Tests for Interlocutory Injunctions
1. Introduction
It is vital to see whether an injunction sought is ﬁnal or interlocutory, because the tests for the
two are signiﬁcantly different.¹
In cases other than interim injunctions and negative covenants, the cases lay down tests for
interlocutory injunctions.²
But the cases differ in terminology and, in details, and vary somewhat
in precisely how the various tests interlock.
One familiar consideration is sometimes put under one traditional heading (such as irreparable
harm), sometimes under another (such as balance of convenience).³
2. Traditional Tests
The ﬁve⁴
traditional tests for an interlocutory injunction are as follows.
(a) The party seeking an injunction must have an arguable cause of action⁵
which it⁶
can enforce
against this defendant, though the Attorney-General (or sometimes a municipality⁷
) may sue
for a wrong done to the public at large.⁸
And the party seeking the injunction must give evidence
to show some merits to its substantive claim. That test is usually now called a serious question

PART 6: RESOLVING ISSUES AND PRESERVING RIGHTS R.6.4
but after this injunction motion.¹
That is called a need to show likely “irreparable harm”. The
harm must be to the applicant (or the public?), not to someone else.²
What is referred to is not
the size of harm; it is whether after trial, fair or reasonable redress for the harm is possible.³
Or maybe is fairly likely; the applicant need not show impossibility.⁴
Irreparable harm only
requires a meaningful risk, and certain harm is not necessary.⁵
A theoretical risk which in
practice is never imposed, is not irreparable harm.⁶
Does it sufﬁce that there is doubt about
whether damages would be adequate?⁷
If damages cannot be computed in a meaningful
way, some cases say that meets this test #3.⁸
Whether damages would sufﬁce is relevant,
but not decisive: the test is whether fair and reasonable redress would be open in a court of
law.⁹
Adequate damages or compensation from a solvent party bar an interlocutory injunction,
because injunctions are equitable remedies imposed only where the common-law remedy
is insufﬁcient.¹⁰
Many things can constitute irreparable harm for purposes of interlocutory
injunctions; the list is not closed.¹¹
Proving irreparable harm is a matter of evidence in each

motion for an injunction, the defendant and others went where the injunction would (if issued) have stopped
them from going, and removed huge sums. That seemed to aid the later injunction: Ermineskin Cree Nation
v. Minde 2006 ABQB 118, 392 AR 342. Death would be irreparable harm: Jin v. Calg. Health Region
2007 ABQB 593, 428 AR 161. So would ending the plaintiff’s sole business: Core Labs. Can. v. Lonkar
Serv.2007 ABQB 505, [2008] 2 WWR 376; Specialized Prop. Evaluations etc. v. Les Evaluations Marc

case, and there are no ﬁxed categories which are always (or never) irreparable harm.¹
The evidence of irreparable harm must be clear, not speculative.²
A contract that certain
acts would work irreparable harm is admissible evidence, but not conclusive?³
Delay in
seeking the injunction may be evidence against irreparable harm!⁴
But test (c) is also a
necessary condition, not a sufﬁcient one.⁵
Irreparable harm must be to the plaintiff seeking
the injunction; harm to others should be considered under (d), balance of convenience.⁶
(d) The applicant seeking the injunction must show⁷
that the balance of convenience favors it.
In other words, it must show that the harm likely to befall the applicant if the injunction were
withheld until trial, is greater⁸
than the likely harm to the opposing party (or to non-parties or to
the public⁹
) should the injunction be given until trial, and then the suit, or the injunction claim, fail
[Footnote 11 continued from prior page]
Bourret etc.2016 ABQB 85, JCC 1501 13965 (Feb 12) (¶’s 60-61). Or loss of market share, especially if
ongoing, but not if quantiﬁable: I Ind. v. Weldco-Beales Mfg.2010 ABQB 404, 500 AR 342; Specialized
v. Les Evaluations, supra. Or loss of a trade secret: Enviro Trace v. Sheichuk, infra. Or breach of a
conﬁdentiality contract: Asher v. Irving, infra (¶ 19). Or leaving an urgent construction project sitting idle for
years? Graham Infrastructure v. Whiteﬁsh L. F. N. , supra (¶’s 71 ff. ).

at trial.¹
This balance is better called the balance of inconvenience.²
Therefore, harm or danger
to one side is not enough, and weighty harm can be overbalanced by greater harm to the other
side.³
The balance depends upon the particular circumstances of the individual case.⁴
The
undertaking as to damages much lowers any harm to the defendant worked by an improperly-
granted injunction⁵
. An interlocutory injunction is to prevent irreparable harm, not to punish,
still less to create it. So a new business should not be shut down, if it is no longer doing any
serious harm by illegal means.⁶
An undertaking by the solvent defendant to keep an account
and pay any damages ordered may swing the balance back against any injunction.⁷
Balance
of convenience does not usually support an injunction to bar enforcement of new legislation.⁸

to equitable relief, such as laches¹⁰
or unclean hands.¹¹
That is one reason that grant of an
injunction is discretionary and it does not go as of right.¹²

R.6.4 PART 6: RESOLVING ISSUES AND PRESERVING RIGHTS
3. Status Quo
One is often tempted to try to preserve the “status quo” until trial, but that factor is probably
often neutral, as it proves surprisingly hard to deﬁne or apply that concept.¹
4. Evidence
There must be good evidence (not mere possibilities) to satisfy each of the proper steps, with
respect to each person proposed to be enjoined.²
But if new evidence contradicts the applicant's
evidence, that is a weighty factor.³
5. Special Types of Injunction
There are some special rules which make it easier to get an injunction to enforce an express
negative covenant in a contract.⁴
Conversely, such an injunction should end when the covenant
sued upon expressly expires.⁵
There are special rules for injunctions if land is involved.⁶
There are special stricter rules for
injunctions against defamation,⁷
against enforcement of letters of credit,⁸
and against competition

And a stricter test for interlocutory mandatory injunctions.²

An

R.6.4 PART 6: RESOLVING ISSUES AND PRESERVING RIGHTS
6. Examples
Interlocutory injunctions arise in many fact settings.¹
There are examples of interlocutory
injunctions granted²
and not granted.³
Where an injunction was unnecessarily given ex parte, and
was very broad, a person affected had it set aside.⁴
There is extended discussion of interlocutory injunctions against enforcing new legislation,
such lawsuits usually alleging that the legislation is unconstitutional.⁵
E. Undertaking as to Damages
1. General⁶
A party seeking an interlocutory injunction almost always⁷
has to give an undertaking as to
damages.⁸
The English sometimes call it a “cross-undertaking”, though “cross-damages” would
be a better name.
The most common form of the undertaking is a promise by the party getting the injunction to
pay any damages which the court later awards because it later concludes that it is just to do so.⁹
The court would normally later award such damages only if it felt in retrospect that the injunction
had been improperly given, and had caused harm to the party enjoined. Then hindsight is proper.
These are not the usual damages which a defendant pays to a plaintiff; they are usually paid (if
awarded) by the plaintiff to the defendant, for usually the plaintiff gets the injunction against the
defendant. (That is where the English get the adjective “cross”. )

6-51
PART 6: RESOLVING ISSUES AND PRESERVING RIGHTS R.6.4
A party who has given an undertaking as to damages can be made to pay such damages,
without any abusive conduct on his part, if the interlocutory injunction he got is set aside.¹
The undertaking is vital, for without it, the balance of inconvenience would usually favor the
defendant, and few interlocutory injunctions would be given.
Usually domestic restraining orders do not require an undertaking as to damages, but getting
an undertaking would be wholesome and would encourage full frank disclosure.²
The undertaking as to damages will be implied unless the court expressly negatives it. But the
better (and common) practice is to give it expressly, precisely, and in writing; one given orally as
an after-thought may not be clear enough. Precision is important.³
The undertaking should be in
the Application or in an accompanying afﬁdavit, not left to the day of the oral hearing.⁴
After all,
the plaintiff’s solvency or the authority of the person signing the undertaking, could be an issue or
require cross-examination.⁵
A few cases say that public bodies need not give an undertaking as to damages, but those
decisions are probably wrong. The balance of convenience is usually no different for such plaintiffs.
Is an undertaking unnecessary if the suit is to divide matrimonial property?⁶
2. Weak Undertakings and Enforcement
There should not be an interlocutory injunction whose only undertaking as to damages is
by a receiver and expressly limited to the inadequate assets held by the receiver. A meaningful
undertaking as to damages is an important part of the balance of inconvenience.⁷
Someone else
may have to give an undertaking too (e. g. , the wealthy creditor who instigated all this), or the
injunction will be denied.⁸
Security may also be required, to ensure that the undertaking is enforceable, especially if the
party seeking the injunction is outside the province or seems insubstantial.⁹
Lack of an undertaking
may become moot if a proper one is given before an appeal can be heard.
The undertaking could be enforced at any time that an interlocutory injunction is ended. That
would often be at trial, and might be after trial,¹⁰
if different evidence had to be led to show the loss

a company of which the defendant to be enjoined is one-half owner and the Board is deadlocked, is not
enough: Scozzafava v. Prosperi 2003 ABQB 248, [2003] 6 WWR 351 (¶ 40). Whether to order security
for the undertaking as to damages is discretionary and entitled to appellate deference: Pendosi Hldg.
v. Forzani Grp.2011 ABCA 171, 505 AR 354. Note unusual conditions and principles for “fortifying” or
not fortifying, an undertaking as to damages to get an interlocutory injunction: Halewood Int. v. Spider
Beverage Co.2014 ABQB 300, [2014] AR Uned 497 (¶’s 86 ff. ).
9 Ritter v. Hoag (#3), supra; Cybersurf v. Shaw, supra.
10 See Ritter v. Hoag (#3), supra.

6-52
R.6.4 PART 6: RESOLVING ISSUES AND PRESERVING RIGHTS
which had been caused by the interlocutory injunction. But there is no set procedure to assess the
compensation, and it may go on for days though technically not be a trial. But it is doubtful that a
new lawsuit is needed.¹
F. Who Can Be Bound by an Injunction
Who can be restrained expressly by an injunction is a complicated question.²
A non-party
to the suit can be enjoined if he or she is so involved in the defendant's wrongful acts that the
non-party facilitates the harm. It is not necessary that the non-party so enjoined be guilty of
wrongdoing.³
But this question may have less importance than one would think. A non-party not named who
knowingly helps a party to disobey an injunction is clearly guilty of contempt of court.⁴
An injunction can expressly bind non-parties, so long as they are ﬁrst told of the order and
given a chance to comply, so that one need not name any John Doe, but doing so is harmless.⁵
The injunction should be clear about to whom it applies.⁶
And it is proper for an injunction to
restrain the defendant, by himself, his agents and servants, from doing such and such. A person
does not need formal service of an injunction to get notice of it; any knowledge sufﬁces.⁷
There may be a duty to make inquiries in some circumstances. People bound by injunctions
have a duty to take care to obey them, and inadvertent but careless disobedience clearly can be
contempt of court.
An order is presumed to take effect at soon as it is pronounced, and entry by ﬁling adds little
or nothing to its force.
Mandatory injunctions are not given against public ofﬁcers.⁸
An interlocutory injunction against
a hearing by an administrative tribunal was refused.⁹
Though ﬁnal injunctions are not issued against the Crown because of the Proceedings Against
the Crown Act, there may be exceptions to that?¹⁰
Provincial superior courts can give injunctions
against the federal Crown in constitutional cases, even if the Charter is not involved.¹¹
See also R.
9.1 n. C.

PART 6: RESOLVING ISSUES AND PRESERVING RIGHTS R.6.4
G. Scope and Terms of Injunction
On who the injunction applies to, see Part F.
It is essential that an injunction be very clear as to what it does and does not forbid (or
require).¹
It is very unfair to impose a vague duty on a party, such as brieﬂy telling it to obey a
multi-clause contract.²
If there is doubt whether a person’s later acts contravene the injunction, he
or she may not be convicted of contempt,³
or the penalty may be slight. Nor should an injunction
purport to compel the impossible.⁴
An injunction, especially a Mareva injunction or an Anton Piller order, should not be wider in
its restrictions, time, people, or geography than necessary, especially when it is given ex parte,
and the fact that the person enjoined could apply later to modify the order is no excuse for over-
breadth.⁵
An injunction should not call for work requiring constant supervision, or whose quality or
adequacy will likely be disputed.⁶
Semble, an application for an injunction or something similar should not produce a split of
issue, or a special trial, without warning.⁷
H. Appeal of an Injunction
There is a considerable measure of discretion in granting an interlocutory injunction.⁸
So
providing that the proper principles were recognized and applied, the Court of Appeal is not
merely to substitute its own opinion.⁹
It will upset a decision as to an interlocutory injunction only
for error of law, new evidence, or change of circumstances.¹⁰
That deference on appeal applies to
weighing competing disadvantages for balance of (in) convenience.¹¹
That is doubly so when the
judge appealed is the case management judge intimately familiar with the suit.¹²

6-54
R.6.4 PART 6: RESOLVING ISSUES AND PRESERVING RIGHTS
Interlocutory injunctions arise on appeal in two ways. First, there may be an appeal from
an interlocutory injunction. And second, an appeal court or judge will occasionally give an
interlocutory injunction pending appeal, so that the appeal will not become academic or nugatory
before it is heard.¹
Leave to appeal an interlocutory injunction is not necessary in Alberta. Even if it forbids
temporarily a foreign suit, it is not a scheduling order for purposes of the fast-track Rules.²
There
should not be permission to appeal from an interlocutory injunction to the Supreme Court of
Canada, especially where the trial is not pursued diligently. The parties should get on to trial
sooner instead.³
If a superior court does not exercise its appellate or judicial review power over an administrative
tribunal, and sits as a court of ﬁrst instance, and an injunction would be discretionary and only
available from the superior court, then in response to a motion for an injunction, the defendant can
raise again an issue as to liability which the tribunal rejected before ﬁning the defendant.⁴
I. Miscellaneous Topics About Injunctions
The Court of King's Bench Act s.17 allows the court to secure the assistance of peace ofﬁcers,
and so some “restraining orders”⁵
call for such help if the respondent is found violating the order;
that is proper. The police dislike a discretion, and so such orders often say that the police shall
assist, but that mandatory language is probably a bad idea. There should not always be a peace-
ofﬁcer clause.⁶
A plaintiff is not obliged to seek an interlocutory injunction if the plaintiff does not want to.⁷
Sometimes a different undertaking is in question. (It should not be confused with the
undertaking as to damages discussed in Part E. ) Where one party seeks an injunction, sometimes
the opposing party suggests that he or she give an undertaking to the court instead. Then no
injunction is made. The effect is much like an injunction, and breach would also be contempt. For
example, if the plaintiff alleges breach of trade mark and wants the defendant to stop using the
goods so marked, the defendant may offer to undertake to the court to keep an account of all
proﬁts made and so to be allowed to go on using and selling the goods, until trial.⁸
At common law, it used to be doubtful whether a public regulatory body could get an injunction
to restrain a public wrong by a private person. That hesitation is hard to understand, and later
authority correctly gives a common-law power to move to enjoin.⁹

6-55
PART 6: RESOLVING ISSUES AND PRESERVING RIGHTS R.6.4
Does public interest always favour enforcing legislation?¹
At common law, an injunction against the Crown was impossible, and s.17(1) of the
Proceedings Against the Crown Act of Alberta substitutes a declaration. There are exceptions,
allowing injunctions against unconstitutional Crown actions, or to prevent communication of an
ultra vires or illegal act.²
The authorities conﬂict somewhat (and they are reviewed at length).
Getting an interlocutory injunction forbidding enforcement of a statute, on constitutional grounds,
is possible but difﬁcult.³
Can a decision on one interlocutory injunction create issue estoppel governing a second
application for an interlocutory injunction between the same parties?⁴
A court adjournment of a company meeting is not the same as an injunction (though similar),
and so the 3-part test for an injunction need not be satisﬁed.⁵
Special rules apply to company
general meetings to elect directors. It takes a strong case to stop shareholders from voting, and
irreparable harm to the directors is not the same as irreparable harm to the company.⁶
Any request for an order without a trial, that someone not dispose of property which he could
otherwise dispose of, is a request for an interlocutory injunction and subject to the usual tripartite
test. If it is for a preservation or attachment order, then the ﬁrst of the three tests is bumped up to
reasonable likelihood that the claim will be established. If it is for a preservation order, pre-existing
property must be shown. And for an attachment order, the Civil Enforcement Act says that there
must be as little inconvenience to the defendant as possible while meeting the order’s purposes.⁷
See further R.6.25n.
A superior court has jurisdiction under the Judicature Act to grant interlocutory injunctions,
where there is no adequate alternative remedy, even in proceedings about a tribunal's decision,
and a stay of execution is a kind of injunction. But the tribunal is usually the better place, with more
expertise.⁸
Semble, one can sue for an interlocutory injunction alone, if later proceedings must be
arbitrated, and the arbitrator cannot give an injunction or similar relief.⁹

R.6.5(1) PART 6: RESOLVING ISSUES AND PRESERVING RIGHTS
Delay could be a ground to end an interlocutory injunction to run until trial, but the onus is on
the defendant, and setting the injunction aside is extraordinary relief. The Alberta Court of Appeal
has been more ﬂexible, but it allows consideration of many factors.¹
Subdivision 2
Application in Foreclosure Action
Notice of Application in Foreclosure Action
6.5(1) In a foreclosure action, notice of every application made by the plaintiff
must be served on each person who ﬁled and served on the plaintiff a statement
of defence, a demand for notice or a notice of address for service.
(2) A defendant or subsequent encumbrancer who is not required to be served
under subrule (1) must be served with notice of an application in a foreclosure
action if the application is for one or more of the following:
(a) a redemption order;
(b) an order that secured property be offered for sale;
(c) an order conﬁrming sale to the plaintiff or other person;
(d) an order for possession, but not a preservation order;
(e) an order appointing a receiver and manager;
(f) a foreclosure order.
(3) A defendant who is not required to be served under subrule (1) must be
served with notice of an application in a foreclosure action if the application is
for personal judgment against that defendant.
(4) A person who is not required to be served under subrule (1) must be served
with notice of an application in a foreclosure action for an order for possession
if the plaintiff seeks possession of secured property from that person.
(5) An offeror or tenderer who is not required to be served under subrule (1)
must be served with notice of an application in a foreclosure action if one or
more offers or tenders have been made on secured property and the application
is for one or more of the following:
(a) an order conﬁrming sale to the plaintiff or another person;
(b) an order for possession, but not a preservation order;
(c) an order appointing a receiver and manager;
(d) a foreclosure order.²
Information Note
Rule 11.24 [Notice of address for service in foreclosure actions] specifies the persons who
may file and serve on the plaintiff in a foreclosure action a notice of address for service in
Alberta.

The plaintiff mortgagee does not fully control a foreclosure suit. A writholder may be given
control of the same foreclosure proceeding.¹
A subsequent encumbrancer avoids the limitation period by riding the ﬁrst mortgagee’s
coattails in its foreclosure suit, and though a limitation could then be pled, the time likely would
not have expired on the relevant date when the main statement of claim was issued, and time
could have been extended by acknowledgment or interim payment.²
There is a general discussion of how judicial sales of land being foreclosed, are conducted
in Alberta.³
The court would not shield a bidder from its loss of its deposit for not closing a land
purchase, a judicial public sale under Court order. It had earlier got an extension of the time to
close, on conditions which it agreed to accept. The integrity of the court sale process is not a
deciding factor, but it is an important one.⁴
A dispute as to valuation of property does not disentitle an Applications Judge from making
an order in a foreclosure suit. The Court of King's Bench Act s.9(3) (b) is only about adjudicative
facts, not competing valuation opinions.⁵
There were competing appraisals for a piece of land,
none cross-examined on, no appraiser commenting on another's work. It was not economical to
try an issue. Two appraisals used adjustments to comparables, one negative, one positive. The
average was taken. Higher weighting was given to the appraisal with closer, more appropriate
comparables.⁶
Each party must be served with each afﬁdavit ﬁled to support an application, even ones only
about the value or price of land being sold.⁷
Even if the order nisi gave directions for sale which

A deserted wife living in the matrimonial home, though having ﬁled no lis pendens, and

unenforceable and illegal, so even after an order for sale, approved by the court and closed, if
the mortgagor does not give up possession, and asks for more time to vacate, the mortgagee
cannot change the locks even if the mortgagor is away for a few days, so any harm done to the
mortgagor's chattels thereby may be recoverable.⁶
Subdivision 3
Responses, Replies and Decisions on Applications
Response and Reply to Application
6.6(1) If the respondent to an application intends to rely on an afﬁdavit or other
evidence when the application is heard or considered, the respondent must reply
by serving on the applicant a copy of the afﬁdavit or other evidence a reasonable
time before the application is to be heard or considered.⁷
(2) The applicant may respond by afﬁdavit or other evidence to the respondent’s
afﬁdavit or other evidence but must
(a) serve the afﬁdavit or other evidence on the respondent a reasonable
time before the application is to be heard, and
(b) limit the response to replying to the respondent’s afﬁdavit or other
evidence.
(3) If either the respondent or applicant does not give the other reasonable
notice, the Court may impose costs on the party who did not give reasonable
notice, and the party who did not give reasonable notice is not entitled to rely
on that party’s afﬁdavit or other evidence unless the Court otherwise permits.

PART 6: RESOLVING ISSUES AND PRESERVING RIGHTS R.6.6(3)

Related Provisions
3.11 (reply materials for originating applications) ; 10.33 (factors in awarding costs) ; 12.44(2)

(reply affidavits in family matters)
For important new directions on delivering an extra hard copy of materials for any Justice
Special Chambers hearing, see R.6.3 n. D.2.
A. Content
Rule 6.6(2) (b) does not bar new evidence by the original applicant if the original respondent
has not ﬁled any evidence. In context of Rr.6.3 and 6.4 and the rest of R.6.6, those Rules just
set out the normal process of bringing applications before the court. The only restriction there is
that “other evidence in support of the application” must be given a reasonable time before the
application is to be heard. Rule 6.6 gives no ground for setting aside an appointment under R.
6.8. Rule 6.6 lets an applicant reply to the respondent’s evidence, but does not mandate that. Nor
does R.6.6 bar either party from ﬁling evidence. Rules 6.3 and 6.6 are about orderly exchange
of information, not primarily about “case splitting”. Though repeated rebuttal afﬁdavits from both
sides may be prevented, a fair chance for each side to present its case is the objective. Tardiness
in giving evidence can generally be dealt with by adjournments or costs, not by excluding relevant
and material evidence.¹
B. Form
The Rules do not contemplate giving formal notice of the grounds for opposing a pending
Originating Application or an ordinary application (motion). Still less do they require a cross-
suit (although notice of opposing evidence must be given). Actual notice of the relief that will be
requested is sufﬁcient, even if it is not in a written application (notice of motion).²
The respondent
to an application need not ﬁle anything seeking costs.³
It is arguable that a “come back hearing” to conﬁrm or upset a previous ex parte order, being
de novo, is under R.6.3(1), not under R.6.6(2) ?⁴
Where there is no basis for applying without notice, and the respondent attends and objects
and seeks an adjournment, it is entitled to its adjournment, and should not be subject to conditions.⁵
If an appeal from a chambers order alleges non-service of the application, evidence of non-
service is needed. And the complaint must be about non-service on the appellant, not on other
parties. And if the appellant appeared and argued the merits, the ground is moot.⁶
May a proposed new defendant participate in opposing the motion to add him? Yes. Rule 1.2
is not very helpful. Pre-2010 cases are less helpful.⁷

R.6.6(3) PART 6: RESOLVING ISSUES AND PRESERVING RIGHTS
C. Timing
On telephone or electronic hearings, see R.6.10n.
Where deadlines are short or near, or times are otherwise tight, recent Court of King’s Bench
practices for booking and ﬁling applications can cause serious dangers and much uncertainty.
This is discussed at length in notes to R.6.2, subpart A.2.
Applications in King’s Bench are now often about important, complex or substantive matters,
such as summary judgment. Written materials, proper preparation, and predictability of timing,
all matter. Having judges idle because of late adjournments or lack of readiness, is undesirable.
Full, complete preparation before selecting a date has advantages and disadvantages. Now the
usual procedure is full preparation, or preparation before selecting a date, or becoming prepared
within a very short time after booking (on pain of losing the date booked). Sometimes the deadline
is impossible to meet, so one must prepare readiness before booking. But if there is a short
deadline for applying, then the whole thing becomes impossible without a time extension, which
(if even possible) requires another application. In theory that policy has advantages, but when
dates available to book are over a year away, that can build a lot of dead time into an application,
or even into the lawsuit. And if one of the parties is given to procrastination, or is deliberately
stalling, the delays can be very bad. It is doubtful that there is any easy way to combat such sloth
or stalling. Formerly one could cross-examine on afﬁdavits and prepare written argument while
waiting for the scheduled date, but that is usually not possible now. Deadlines run from the ﬁling of
the application (or even before), not backwards from the date booked for hearing the application.
For a special chambers application booked months before, and with a waiting list for new
dates, the respondent’s ﬁling and serving a reply afﬁdavit only 3 days before the applicant’s brief
is due, is disrespectful of the applicant and the court, and unreasonable.¹
Filing it one day before,
violating a judge’s deadline, is worse, and there will be a costs penalty, even if the afﬁdavit is
accepted.²
After business hours on the last business day before a long weekend, a few days
before an important motion was to be heard, the respondent gave its opponent a reply afﬁdavit,
contrary to R.6.6(1). This is too common an abuse, leading to lengthy adjournments. Rule 6.6(3)
bars reliance on such late evidence, unless the Court permits.³
The lateness was waived and
the afﬁdavit considered, maybe because the judge thought the party tendering it would lose?⁴
Late production of evidence may lead to penalties.⁵
The previous order giving a deadline for
[Footnote 7 from prior page]

PART 6: RESOLVING ISSUES AND PRESERVING RIGHTS R.6.6(3)
afﬁdavits may be extended if there is no surprise and there is lots of time to cross-examine and
get transcripts and the hearing need not be postponed, but the party getting the time extension
may have to pay costs, not receive them.¹
A party ﬁling a reply afﬁdavit late and so fairly shortly before the date for the hearing of the
application, should give a substantial degree of cooperation to allow quick cross-examination.²
But
a party wishing to enforce quick cross-examination must give a formal notice and serve conduct
money.³
Where a judge has set a procedure (with timelines) for trying and deciding disputed questions
of costs, if one party then tenders late further afﬁdavits of clearly marginal weight, the court may
refuse to accept them, to avoid further delay.⁴
The time limit of ten days for ﬁling an afﬁdavit which will oppose judgment by way of summary
trial under R.7.6 did not apply, and the shorter time limit of 5 days under R.7.8 for certain
objections such as unsuitability of summary trial did apply. The afﬁdavit was therefore in time.⁵
Ultimately both sides agreed as to the disposition of an application. But the motion had been
brought late, forcing the other side to prepare and attend on short notice, so the party moving late
had to pay a premium of $500 as costs.⁶
An abandoned application produced two concessions or
acknowledgments, so some partial costs were proper.⁷
D. Evidence
The court would not accept a 451-page afﬁdavit, mostly without personal knowledge, all
without information or belief, nor a source, and containing lengthy arguments and opinion.⁸
Still
less many papers merely attached to a written argument, none of their sworn to or even available
for cross-examination.⁹
Third-hand hearsay was rejected, especially as the legal basis for its relevance was shaky.¹⁰
An applicant need not ﬁle any new afﬁdavit in support of his or her application, if the other side
has given an afﬁdavit and been cross-examined on it, and the evidence there would be relevant
to support the motion.¹¹
There is a general discussion of evidence tending against an application
and how to handle it.¹²
Where a child’s views are relevant to custody, they should not be given unsworn by counsel in
argument, as that is not evidence and cannot be cross-examined upon.¹³

Questioning on Afﬁdavit in Support, Response and Reply to Application
6.7 A person who makes an afﬁdavit in support of an application or in response
or reply to an application may be questioned, under oath, on the afﬁdavit by a
person adverse in interest on the application, and
(a) rules 6.16 [Contents of appointment notice] to 6.20 [Form of
questioning and transcript] apply for the purposes of this rule, and
(b) the transcript of the questioning must be ﬁled by the questioning
party.³

A. General Right
1. Introduction
This is about cross-examination on an afﬁdavit, and is very different from an examination for
discovery: see R.6.20n. It is most unfortunate that the current Rules call two so very different
things by the same name, "questioning"; that can produce many types of confusion.⁴
2. Right to Cross-Examine
On who can cross-examine, see subpart 7 below.
An adverse party has a wide general right to cross-examine on an afﬁdavit⁵
ﬁled in a lawsuit
for or against an application.⁶
Therefore the adverse party has a right to an adjournment of the

6-63
PART 6: RESOLVING ISSUES AND PRESERVING RIGHTS R.6.7
application in question for that, if there is not time between service and return date, and if he or
she seeks the adjournment promptly.¹
That is especially so if the afﬁdavit is by the party bearing
the onus of proof.²
That is true even if the afﬁdavit is unnecessary or irrelevant, or if the party
ﬁling it seeks to withdraw it.³
But the adverse party has no right to wait silently for weeks, or keep
turning down many dates to cross-examine without reasonable excuse, and then on the return
date of the application ask for such an adjournment.⁴
The right to cross-examine is important. That
an application is due to be heard soon and would be delayed is important, but if that is because
the parties booked the application before the material was ready, denying cross-examination was
not a proportionate response.⁵
But a belated adjournment for more cross-examination may be
needed if the chambers justice sees an important pleaded issue not argued by either party.⁶
Subject to promptness, the right to cross-examine is almost absolute.⁷
But not quite.⁸
The right
to cross-examine is inherent in any factual issue, and there is no need to show untruthfulness
or recklessness of the witness before one can cross-examine; if one could show that, cross-
examination would often not be necessary.⁹
Cross-examination was denied where its stated aim
was a topic not in issue.¹⁰
One need not ﬁle an opposing afﬁdavit in order to cross-examine.¹¹

R.6.7 PART 6: RESOLVING ISSUES AND PRESERVING RIGHTS
There is a right to cross-examine on an afﬁdavit of records, by a party adverse in interest.¹
There is no automatic right to cross examine on an afﬁdavit called for by a statute, and not
related to any litigation.²
The afﬁdavit must either be ﬁled in this suit, or the deponent must have
knowingly consented to its use in this suit.³
But what if the afﬁdavit is to be used in a different suit
without the deponent’s consent?
If the applicant seeks pre-trial relief and the respondent wants to adjourn to cross-examine,
perplexing questions can arise as to whether to give interim relief during the adjournment.⁴
3. Who May Be Cross-Examined
Any deponent (afﬁant) of an afﬁdavit or statutory declaration ﬁled to support or oppose a
pending application may be cross-examined, even if the deponent is not a party.⁵
Or the afﬁant
of an afﬁdavit which either party has stated it will rely on for or against the pending application.
4. Denying Cross-Examination
See also subpart 2 above.
Most of the cases say that a request to adjourn to cross-examine may be refused on the theory
that the cross-examination would not be productive,⁶
or designed only to stall.⁷
It would be fruitless
if the afﬁdavit is procedural in nature.⁸
If a topic is advanced in the alternative and the other
branch succeeds, then cross-examination on the alternative branch is not in issue, and so cross-

PART 6: RESOLVING ISSUES AND PRESERVING RIGHTS R.6.7
examination is unnecessary and not adjourning to cross-examine is no ﬂaw.¹
It may be denied if
the case is plainly statute-barred.²
But one must be careful, and recall that cross-examination is
not limited to attacking the afﬁdavit; it can be to get other evidence from the witness.³
5. Afﬁant Preventing Cross-Examination
Generally, if an afﬁant refuses to be cross-examined, or to answer relevant questions, the
afﬁdavit is not admitted or is given no weight. A party cannot thus put one-sided unchallenged
evidence on the record.⁴
The Court may strike out an afﬁdavit if the deponent is too busy to
come and be cross-examined, and the party adducing the afﬁdavit has delayed revealing the
problem.⁵
An afﬁdavit should not be struck out for missing a deadline to be cross-examined on
an afﬁdavit, where the court later extended the deadline (because of a medical condition) and
cross-examination was held within the extended deadline.⁶
On more penalties for repeatedly not
showing up to be cross-examined, see the Bourque case.⁷
On what to do if the witness refuses to answer some cross-examination questions and the
application is pending (and even urgent), see the Winram, Myron, and Murphy cases.⁸
An
afﬁdavit should not be struck out for objecting to answer questions on cross-examination, even if a
deadline for being cross-examined had been set, especially where the objections were arguable.
Moving to compel answers is usually the better remedy.⁹
6. Late Cross-Examination
On adjournments of an application to allow cross-examination, see n.4.2 above.
The right to cross-examine ends when the application is decided or withdrawn,¹⁰
or if there
is no application. But one can cross-examine on an afﬁdavit used to get an order, if there is a
pending application to set aside the order.¹¹
While the application is pending, an appointment to

7. Who Can Cross-Examine
The Rule changed in 2010. The previous Rule mentioned a party, and case law allowed
someone with an immediate adverse interest to cross-examine; now R.6.7 is broader and gives
that right to a person adverse in interest.⁴
But who can make use of the resulting transcript may
be limited to a party to the application?⁵
Anyone with an immediate interest in the outcome of the suit may conduct the cross-
examination, including one with a statutory right to defend for a party.⁶
If the person wanting to
cross-examine is not a party, but says that he has an interest in the land and the motion, he should
be given a chance to show that. If all that is denied, he has a right to appeal.⁷
Standing to cross-examine on an afﬁdavit ﬁled for a government Reference to an appeal court
is somewhat puzzling.⁸
B. Duty to Cross-Examine?
The rule in Browne v. Dunn does not require a party to cross-examine on an afﬁdavit for a
pending summary judgment application, in order to lead opposing evidence, especially when
the afﬁants are parties easy to get evidence from.⁹
The rule in Browne v. Dunn exists to help
weighing evidence, but on a summary judgment motion evidence is not weighed, and so that rule
does not apply to such a motion.¹⁰
But if evidence conﬂicts, failure to cross-examine on central or
core issues is serious.¹¹
There may be a duty to cross-examine if the application pending seeks
ﬁnal relief, and the credibility issue was not known or revealed.¹²
A party's suggestion that a fact upsets a conclusion or assertion in an afﬁdavit, may be
dismissed as speculation, if that party did not cross-examine on the relevant witness afﬁdavit.¹³

PART 6: RESOLVING ISSUES AND PRESERVING RIGHTS R.6.7
Not always must a witness’ evidence be taken to be true just because she was not cross-
examined on her afﬁdavit.¹
C. Content of Cross-Examination on Afﬁdavit
1. References
On the form of questioning and the scope of cross-examination generally, see R.6.20n. This
part of this book is approved.²
2. Scope of Application and Cross-Examination
Relevance of cross-examination questions depends on the application then pending.³
Cross-
examination is not conﬁned to the topics in the afﬁdavit.⁴
So even if there was a narrow application
on foot when the witness refused to answer the question, if there is a later very wide application
and request to use the cross-examination evidence for it, then an application to compel an answer
may have a wider scope and chance of success.⁵
Conversely, if the issues for proposed cross-
examination have been conceded for the application, or are not to be gone into in much depth,
then cross-examination can be refused.⁶
And the answers should be interpreted in the context
of the application pending when they were given.⁷
The court will not compel an answer to an
irrelevant question.⁸
There is a conﬂict about the test for relevance: is it a semblance of relevance?⁹
Or is it
“relevant and material?”¹⁰
The test for relevance may be affected by proportionalities, i. e. probative
value outweighing the expense and delay involved.¹¹
The perjorative word "ﬁshing" crops up in
this content, though it is vague and usually conclusory. One judge deﬁned it as a request for
information founded only on conjecture or speculation or dubious relevance, or posing an onerous
burden to answer.¹²
If the topic of a question on cross-examination on an afﬁdavit clearly ﬂows from one passage
to the afﬁdavit (which passage is not abandoned), then the party ﬁling the afﬁdavit has conceded
relevance of the topic, and some questions on the topic must be answered.¹³

R.6.7 PART 6: RESOLVING ISSUES AND PRESERVING RIGHTS
Cross-examination can also go into the witness’ (afﬁant’s) credibility, and reliability of his or her
evidence.¹
But is credibility somehow limited to what is in the afﬁdavit?²
Cross-examination on afﬁdavit and examination for discovery questions are very different, and
the former should not turn into the latter.³
3. Informing Self
Sometimes an afﬁant being cross-examined on his or her afﬁdavit can be required to inform
himself or herself. He or she is like a witness at trial, and so expected to have taken reasonable
steps to inform himself or herself beforehand on the subject on which he or she is expected to
testify, and is expected to bring with him or her all records in his or her control relevant to the
issues in the lawsuit. The afﬁant’s informing himself or herself should be ordered only:⁴
(a) where
the deponent referred to other information or documents in his afﬁdavit, or must have done so
to make a statement he swears to;⁵
or (b) if the issue subject sought is important and obeying
the undertaking would not be unduly onerous,⁶
or (c) where the afﬁant swore on information
and belief,⁷
or (d) maybe where there is a refusal to give information or documents proper?⁸
The
witness cross-examined is not ordinarily required to inform himself or herself further than that;
the court should be cautious about such an order.⁹
There is an extended discussion of when the
work of answering the undertaking is excessive.¹⁰
In particular, the afﬁant cannot be compelled
to undertake to produce further records or information which is only slightly relevant and is not
truly material.¹¹
Whether the witness must undertake to give more records, or to request them from someone
else, during cross-examination on an afﬁdavit, depends on whether the deponent referred
to or relied upon these documents, whether the undertaking about an important issue in the
application, not too onerous to carry out, and whether the results signiﬁcantly would assist the
court in deciding.¹²
However, the afﬁant may voluntarily give a broader undertaking e. g. to produce non-material
papers. The afﬁant may gain advantages by volunteering such undertakings, e. g. avoiding having
to come back later for further cross-examination. The afﬁant is bound by such voluntary or broader

PART 6: RESOLVING ISSUES AND PRESERVING RIGHTS R.6.7
undertakings, and can be forced to comply with them.¹
The undertaking given can be interpreted
in light of the discussion in the transcript leading up to it.²
If cross-examination is pointless because it is about topics no longer relevant, there is no
reason to enforce such undertakings.³
4. Improper Conduct
There are comments on various types of offensive and improper and abusive conduct by
a lawyer cross-examining an afﬁant on her afﬁdavit, including cutting off the witness’ answers
and making unnecessary offensive comments. This is not an examination for discovery, but is
evidence, all before the court. Absence of a judge during questioning does not permit liberties.⁴
On cross-examination about an afﬁdavit of records, see Part A above.
D. Miscellaneous
Rule 6.7(b) leaves no discretion; the party questioning (cross-examining) on an afﬁdavit must
ﬁle a transcript with the court.⁵
On a motion, a party can put into evidence answers of its own
afﬁant when cross-examined by the opposition. This is not an examination for discovery, and all
cross-examination answers automatically are part of the record before the court, upon which any
party may rely.⁶
Rarely can any party to the lawsuit be excluded from attending someone else’s cross-
examination, even if this party will also be a witness or cross-examined later.⁷
The right to cross-examine does not necessarily give the cross-examiner the right to receive
costs, and indeed he or she may have to pay them, if the cross-examination proves useless or too
broad.⁸
Highly obstructional conduct calls for higher costs, even solicitor-client costs.⁹
Even if an epidemic has prevented face-to-face court proceedings, the court may order that
necessary cross-examination on an afﬁdavit be conducted at a distance by electronic means,
without waiting for the end of the epidemic.¹⁰

If a party selects a distant afﬁant, who must then travel here to be cross-examined, the court
may cut down the conduct money paid to reimburse the deponent’s travel expenses.¹
Flaws or gaps in one party's afﬁdavit maybe cured or plugged, by evidence by the opposing
party, or by the cross-examination on it.²
Rule 13.18 which bars information and belief in afﬁdavits without the source of the information,
does not apply to information elicited by cross-examination on an afﬁdavit. The witness cross-

Questioning Witness Before Hearing
6.8 A person may be questioned under oath as a witness for the purpose of
obtaining a transcript of that person’s evidence for use at the hearing of the
application, and
(a) rules 6.16 [Contents of appointment notice] to 6.20 [Form of
questioning and transcript] apply for the purposes of this rule, and
(b) the transcript of the questioning must be ﬁled by the questioning
party.⁵

Rule 6.8 is most useful when a prospective witness does not want to swear an afﬁdavit.⁷
There

on an afﬁdavit for a pending application.⁸

PART 6: RESOLVING ISSUES AND PRESERVING RIGHTS R.6.8
There are few restrictions on when this Rule may be used,¹
so long as it is to get information
for an application (motion) then pending, and is not merely a concealed questioning (examination
for discovery).²
The usual test is whether the witness could give relevant evidence.³
The Rule
cannot be used where the questions to be asked would likely be irrelevant.⁴
Any party has a prima facie right to use R.6.8 without consent or court order, even if the
witness does not want to cooperate or is adverse, and an appointment to do so cannot be set aside
as abusive without evidence of abuse. It cannot be set aside under R.6.6. One cannot assume
that the questions will be improper, nor objected to. Delay can be controlled by case management
or costs, and overbreadth of papers ordered produced, by amendment. The transcript will all be
evidence before the court, not just what one party seeks to read in.⁵
One can even examine someone from the opposing side.⁶
Under R.6.8(a), the physical set-up of the examination looks much like questioning (an
examination for discovery) or examination on afﬁdavit; but the legal rules are very different.⁷
For
one thing, the witness is the witness of the counsel procuring his or her examination. And for
another, all his or her answers are evidence for and against all parties. Using either R.6.8 or R.
6.11(1) (g) can be dangerous, especially if the witness is unfriendly or an unknown quantity. The
witness is the witness of the lawyer who compels his or her attendance, so that lawyer can only
examine in chief, with no leading questions.⁸
Cross-examination is for the opposing lawyer. The
whole transcript goes into evidence, so any party can rely on any answer, as under R.6.7.⁹
The
witness may have his or her own counsel present if he is not a party?¹⁰
On the form of questioning and the scope of cross-examination, see R.6.20n.
This Rule applies to every application in the Court of King's Bench unless a Rule, enactment,
court order or direction says the contrary.¹¹
This R.6.8 may be used to get evidence for a Surrogate
application, and is not a Rule about discovery.¹²

6-72
R.6.8 PART 6: RESOLVING ISSUES AND PRESERVING RIGHTS
Rule 6.8 is not clear and is open to two possible interpretations, so one can look at historical
matters, such as the Institute’s July 2004 consultation memorandum. What was intended here
was streamlining and clarifying of 1968 Rr.266, 267 and 268. The Institute drafting R.6.8 seems
to have run together hostility of a witness with his adversity, and the interest of a witness based on
whom he works for. R.6.8 allows cross-examination of a witness by the party calling that witness
without ﬁrst having a judge declare the witness hostile? That will avoid having to make a separate
application. Leading questions are not absolutely prohibited even in direct examination, so one
does not always have to resort to cross-examination to ask a leading question.¹
Even if co-employees of a deponent are subpoenaed to supplement his cross-examination,
the witnesses are witnesses of the party calling them; can they be cross-examined by that party?²
If an Applications Judge summarily dismisses an action and then there is an appeal from
that, the appeal is almost de novo, and so for that purpose the action and the application are
considered still alive, and there is no bar to an examination under R.6.8.³
The person examining
must choose the witness, and cannot force the opposite party to choose one. There is no right of
a party to remain silent and so to be exempt from R.6.8, even if the inquiry is into one’s resources.
A party’s lawyer could be examined under R.6.8 after the party waived privilege by blaming
her lawyers for the present problem.⁴
The fact that a lot of what a proposed witness may know is
privileged is no reason to bar his or her examination totally.⁵
Use of much more general legislation to allow examination of a witness (in a bankruptcy
proceeding) is found in the Metanczuk case.⁶
B. Procedure
Do not forget conduct money for the witness.⁷
Where an examination with no one to control it would degenerate into ﬁghting, the court
instead ordered oral evidence (and cross-examination) on the application, before a judge, who
could rule on disputed questions and conduct.⁸
Ordinarily one can get a stay pending arbitration, but there is an exemption for summary
judgment. That exemption only applies if there has ﬁrst been a summary judgment motion ﬁled.
Therefore, such questioning of a witness for that is permitted only if there is such a valid motion
pending.⁹

How the Court Considers Applications
6.9(1) The Court may consider a ﬁled application in one or more of the following
ways:
(a) in person, with one, some or all of the parties present;
(b) by means of an electronic hearing if an electronic hearing is permitted
under rule 6.10 [Electronic hearings];
(c) by a process involving documents only.
(2) Applications may be decided by a judge or applications judge.
[Alta. Reg.124/10;136/22]

A. Jurisdiction of Applications Judges
1. Law About Applications Judges' Powers
What kind of matters an Applications Judge can hear is not in the Rules, and is set by s.9 of
the Court of Queen's Bench Act.⁵
It says that Applications Judges can only act if there is a matter
brought or proposed to be brought in that court. If so, generally an Applications Judge has the
same powers as a judge sitting in chambers.⁶
Given the deﬁnition of “court”, that would probably
cover almost everything in a normal lawsuit except a trial. However, an Applications Judge does
not have special powers outside a suit, given by statute to “a judge. ” An Applications Judge does

R.6.9(2) PART 6: RESOLVING ISSUES AND PRESERVING RIGHTS
have special powers given by statute to “the Court, ” so long as that was not the Alberta Court
of Justice or the Court of Appeal. An Applications Judge can make various orders under the
Maintenance Enforcement Act.¹
But s.9 of the Court of King's Bench Act gives some exceptions to Applications Judges’
general powers, matters which only a judge can handle:
1. appeals or anything similar or connected to appeals;²
2. varying or rescinding a judge’s order;
3. stays of proceedings, unless the parties all consent; but an applications judge can stay
license suspensions under the Motor Vehicle Administration Act, and can vary redemption
periods or reopen ﬁnal foreclosures in his or her own orders;
4. trials of actions (except as a referee;)³
5. deciding contested fact questions, unless the parties consent;⁴
6. criminal proceedings or liberty of the subject or other aspects of civil contempt;⁵
7. injunctions;⁶
8. certiorari, prohibition, mandamus, or quo warranto.
Section 96 of the Constitution Act 1867 has been interpreted as putting some limits on what
an Applications Judge can do, but the majority of cases hold that the type of things which Alberta
Applications Judges do are constitutional: for example, granting summary judgment. If magistrates
heard cases in 1867 broadly similar to a topic (even if superior courts then also did), a province
can create a new statutory tribunal and give it exclusive power over that topic without violating
s.96. In that case, that ends the question, and (in that event) goals and remedies of the modern
legislation are irrelevant.⁷

1 RSA 2000 c. M-1.
2 An appeal from a bankruptcy registrar to a judge may or may not be a true appeal, given s.96 of the B. N. A.
Act, and the judge expressed no ﬁnal decision on that point: Re Dornan (Dingwall v. Dornan) 2015 ABQB
647, 29 CBR(6th) 57 (¶’s 24-25). Asking an Applications Judge to review or change the decision of another
decision-maker, even on procedural matters like service, is in substance an appeal. Applications judges
cannot hear appeals, here from a tenancy board: Abougouche v. Miller (M) 2015 ABQB 724, 84 CPC(7th)

has inherent power to stay it or set it aside if it was given in breach of the rules of natural justice, and an

6-75
PART 6: RESOLVING ISSUES AND PRESERVING RIGHTS R.6.9(2)
An applications judge or a justice can hear and decide an application for summary judgment, and
an Applications Judge is bound by precedent not to ﬁnd the Rules of Court on that unconstitutional.
To hold otherwise would be to invalidate the Rules and the Court of Queen's Bench Act. Decisions
suggested to indicate the contrary are no longer accurate under current Rules or current case
law. And 1916 Court of Appeal authority says that given the easy appeal, the decision is really
by the Court not the Applications Judge.¹
The Queen's Bench Act gives an Applications Judge
full power to hear a summary judgment, but not contested fact matters unless the parties agree.
Weir-Jones changed the rules for deciding disputed facts and the procedure is much more
like that on a trial. But summary judgment is still not a trial; it is really prediction, and does not
weigh competing pieces of evidence. That sort of thing goes on in many types of interlocutory
application. Direct conﬂicts of evidence are not usually decided by Applications Judges. The line
between weighing disputed evidence and deciding if the evidence merits summary judgment
is really deciding whether there is a real issue to be tried. Applications Judges hear (in the ﬁrst
instance) all summary judgment applications. The “fact-ﬁnding” exercise is really notional.²
2. Fact Disputes
Though an Applications Judge is not to weigh facts,³
there is no weighing involved in ﬁnding
that the evidence does not clash and that the only issues about it is its admissibility, or in deciding
that summary judgment is or is not appropriate.⁴
A dispute as to valuation of property does not disentitle an Applications Judge from making an
order in a foreclosure suit. The Court of Queen's Bench Act s.9(3) (b) is only about adjudicative
facts, not competing valuation opinions.⁵
Deciding damages is not forbidden to an Applications
Judge⁶
Calgary clerks were instructed not to put before a judge an application which a Master can
hear. Edmonton clerks would ask the party moving to change the application to read that a Master
will hear the matter, in those circumstances.⁷
A December 15, 1993 circular of the Associate Chief
Justice in Edmonton directed the Clerks automatically to put on a Master’s list applications which
a Master could hear, but whose notice (application) says that they are returnable before a judge.
Confusion can then occur (two different sides waiting in two different courtrooms, each thinking
that the other is a no-show).⁸
Masters are now called Applications Judges.
The December 15, 1993 circular is now enshrined in Practice Note 2 which states that where
possible, any application involving a matter that is within the jurisdiction of an Applications Judge
should be returnable before the Applications Judges, not a Justice of the Court of King’s Bench.
The Practice Notes sets out the applications that can be heard by Applications Judges and
applications that are outside of an Applications Judges’ jurisdiction. The Practice Note says that
applications within the jurisdiction of an Applications Judge brought in Justice Chambers will be
removed from the Justice Chambers list and placed on the Applications Judge List.

Since there are backlogs of pending applications, trying to get them heard on a shorter list,
especially the commercial list, is desired by many applicants. One decision refuses that, analyzing
many aspects of the powers of an applications judge, and insists that applications judges hear
applications which are within their powers.¹
3. Miscellaneous
If an Applications Judge would ordinarily hear the application, but an Applications Judge is not
available and a judge hears it, he must sit as a justice, not an Applications Judge, and the only
right of appeal from the judge is to the Court of Appeal.²
B. Applications in Chambers
1. Method
The Rule recognizes oral, electronic, and “desk” applications. In that third instance, the
application is decided on written material sent in via the Clerk.³
The Covid pandemic created a number of changes to when where and how Applications were
heard in the Court of King’s Bench (formerly Queen’s Bench). Those are virtually all gone now.⁴
A complex matter should not be heard in morning chambers, especially as it often leads to
partial or tentative orders, whereas an afternoon special would decide it all.⁵
On how to book and ﬁle various kinds of application, see R.6.2 n. A.
2. Timing
The respondent to an application had been served, but wanted more time. He was properly
given a choice of oral argument at once on the return day, or further submissions in writing to be
ﬁled later. The respondent to the application chose the later written argument.⁶
On running out of time after partial hearing of a motion, see R.6.3n. G.5. On a new event
occurring partway through the argument of an application, see R.6.3n. G.1.
There is no rule that a bulky lawsuit cannot be dismissed under R.4.33 in morning chambers,
or needs a special hearing, if the facts and issues for R.4.33 are not complex or bulky especially
if the judge reviews the afﬁdavits before the hearing. The decision not to adjourn is discretionary.⁷
Applications should be heard at one time, not chopped up with preliminary issues heard one
day and the main issue another.⁸
A preemptive cross application to bar the applicant on a previous
application from making certain arguments should not be severed and heard ﬁrst; the whole
application should be heard at the same time.⁹

3 See R.12.50 on desk divorces.
4 For details, see notes to R.6.2n. A.3 and 6.3n. C.2.

PART 6: RESOLVING ISSUES AND PRESERVING RIGHTS R.6.9(2)
Through a misunderstanding, a chambers judge heard argument only on a preliminary point,
and gave decision before hearing the losing party’s counsel on the merits of the (main) application.
On appeal, a new hearing was ordered.¹
Someone who had just hired a lawyer got a one-month adjournment of cross-applications, but
conditional on more security for costs and paying (out of existing security) to his opponent costs
recently taxed (assessed), and costs of the adjournment.²
3. Recording
If there is any chance whatever of an appeal, it is the duty of counsel to have any oral reasons
for decision recorded somehow.³
Usually there is some form of electronic sound recorder in
the courtroom (often a central computer memory). If it is not already running, counsel should
ask the Applications Judge or justice either to have the Clerk turn it on, or to furnish written
reasons. If an appeal is ﬁled, counsel should have Transcript Management Services transcribe
the electronically-recorded reasons and furnish the transcript.⁴
Counsel cannot expect a justice
or Applications Judge to recreate the oral reasons six months later, and one should not appeal
without transcribing any reasons already given and somehow recorded.
If a Chambers justice gives no reasons to dismiss a motion, and the parties cannot agree
whether he did so on the merits, the Court of Appeal may send the matter back to King's Bench
to examine the papers, see which were relevant, and then decide on the merits.⁵
4. Consent
A respondent to an application can confess judgment to the whole application, but cannot
consent to some parts and call that an agreement, while still litigating the rest.⁶
5. Reconsideration
Whether one can ask the justice or Applications Judge to reconsider his or her decision is
complicated.⁷
Refusal of an interlocutory injunction can be relitigated if material new evidence
is found, through an accounting ordered at the time of the original refusal.⁸
But reopening is
exceptional and not allowed merely for more argument, even by a self-represented party.⁹
6. Misconduct
On misconduct of counsel by disobeying procedural directions by a previous judge, taking
an order by one justice or an Applications Judge to a different justice or an Applications Judge
to amend and sign, misstating various facts to the court, and faking the appearance of notice to
opposing counsel, see the Shehu case.¹⁰

7. Miscellaneous
On notice and surprise, and their importance, see Rr.6.1 n. A.2 and 6.3 nn. B, C.5, D.3, and E.
Applications under the Insurance Act allow for advance payment orders against insurance
companies. Such an application should be made by Originating Application and not in any existing
torts action. Information from one of these court ﬁles will not be on or inadvertently disclosed in
the other ﬁle.¹
A judge heard an application to require psychologists to disclose their notes. Though in the
meantime disclosure had been made, there was still an adversarial relationship between the
government and the parents and an ongoing ﬁght over custody, so despite mootness of this issue,
the court let the matter be decided.²
A new hearing was ordered on appeal, because the chambers judge granted one party’s
support motion on the merits, before dealing with a cross motion for more disclosure of income,
and never did rule on that.³
Such disclosure could well have helped rebut the support income
motion.
A chambers decision was upset for bias. The judge arranged to have police and child welfare
ofﬁcers attend, which seemed to show that he had prejudged. On an earlier occasion he got very
upset with the conduct of the parties and properly said that he could not go on that day. If the
matter was to proceed, a different judge should have heard it.⁴
The appellant had been removed from the chambers room and then later on he returned. It
was wrong for the chambers judge to hear further argument in the interim.⁵
Prejudice to the respondent is a relevant factor in any application (if it is alleged), and must be
balanced with any other relevant factors.⁶
C. Disputed Facts and Evidence
1. General Rule
The most important and most overlooked rules about applications have to do with contested
facts. For the most part, an Applications Judge has no jurisdiction to decide fact disputes.⁷
The traditional cases say that a judge must not do so on the basis of competing afﬁdavits.⁸
But sometimes, the judge can decide the motion without deciding the fact dispute; e. g. , for an
injunction the judge need only ﬁnd a serious issue to try. Or one party may lose even if the
facts are assumed to be as he contends. Immaterial disputed facts may be ignored.⁹
Otherwise,

PART 6: RESOLVING ISSUES AND PRESERVING RIGHTS R.6.9(2)
contested facts (especially credibility ﬁghts) traditionally force the judge either to dismiss the
motion, to adjourn it to the trial judge, or to direct trial of an issue with live evidence.¹
A summary
trial might properly be ordered in some cases. Much the same is true of missing facts.²
Issues from taking a child to live in another province ordinarily need a more comprehensive
hearing than morning chambers offer. At the outset of every application counsel should tell the
chambers judge the exact nature of the application and if it is one of this type, why it cannot wait
for an afternoon special chambers applications.³
2. Exceptions
There are some times when a fact dispute does not bar a decision in chambers on afﬁdavits.
The traditional ones are narrow, such as
(a) a summary trial (which in law is a trial, not an application),⁴
(b) setting the price under a Rice order,⁵
(c) maybe a statute or Rule expressly allowing a summary hearing,⁶
(d) maybe ﬁxing interim maintenance, and
(e) desirability of ruling promptly on amendments and not deferring them to the trial judge,
thus leaving the pleadings unsettled.⁷
(f) conﬂicts on mere opinions, not facts,⁸
(g) an afﬁdavit may get little weight if it is argumentative and merely conclusive, lacking any
detail or supporting evidence.⁹
