6-80
R.6.10(1) PART 6: RESOLVING ISSUES AND PRESERVING RIGHTS
In addition, the modern shift of philosophy to being more receptive to summary disposition of
suits without trial quite logically has been extended to many other types of chambers application.
Where an application reveals substantial factual disputes and conﬂicting evidence, but is not
necessarily error for a chambers judge to make ﬁndings on some of the disputed facts, purely with
afﬁdavits, no oral evidence. The test is whether the record permits a fair and just adjudication.¹
On an issue of what were the contract's terms, there was signiﬁcant objective evidence, so the
judge need not give any weight to one party's explanation that he did not read the contract before
signing it.²
A vague statement in one afﬁdavit that the deponent “does not accept” the statements in the
other afﬁdavit, does not create such a clash of evidence.³
The Vue-Weekly case also deals with
ﬁnding contempt on conﬂicting evidence.⁴
Delay may remove one’s right to seek trial of an issue.⁵
A party who argued in chambers that
the facts were undisputed and simple and that the case was ﬁt for summary decision in chambers,
could not later appeal on the ground that the matter was not ﬁt for a summary decision.⁶
A decision should be made in chambers and the matter not be deferred to trial, if the evidence
supports the application and is not contradicted,⁷
or if there is really no evidence to support the
application.⁸
An appeal to a King's Bench justice (e. g. from the Alberta Court of Justice) can be decided in
chambers, on the record, if the issue is essentially one of law.⁹
A custody ﬁght and change of custody do not necessarily call for live evidence, if the afﬁdavits
do not conﬂict on material points; the test is whether the chambers judge can fairly and justly
decide on the basis of the existing evidence.¹⁰
Where the plaintiff plainly puts his physical state into issue, as from previous applications the
Chambers Judge knows, a R.5.41 examination may be patently necessary, so the Chambers
Judge may conﬁne argument to whether the experts selected are suitable.¹¹
Electronic Hearing
6.10(1) In this rule, “electronic hearing” means an application, proceeding,
streamlined trial or trial conducted, in whole or in part, by electronic means
in which all the participants in a hearing and the Court can hear each other,
whether or not all or some of the participants and the Court can see each other
or are in each other’s presence. [Alta. Reg.126/23]

(2) An electronic hearing may be held if

Related Provisions
8.18 (electronic evidence at trial)

The 2021 amendment to R.6.10(2) (b) makes it plain that the Court can choose an electronic
hearing without either party's having requested that.
Rule 6.10 allows telephone conference applications.¹
The Court of Appeal also allows telephone
conferences for procedural motions before one judge where not all the persons involved are in or
near the same city.
Telephone applications can be very difﬁcult if the matters involved are lengthy or complicated.
At the very least, counsel should try to ensure that all concerned receive the relevant papers well
ahead of time, by courier, electronic means, or fax.
The Covid pandemic hugely expanded use of electronic hearings but in the Court of King’s
Bench, that has largely ended, as revealed by the Court of King's Bench website.
Counsel may appear in Regional Chambers by video and by telephone as set out on the
website. (Schedule/Family & Civil Regular Chambers).
Some criteria to use to evaluate a request to hear evidence by television hook-up are given in
the Hinkley case.²
Video teleconferencing is not inherently inferior, and sometimes even superior.
On the other hand, some cheaper versions can be quite poor in picture and sound. There is no
statutory or constitutional right to a physical confrontation in the same room if the video hook-up
is clear, in any event where credibility is not a big issue, nor is demeanor.³

1 Further details are set in King's Bench Civil Practice Note 5, found in Volume 2, p.3-37.
2 R. v. Hinkley 2011 ABQB 567, 523 AR 400.

6-82
R.6.11(1) PART 6: RESOLVING ISSUES AND PRESERVING RIGHTS
This Rule does not apply directly to pre-hearing procedures such as cross-examination on
afﬁdavit, but this Rule applies to these indirectly via R.1.7 on analogies.¹
Evidence at Application Hearings
6.11(1) When making a decision about an application the Court may consider
only the following evidence:
(a) afﬁdavit evidence, including an afﬁdavit by an expert;
(b) a transcript of questioning under this Part;
(c) the written or oral answers, or both, to questions, under Part 5
[Disclosure of Information] that may be used under rule 5.31 [Use of
transcript and answers to written questions];
(d) an admissible record disclosed in an afﬁdavit of records under rule
5.6 [Form and contents of afﬁdavit of records];
(e) anything permitted by any other rule or by an enactment;
(f) evidence taken in any other action, but only if the party proposing
to submit the evidence gives every other party written notice of that
party’s intention 5 days or more before the application is scheduled
to be heard or considered and obtains the Court’s permission to
submit the evidence;
(g) with the Court’s permission, oral evidence, which, if permitted, must
be given in the same manner as at trial.
(2) An afﬁdavit or other evidence that is used or referred to at a hearing and that
has not previously been ﬁled in the action must be ﬁled as soon as practicable
after the hearing.

Related Provisions
6.11(2) (use of unfiled affidavits) ; 6.20(5) (filing transcripts) ; 8.17(3) (evidence in other

actions); 13.19 (hearsay in affidavits) ; 13.20 (requirements for affidavits) ; 13.25 (use of any
filed affidavits); 13.29 (certified copies of records) ; 13.31 (authenticated photographs of
property)
On conﬂicting evidence, see R.6.2 n. , 6.3 n. , and 6.9 n. C.
A. Need for Evidence
1. General Rule
Even on a chambers application, the court can only act on sworn²
evidence³
(or occasionally,

2 or afﬁrmed.

PART 6: RESOLVING ISSUES AND PRESERVING RIGHTS R.6.11(2)
on its own records: R.13.29).¹
Even for procedural topics like abuse.²
Any afﬁdavit ﬁled in the action
can be used: R.13.25. An afﬁdavit ﬁled earlier in this suit can be used for the new application, if
there is written notice that it will be so used.³
Factual statements in court by counsel are not evidence, and argument proves no fact.⁴
Unsworn statements, by counsel or anyone else, are not evidence.⁵
Even a case management
judge cannot make a substantive order without evidence, such as child access.⁶
Costs hearings
also are subject to the rules of evidence.⁷
Nor is a letter handed in to the chambers judge without oath or other proof, evidence.⁸
Oral
submissions about a bundle of vouchers cannot be relied upon, especially if they are ﬁled too late
for the opposing party to test or rebut, still less if the party tendering them had a duty to produce
them far earlier. A decision based on them cannot stand.⁹
But if documents before the court show that information sought by a subpoena is plainly
irrelevant, then the subpoena can be quashed without further evidence.¹⁰
2. Reciting Supposed Facts
Facts must not be stated in chambers by counsel, unless they are in a ﬁled afﬁdavit or shown
by the court’s own records.¹¹
A counsel stating facts implies to the justice or Applications Judge that
he or she is summarizing an afﬁdavit; if counsel is not, that is serious professional misconduct,
and likely to tarnish counsel’s credibility permanently.¹²

R.6.11(2) PART 6: RESOLVING ISSUES AND PRESERVING RIGHTS
It is error for a chambers judge to rely on unsworn statements by the respondent to a summary
judgment or summary dismissal motion, to ﬁnd an issue for trial.¹
A trustee in bankruptcy was criticized for not putting all the facts before the Chambers Judge
and just telling enough to make out a prima facie case in his favor. Trial of an issue was directed.²
But it is proper to tell the judge what the Clerk of the Court did or received or rejected, and
what other applications are ﬁled and pending, and sometimes one must reveal such information.³
3. Judicial Notice
On judicial notice in applications, see R.6.3 n. C. On notice of court proceedings and
documents, see subpart C.2 below.
A chambers judge was reversed for having taken judicial notice of facts. The test for judicial
notice is strict, partly because no testing by cross-examination is possible, and any doubt
precludes taking notice.⁴
The judge must be cautious, all the more so when the act to be noticed
is a social phenomenon, and still more so when it is close to the dispositive issue or to the centre
of the controversy. And there was some evidence (albeit not conclusive) contrary to the fact which
the judge “noticed”. So then taking notice is contrary to the rule that the fact to be noticed must be
notorious and beyond reasonable debate, or capable of immediate and accurate demonstration
from readily accessible sources of indisputable accuracy.
4. Exceptions
An admission against interest may be received against a party.⁵
Where hand calculations were impractical and everyone used computer software, its maker
was allowed to attend as a friend of the court and explain discrepancies in calculations.⁶
If there was a dispute about whether to receive or exclude evidence and an allegation that it
would be prejudicial, and the evidence was excluded, unsworn statements by both counsel to the
Court of Appeal on those topics, including what the evidence would have been, can be received
because of practicalities, even if the theory is questionable.⁷
[Footnote 12 continued from prior page]
(one JA) ; Law Society's Code of Professional Conduct, ¶ 5.2-1, Commentary 1. And it is likely to attract
costs payable personally: Re Rahall 2003 ABQB 1050, 349 AR 263. The Court of Appeal was critical of a
decision by a case management judge. The two lawyers had made many factual submissions to the court
not founded on any afﬁdavit or other admissible evidence: Chern v. Chern 2006 ABCA 16, 380 AR 267.
Misstatement of the previous history to the chambers judge led to reversal of the resulting order, in Edm.
Region Community Bd. for Persons with Developmental Disabilities v. Protegra 2006 ABCA 363. Final
family law orders are rarely made in chambers on conﬂicting afﬁdavits: Olson v. Olson 2014 ABCA 15, 37
RFL(7th) 414.

PART 6: RESOLVING ISSUES AND PRESERVING RIGHTS R.6.11(2)
On whether an adverse inference may be drawn from failure to ﬁle an afﬁdavit by a particular
witness, see the commentary on R.8.15.
There is an extended discussion of the “documents in possession” rule as a ground for making
records admissible in evidence.¹
Where a statute requires an afﬁdavit verifying an application (motion), the afﬁdavit is essential,
but there is no objection to swearing that afﬁdavit some months before the application is ﬁled
or deemed to be ﬁled. If the facts were known for a long time, swearing earlier is natural. And
technical formalities are to be avoided.²
Unless legislation says otherwise, a party bringing an application bears the onus of proving
that application, so a motion under R.4.33 requires proof of no signiﬁcant advance of the action
in the period before the applicant moved.³
5. Miscellaneous
There is no “equitable” power to ignore important evidence in an afﬁdavit, even if it operates
against the deponent, who is a party.⁴
Even in case management, and even if an afﬁdavit is ﬁled just before hearing, a judge should
not decide an issue without reading all the afﬁdavits ﬁled by the parties for the motion.⁵
Generally a party should not split its case. Filing two separate rounds of afﬁdavits to support
an application may be that. The anti-splitting rule is less strictly applied in chambers applications,
if no prejudice to the opponent resulted. But costs may be affected.⁶
But splitting may be a
consideration for trial, not for interlocutory motions.⁷
See also R.6.3n. C.6
A new afﬁdavit ﬁled and served later likely can cure technical or typographical ﬂaws in the ﬁrst
afﬁdavit.⁸
B. Bars to Evidence
1. Tardiness
The afﬁdavits supporting the application must be ﬁled and served with (or before) the written
application.⁹
Some counsel try to adduce opposing afﬁdavits at the last moment, but Rr.6.3(3)
and 6.6 prevent that.

1 Signalta Res. v. Cdn. Nat. Res.2022 ABQB 89, JCC 1001 17217 (Feb 1) (¶’s 20-27, 50-53). “Documents
in possession” are not hearsay, because they are not adduced to prove the truth of their contents, but rather
to show the possessing party’s knowledge of their contents, or his connection with, or complicity in, the
transactions to which they relate, or his state of mind with reference to that (id. at ¶’s 22, 24-25). When that
doctrine is advanced for documents possessed by a large organization, some person in authority must have
had knowledge of the record. Merely ﬁnding the document on the company’s premises is not enough (id. at
¶’s 25-26).

R.6.11(2) PART 6: RESOLVING ISSUES AND PRESERVING RIGHTS
It is important to comply with orders setting schedules or deadlines. Unilateral non-compliance
is improper, especially without notice to the other parties of it and the alleged reasons for it. A
time extension cannot be got later without reason for the delay. So big parts of a late afﬁdavit
containing information obtained within the deadline were struck out before the hearing.¹
However, excluding evidence because it is late, or should have been ﬁled at an earlier stage,
is a harsh remedy, and usually case management orders or costs are a better remedy.²
And one
must be sure, looking at the whole context, that the evidence now tendered had a full chance to be
known, needed, and adduced earlier, by that party.³
Nor should evidence be excluded in advance
without ﬁrst seeing it.⁴
Even if some parties applied ex parte, if later parties had applied on notice,
the rules about disclosing all one’s information do not apply to those later parties, who can adduce
later evidence, even on a similar topic.⁵
Failing to ﬁle an afﬁdavit which is in court and relied on and described (before moving for leave
to serve a written Application) is a curable irregularity,⁶
especially if the leave is only effective on
an act which occurs at the same time as the ﬁling.⁷
But the ﬂaw may not be cured if no explanation
is given.⁸
Reopening proceedings with “new evidence” ﬁrst requires that due diligence in getting it and
applying have been followed. Records publicly available predating the earlier proceedings do not
qualify. Ignoring this leads to too much relitigation of decided points. Another requirement is that
the “new evidence” be practically conclusive.⁹
2. Live Testimony
This Rule says that all evidence on applications will be documentary except by special leave,
or special Rule. An application to testify live for an application was refused. Afﬁdavits prevent
surprise.¹⁰
It is exceptional to allow live evidence on applications.¹¹
Oral evidence is occasionally
allowed.¹²
3. Laws of Evidence
An objection that an afﬁdavit contains inadmissible hearsay must be made when it is tendered
in court, not earlier (during cross-examination on it) or later (on appeal).¹³
Applications should

PART 6: RESOLVING ISSUES AND PRESERVING RIGHTS R.6.11(2)
not be fragmented, and whether evidence is admissible should be decided on return of the main
application, not as a preliminary motion.¹
That evidence is inadmissible as hearsay, is cured
or becomes academic, if the opposing party's afﬁdavit or cross-examination on it, supplies or
conﬁrms necessary evidence of the "missing" fact.²
A fact ﬁnding in another unrelated lawsuit between different parties has no binding effect in
this lawsuit.³
Fact ﬁndings have limited precedential effect?⁴
But sometimes relitigation factual
questions decided in a previous trial is abuse. See R.3.68 n. F.1(c).
Rule 6.11(1) (a) conﬁrms that expert evidence can be used on summary judgment applications.
The witness’s expertise can be established by the afﬁdavit.⁵
A “report” on social research is not
admissible evidence. And merely attaching it to an afﬁdavit, without swearing to belief, still less.
The document does not speak for itself, and no Rule says that it does. It is no better if it is material
from some other lawsuit.⁶
Where an expert report was merely attached to another person’s
afﬁdavit, but the expert was cross-examined and adopted his own report, there is no unfairness.⁷
Rule 6.11 sets no procedure for it. Trial procedure should be copied: qualiﬁcations and
scope of opinion should be precisely deﬁned and the substance of the opinion should be clearly
expressed. That should be set out in the expert’s afﬁdavit or in a written submission.⁸
But a two-
stage procedure is only necessary at trial, though it is a good practice on an application.⁹
Rule
8.16(1) does not expressly apply, but should be copied; its aims apply on motions too. That one
expert is necessary is not enough; one needs reasons why two or more are needed. That each
extra expert has distinct perspective is not enough.¹⁰
An afﬁdavit making, explaining, or supporting a claim of privilege, even on a later application
contesting the privilege, can give a conclusion or opinion without contravening common-law rules,
or Rules of Court, about opinion or expert evidence.¹¹
The court reviews the case law about whether “illegally-obtained” evidence (surreptitious
recordings) is admissible.¹²
If it is alleged that a mediation ended in an interim agreement, then the consensual outcome is
not privileged. This is not a request for content of negotiations or discussions during the mediation.¹³
The court thought that the implied undertaking applied to cross-examination on afﬁdavit, and
would not release it to use in cross-examining a different ofﬁcer of the same employer, in a later
suit after the previous suit was stayed and arbitrated. None of the tests was met.¹⁴

6-88
R.6.11(2) PART 6: RESOLVING ISSUES AND PRESERVING RIGHTS
4. Volume of Evidence
The Court of King's Bench has a Practice Note for family law special applications which
conﬁnes each side to three afﬁdavits (and then one in response), but a fourth was allowed where
it was the other side’s fault for raising a new issue.¹
5. Miscellaneous
Late evidence may be allowed by a chambers justice where the judge ﬁnds there is an
important pleaded issue which has not been dealt with in evidence or argument; otherwise much
time and money might be wasted on successive similar applications.²
Semble it would be very difﬁcult to have evidence in a civil suit excluded because it was
gathered by a process authorized by an ex parte order which was got improperly?³
That evidence
was obtained illegally or surreptitiously is not usually a ground to exclude it in a civil lawsuit.⁴
A court hearing a custody issue may refuse to hear evidence from the children if they are 14
or younger.⁵
A judge may strike out of the appeal record some afﬁdavits which had been ﬁled for a later
application for a stay and were not part of the appeal record in the court appealed from.⁶
C. Special Types of Evidence
See also the checklist in Part D below.
1. Afﬁdavits and Transcripts of Testimony
If evidence is not clear enough, the court some times may adjourn the application to let the
party applying ﬁle a better afﬁdavit.⁷
But a chambers judge who dismisses an application for
failure to comply with an express Rule about evidence should not give leave to reapply with better
material, if the applicant had been warned, and there never was any explanation for persisting.⁸
The applicant in family law special chambers ﬁrst ﬁled an incomplete and confusing afﬁdavit.
Then later she ﬁled a voluminous second afﬁdavit containing many new allegations which existing
procedures did not let the respondent reply to. Though the applicant had some success, she had
to pay costs.⁹
A judge hearing a motion can draw inferences from the evidence, e. g. likelihood of destruction
of evidence from past improper conduct.¹⁰

PART 6: RESOLVING ISSUES AND PRESERVING RIGHTS R.6.11(2)
On a motion, a party can put into evidence answers of its own afﬁant when cross-examined
by the opposition. This is not an examination for discovery, and all cross-examination answers
automatically are part of the record before the court, upon which any party may rely.¹
The counsel
cross-examining on an afﬁdavit (or examining a witness for a pending application), must promptly
ﬁle with the court the full transcript; putting in some excerpts is not enough, as all parties can rely
on the transcript.²
See further R.6.7n.
The applicant can rely on questioning (examination for discovery) read-ins, without further
afﬁdavit.³
But they can be used only against the party who was examined for discovery, not
against other persons.⁴
A non-afﬁant can be examined to get a transcript to use at an application, if Rr.6.16-6.20 are
observed.⁵
2. Court Decisions and Records
A case management judge can always look at the court records about this suit, or previous
orders made in the suit.⁶
See further R.4.14(3) n.
A relevant previous judgment is admissible as evidence of its ﬁndings or conclusions, in later
interlocutory proceedings; its weight depends upon a host of circumstances. But the person whom
it would adversely affect must be given a chance to lead evidence to rebut it or lessen its weight
(absent res judicata or the like).⁷
The Evidence Acts allow pleadings or other court documents from the court ﬁle, to show
steps taken, but not to prove their contents, even if they are forms provided for by legislation.⁸
To
prove that a litigation representative's appointment had been consented to, the afﬁdavit leading
to that appointment was produced. Its admission was objected to on the ground that it was ﬁled
in another parallel probate suit (in the same court). The judge accepted that evidence, without
requiring formalities such as reﬁling it in this action.⁹
3. Evidence from a Different Suit
A quick reading of R.6.11(f) might suggest that a transcript of evidence from one
lawsuit can be entered as evidence (of the truth of its contents) in a different lawsuit.
However, the common law did not allow it,¹⁰
and this Rule was interpreted not to change

6-90
R.6.11(2) PART 6: RESOLVING ISSUES AND PRESERVING RIGHTS
that.¹
More recent authority suggests a wider reading of the Rule permitting use of evidence from
a previous, likely unrelated lawsuit, but with serious qualiﬁcation.²
However, the use of evidence from another suit is very limited. It may prove what someone did,
such as consenting or opposing, and may prove what action the court took. But the relevant Rules
of court or statutes rarely say that the writing to be admitted is proof of the truth of what it says,
and it is not proof of that.³
However, the Supreme Court of Canada has put important limits on use
of evidence from another suit.⁴
The transcript of another court proceeding and uncertiﬁed copies
of court documents are likely hearsay.⁵
This Rule only gives the mechanism where such evidence
is admissible, i. e. , where the parties (or their privies) in the two suits are the same.⁶
There are
other practical limitations as well, and in all cases, leave of the court is required.⁷
Some of the reasons for not admitting evidence from another suit sound somewhat technical.
But the most important reason is that “evidence from another suit” is never the testimony capable
of cross-examination. It is always a transcript, an afﬁdavit, or a report, with no guarantee of getting
its author to come and be cross-examined. So in substance, it is hearsay.⁸
Usually an afﬁdavit
ﬁled in one suit cannot be used in another. There would have had to be a chance for the present
opponent to cross-examine on it there, and the issues would have to be substantially the same,
and the parties would have to be the same.⁹
If an afﬁdavit ﬁled and sworn for a different lawsuit is produced late for this lawsuit, surprising
everyone, that tardiness alone is enough ground for the judge to refuse to consider the afﬁdavit.¹⁰
Even on a security for costs application, the reasons of a previous judge for acquitting a
defendant of parallel criminal charges are inadmissible (as is the fact of acquittal).¹¹

PART 6: RESOLVING ISSUES AND PRESERVING RIGHTS R.6.11(2)
4. Miscellaneous
The Alberta Evidence Act, s.41.3 does not restrict how to prove emails, and metadata are not
the only way, and their loss does not mean that the emails are necessarily inadmissible.¹
A person
can testify that he sent a letter to another by posting it in a mailbox, and identify the letter or a ﬁle
copy of it. There is no reason that analogous evidence cannot prove sending an email.
A record disclosed in an afﬁdavit of records, but not supported by other evidence, can be used
to prove the existence or authenticity of that record, but almost certainly not to prove the truth of
its contents.²
The parties cannot depart from an agreed statement of facts, and an agreement that some
document is admitted as evidence “for the truth of its contents” has that effect?³
Arguments and submissions to the court are not evidence, unless they are admissions. And
an admission by counsel (not directly by a party) is only admissible in that lawsuit. After all, a
different suit with different issues and different instructions to counsel might not create any such
admission. “Facts” depend on the issues and evidence at hand, not on all possible evidence
anywhere in the world on irrelevant topics.⁴
In a bankruptcy, a trustee's report should be accepted at face value.⁵
A statute requiring ﬁling a trial transcript, within a deadline, to appeal from the Alberta Court
of Justice to the Court of King’s Bench (in a civil case) is mandatory, and cannot be excused or
extended.⁶
A Saskatchewan Rule on when one can get a transcript of court proceedings was interpreted
broadly.⁷
D. Possible Evidence for Applications
There are many possible types and sources of evidence for an application, as shown by the
following checklist.⁸
1. Read-ins from questioning (examination for discovery) transcripts of the opposing party:
Rr.5.30(2), 5.31 and 6.11(1) (c).
2. Live evidence (with leave of the judge) : R.6.11(1) (g) ; cf. R.3.14(g). This is rarely done.⁹
3. Transcript of the evidence of a witness previously examined for the motion: Rr.6.8 and
6.11(1) (b) (either side may use).¹⁰

who should have been served with notice of the application was not served, the
Court may
(a) dismiss the application,
(b) adjourn the hearing for notice to be served, or
(c) if the Court considers it appropriate to do so, hear and decide the
application.⁴

R.6.13 PART 6: RESOLVING ISSUES AND PRESERVING RIGHTS
Where legislation lets a “party affected” apply to set aside an order, that phrase quoted can
have a wide meaning, though the context is very important.¹
See further on some parties not
served, the notes to R.6.3 and 9.15.
Lack of notice is very serious, because it is a denial of natural justice.²
See further R.6.3 n. E.
Failure to give both Attornies-General notice of a constitutional law argument means that the court
should not hear that argument.³
A judge strongly criticized a spouse who had ongoing King's Bench litigation with counsel,
but kept trying to get an emergency protection order ex parte (without counsel) from a Justice
of the Peace. She ﬁnally succeeded, partly by lying, but more importantly by leaving out a mass
of highly relevant facts, some of which made her claims most doubtful. The Protection Against
Family Violence Act forbids making a frivolous or vexatious complaint under the Act with malicious
intent; that occurred here. The court created a remedy, and prohibited her from applying ex parte
for any emergency protection order or similar relief in the future. $4, 000 costs were assessed.⁴
Lack of notice bars relief on grounds not notiﬁed.⁵
This Rule does not help a respondent who got a written application with the right contents, but
the wrong Form number in the corner.⁶
Even if the parties agree to dispense with a written Notice of Application, and just show up
together in chambers, ﬁling a formal notice is preferable, as it gives a good deal of information (as
per R.6.3(2) ).⁷

6.13 Unless the Court otherwise orders, a hearing of an application in which only
one party makes a personal appearance must be recorded word for word by a
method that is capable of providing a written transcript.

PART 6: RESOLVING ISSUES AND PRESERVING RIGHTS R.6.14(1)
Subdivision 4
Appeal from Application Judge’s Judgment or Order
Appeal From Application Judge’s Judgment or Order
6.14(1) If an applications judge makes a judgment or order, the applicant or
respondent to the application may appeal the judgment or order to a judge.
(2) A notice of appeal in Form 28 must be ﬁled and served within 10 days after
the judgment or order is entered and served and returnable within a reasonable
time, not exceeding 2 months, after the date the notice of appeal is ﬁled.¹
(3) An appeal from an applications judge’s judgment or order is an appeal on
the record of proceedings before the applications judge and may also be based
on additional evidence that is, in the opinion of the judge hearing the appeal,
relevant and material.
(4) The record of proceedings is
(a) the application before the applications judge,
(b) afﬁdavits and other evidence ﬁled by the parties respecting the
application before the applications judge
(c) any transcript of proceedings before the applications judge, which
must be ordered and paid for by the appellant, unless the Court
determines, or the parties agree, that transcripts are not needed, and
(d) the applications judge’s judgment or order and any written reasons
given for the decision.
(5) The appellant must ﬁle and serve on the respondent to the appeal, within one
month after service of the notice of appeal,
(a) any transcript of proceedings described in subrule (4) (c),
(b) any additional evidence referred to in subrule (3), and
(c) any further written argument.
(6) The respondent to the appeal must ﬁle and serve on the appellant, within 20
days after service of the documents referred to in subrule (5),
(a) any further written argument the respondent wishes to make, and
(b) any additional evidence referred to in subrule (3).
(7) The appellant may, within 10 days after service of the documents referred
to in subrule (6), ﬁle a brief written argument responding to any unanticipated
additional evidence or further argument raised by the respondent.
(8) A party may rely on its original written argument, if any, that was before the
applications judge or any further argument ﬁled under subrule (5) (c) or (6) (a), or
both the original argument and the further argument.
[Alta. Reg.124/10;143/11;136/22]

Footnote
1 Quite similar to previous R.500(2). There used to be a two-step time limit, which was hard to meet. The 1999
amendment (Alta. Reg.172/99) replaced it with a much laxer one. Then the 2000 amendment (Alta. Reg.
68/00) put a deadline on ﬁling to enable certiﬁcates of no appeal. The 1983 amendment merely split one
subrule into two, and deleted references to local judges.1968 R.500 came from 1944 C. R.582, and was
similar to R.313(2), but changed the times and how to calculate them.

A. Appeals From an Applications Judge to a Justice
1. Cross-References
On the jurisdiction of Applications Judges, see R.6.9n.
Appeals from Applications Judges are authorized by s.12 of the Court of King's Bench Act.
2. Times
Where times are tight (as here), Court of King’s Bench procedures for booking applications
and ﬁling written material before them, can create grave dangers. Long notes on that are found
in R.6.2n. A.2. Whether the Court of King’s Bench’s actual procedures provide a method for an
appeal from an applications judge (Master) is now doubtful. See also R.6.6n. C.
It seems probable that the procedures for scheduling and ﬁling an appeal to a judge from
an Applications Judge have changed in a way not fully reﬂected on the Court of King's Bench
website.
Rule 6.14’s timelines cannot be met. The appellant must ﬁle, within one month of the notice
of appeal (which is due 10 days after service of the decision being appealed), the transcript
below, any new afﬁdavits, and “any further written argument”, i. e. a new brief. The respondent’s
additional evidence and brieﬁng is due 20 days after the applicant’s materials. These deadlines
are functionally impossible. When new afﬁdavits are introduced on appeal to a Justice, which is
common, they are usually cross-examined upon. Counsel cannot draft and submit a brief without
both sides’ evidence being ﬁled and examinations completed. Rule 6.14’s front-loading is also
not consistent with the long lag times for a hearing. Appeals of applications judges’ decisions to a
justice are, by virtue of an unpublished Court directive, booked for a full day Special. The wait time
for these is approximately one year. So the normal practice for these appeals is to ﬁle the Notice
of Appeal but vary the remaining deadlines by consent order of a Justice.
The time for appeal can be extended under R.13.5.¹
1 No intent to appeal during the ordinary time period, no explanation of the delay, nor any real prospect of
success: Loughlin v. Murdoch 2007 ABQB 76. Time will not be extended unless the appeal is shown to
have a reasonable chance of success: Cho v. Baccari (Phimsarath) 2003 ABQB 235, 332 AR 329. Even
lateness of the appeal should not bar a 2-day adjournment needed to get an afﬁdavit about the lateness:
Barker v. Sowa (#2) 2004 ABCA 93. An appeal from an Applications Judge should not be dismissed just
because it was ﬁled one day late in King's Bench, if there is no particular urgency in the law suit: Owners
Condo. Plan 9610377 v. W. E. Roth Constr.2005 ABCA 373.

6-97
PART 6: RESOLVING ISSUES AND PRESERVING RIGHTS R.6.14(8)
On a motion to extend time to appeal from an Applications Judge, instead of the usual 4
Cairns criteria in Alberta, 6 criteria from a Supreme Court of Canada decision¹
were given:
communicated genuine intent in time to appeal, moving diligently, proper explanation for delay,
extent of delay, prejudice to other parties, and merits of the proposed appeal.²
It is not clear
whether the four-part Cairns test should be applied to time for an appeal from an Applications
Judge to a Justice.³
Subrule (2) does not bar an adjournment on proper grounds.⁴
Under both the former Rules and the 2010 Rules, an appeal from an Applications Judge need
not also be served within the same time limit as is ﬁxed for ﬁling the appeal papers, and there is
no general need ever to serve the appeal on subsequent encumbrancers.⁵
Such an appeal requires ﬁling and service within 10 days of entry and service of the order
appealed from. That assumes that the winning party will do that. Where the applications judge
directed the losing respondent to prepare the order, that was done literally under the Rules but
outside their expectations. Service is service, and knowledge (because one prepared the order)
is not service; “self-service” does not exist.⁶
An appeal was dismissed because it was late, counting from email service of the order (to an
email address given) and its conﬁrmation, and as there was no application to extend time. New
evidence on the appeal was ﬁled too late (under the Rules and a court order about expert reports)
and too close to the hearing.⁷
Time to appeal runs from entry and service of the formal order, but if it is prepared by the
losing party before the Applications Judge (not the winner as is usual) there is no formal service
on the appellant or its counsel. When the losing party entered the order through their solicitor, he
knew that had happened and so in effect served himself and his client?⁸
The case law on error by counsel about the time limit, as a reason to extend time, is reviewed.⁹
Time was not extended, where there was no evidence of a genuine timely intent to appeal,
no sufﬁcient explanation for the delay of about a year,¹⁰
and serious prejudice to the respondent
plaintiff, who had levied execution under the judgment, got paid, and released another party on
the basis of the payment.¹¹
If the appeal has little chance of success (e. g. the evidence was one-
sided), then it is proper to refuse to extend time.¹²

Where there were no proper grounds permanently to bar entering an order, it was improper
to refuse to hear a (timely) appeal from it on the grounds that it had gone unentered for over a
year.¹
If the hearing de novo to a judge conﬁrms the order but the winner had originally not given
notice and not told the Applications Judge of the previous procedural history, he may lose costs.²
Two months’ delay in appealing were excused because the Covid epidemic reduced King's
Bench speed and accuracy in ﬁling, especially when exacerbated by a new electronic ﬁling
system, and trouble in entering the Applications Judge decision electronically.³
A chambers justice directed that an appeal from an Applications Judge proceed (to trial of
an issue) and not be dismissed for want of prosecution, and no appeal was taken from that.
Therefore, a later motion to dismiss the appeal for want of prosecution should be denied.⁴
On an appeal from an Applications Judge to a judge where the appellant does not ﬁle his brief
etc. in time, so that the Clerk of the Court strikes the appeal off the appeal or applications list,⁵
the
tests for later restoring that appeal to the list are like those used for dilatory appeals to the Court
of Appeal.⁶
3. Miscellaneous
A party dissatisﬁed with an Applications Judge's decision should appeal, not just apply afresh.⁷
Given R.6.3(2) and Form 28, a notice of appeal from an Applications Judge should give the
grounds of appeal.⁸
Lack of speciﬁcity in a notice of appeal from an Applications Judge, is not
merely a technical defect, and the other side cannot be expected to ﬁll in the blanks. One cannot
defend against unclear and non-speciﬁc allegations, and the court cannot control the proceedings
or identify the issues or ﬁnd the relevant facts. It is even worse if Charter remedies against a non-
government party are sought.⁹
If the appellant wishes relief which the Applications Judge denied,
then he or she would be wise to ask for that relief as well as for reversal of the Applications
Judge's order; especially if the motion is heard de novo, e. g. because there is new evidence
before the Judge.
On an appeal from an applications judge, it may be proper for a chambers justice to raise the
issue of mootness which the parties have not argued, and if the Applications Judge was appealed
long after his decision, the appellant should not have been surprised by the mootness issue.¹⁰
[Footnote 12 continued from prior page]
162. A delay of a few days in ﬁling an appeal from an Applications Judge may not be excused, where no
reasonable chance of success is shown. But cf. the dicta in Barker v. Sowa (#1) 2003 ABCA 159, 327 AR
292; and see Cho v. Baccari, supra. See the C. P. E. , Chapter 18, Part J. On appeal to the Court of Appeal,
time extensions in King's Bench are viewed deferentially: Stoddard v. Montague, infra. Conversely, 2 ½
years’ delay was not waived, despite excuses of lack of funds: Heffernan v. Korman 2006 ABCA 262.

6-99
PART 6: RESOLVING ISSUES AND PRESERVING RIGHTS R.6.14(8)
There is a discretion to stay an Applications Judge's order pending appeal from it.¹
When a Judge is asked to discharge an ex parte order made by an Applications Judge, that
is not an appeal from an Applications Judge, and new evidence can be heard.²
Failure to provide the Court of King's Bench Judge the Applications Judge's reasons is fatal.³
Even if the parties thought no transcript of the Applications Judge was necessary, the justice may
adjourn the appeal and get one (to see what test the Applications Judge used).⁴
A decision of an Applications Judge can be appealed to a King's Bench Justice, even if the
Applications Judge was acting under the Arbitration Act, and s.7(6) of that Act bars certain
appeals. The appeal to a justice is provided by the Queen's Bench Act, not just the Rules. No
Alberta case law bars such appeals.⁵
Whether a party has a cross-application during an appeal from an Applications Judge to a
Judge, is governed by the notice of appeal, not by the de novo nature of such an appeal.⁶
Given the test of correctness on appeal from an Applications Judge to a King’s Bench Justice,
if the appeal involves the same record and arguments, the justice may frame his or her reasons
with reference to the Applications Judge's reasons, where the Justice agrees with those reasons.⁷
On an appeal to a King’s Bench justice, a ﬁnal decision on costs can be reopened, and the
court is not functus officio.⁸
And a fortiori as to an earlier undertaking to pay such costs.⁹
4. Nature of Appeal
(a) Generally
In Alberta, an appeal from an Applications Judge to a justice was traditionally (and often still
is) in effect de novo.¹⁰
That means two main things.¹¹

6-100
R.6.14(8) PART 6: RESOLVING ISSUES AND PRESERVING RIGHTS
First, new evidence or cross-examination is possible,¹
and new grounds can be raised.²
Rule 6.14(3) (amended in 2011) changes that slightly, as it now says these appeals are “on the
record”, but it also allows, with leave, new evidence that is signiﬁcant enough that it is relevant
and material.³
Since most relevant evidence would meet that test,⁴
and since whether the new
evidence should be permitted is not decided as a preliminary matter, but with the appeal itself,⁵
the 2010 Rule is substantially the same as the previous one, certainly if any new evidence is
heard by the judge. Then the Applications Judge and the justice have heard different cases.⁶
Therefore if an Applications Judge summarily dismisses an action, an appeal from him or her is
de novo, and so for that purpose the action is considered still alive, and the Applications Judge’s
order is no bar to an examination under R.5.17 or 6.8.⁷
If the Applications Judge may wrongly
have weighed evidence, or decided among competing evidence, if any new evidence is given
on appeal to the justice, that problem is solved and the justice can decide that fact question.⁸
So
the 2011 amendment only has any real effect if no one adduces any new evidence on appeal, or
(rarely) if that new evidence is excluded as not material.
Any new evidence tendered on appeal must be admissible evidence, e. g. if adduced to get
summary judgment, it must be personal knowledge.⁹
And second, the justice exercises any discretion afresh and substitutes his or her views for the
Applications Judge’s.¹⁰
The test on appeal is whether the Applications Judge was correct.¹¹

is (in a sense) no standard of review.¹
It is not really an appeal.²
The Applications Judge can
discover and rule on a legal issue not noticed by the parties, and lack of chance for them to argue
it is cured by their chance to do so if it is appealed to a judge.³
On appeal from an Applications Judge to a justice, if the factual record and the submissions
are the same as they were below, the justice can describe his or her analysis and conclusions in
terms of what the Applications Judge said, if the justice ﬁnds no legal or factual errors there. Doing
that is not appellate deference, nor is it about standard of review.⁴

Indeed, the judge hearing the appeal can correct oversights in the Applications Judge's
judgment, even in favor of a party not appealing.⁵

6-102
R.6.14(8) PART 6: RESOLVING ISSUES AND PRESERVING RIGHTS
Though the tests for new evidence on appeal and standard of review are very lax, a party who
litigates by instalments, putting up a weak case before the Applications Judge, and then curing
the gaps on appeal, wastes time and money, and that is to be discouraged. If that party wins on
appeal, it may have its costs there much reduced for that poor conduct.¹
Two judge-made rules
seem to clash. First, that a summary judgment application to an Applications Judge requires each
party's best evidence. And second, that new evidence is admissible on appeal to a judge. The
solution is to adduce the further evidence on the appeal, but punish the dilatoriness of that new
evidence with costs.²
(c) Appeals from Other King's Bench Ofﬁcers
Presumably the same de novo approach would apply to appeals from analogous ofﬁcers,
such as Registrars in bankruptcy (who are the Applications Judges),³
and Clerks of the Court, or
the Registrar of the Court of Appeal. (But appeals from review ofﬁcers and assessment ofﬁcers
under Rr.10.26 and 10.44 make no allowance for fresh evidence. )
Appeals from referees or other court-appointed claims ofﬁcers or hearing ofﬁcers, and what is
the standard of review on appeal, are discussed in the Pacer case.⁴
B. Appeals From Other Courts to King's Bench
Statutory rules for appeals must be strictly applied. There is no general power to relax times
or procedural requirements which are in statutes, not Rules of Court, especially where one party
expressly raises and relies on the breach of the statute. So an appeal to the Court of King's Bench
was dismissed for failure to serve in time.⁵
Legislation on appeals from the Alberta Court of Justice said nothing requiring that a transcript
be ﬁled. Nothing incorporated the Alberta Court of Justice Act. So the appellant need not ﬁle a
transcript. The special legislation told the Alberta Court of Justice Clerk to forward to King's Bench
“the record of the evidence taken”. That includes any transcripts already existing and may include
electronic discs or tapes. King's Bench can decide whether it wants the latter transcribed.⁶
But
other legislation on other types of litigation imposes a time limit for ﬁling a transcript on appeal
from the Alberta Court of Justice to King's Bench. It cannot be extended except by order made
before it has expired. Some cases are overruled.⁷
Statute mandates 90 days to hear child welfare appeals from the Alberta Court of Justice to
King's Bench, and where the time had passed and the appellant had been doing nothing and had
no explanation, the appeal was struck for want of prosecution.⁸

(2) The notice of appointment for questioning must be served 5 days or more
before the appointment date
(a) on the person to be questioned, or if a lawyer acts for that person, on
the lawyer, and
(b) on each of the other parties.
(3) On application, the Court may resolve a dispute over the date, time, place
and person to be questioned and any related matters, and the records to be
produced at the appointment for questioning.
(4) The attendance of a person to be questioned and the records to be produced
at the appointment for questioning may be required by an order under rule 6.38
[Requiring attendance for questioning].
Information Note
If the person to be questioned on an affidavit in support of an application or originating
application is outside Alberta and a party to the application seeks to question that person
in Alberta, an application for a certificate to attach to a subpoena must be made under the
Interprovincial Subpoena Act.

Related Provisions
3.6 (venue) ; 5.14 (inspection of records) ; 6.38 (order to attend) ; 11.20 (service)

A Court of Appeal should be slow to upset a Chambers justice on a question of logistics,
especially a case management justice. Ordering cross-examination on afï¬�davits to be conducted
by teleconference is not denial of cross-examination, just a question of practicality.¹
Payment of Allowance
6.17(1) When a notice of an appointment for questioning is served, an allowance
must be paid by the questioning party to or on behalf of the person to be
questioned, unless the Court dispenses with an allowance.
(2) If an allowance is not paid, the person who is the subject of the notice of
appointment for questioning need not attend the appointment unless ordered to
do so by the Court.²
(3) The allowance to be paid is
(a) the amount determined under Schedule B [Court Fees and Witness
and Other Allowances], or
(b) if there is a dispute over the amount to be paid, the amount ordered
by the Court.³

1 See the C. P. E. , Chapter 45, Part K.4.

6-105
PART 6: RESOLVING ISSUES AND PRESERVING RIGHTS R.6.18(1)
Related Provisions
6.38 (order to attend) ; 13.33 (setting amount of allowance) ; Schedule B (fees for witnesses)

The money to be paid in advance under this Rule is customarily called “conduct money”. Of
course it is only interim, and at the end of the motion, the costs award for this disbursement can
be higher or lower.¹
Though the Court can reduce the amount of conduct money in a proper case, ordinarily it
covers all the travel costs from the deponent’s home, however far away, if he or she is a suitable or
necessary deponent, e. g. because he or she has the necessary personal knowledge.²
The party
moving for summary judgment usually pays the conduct money for cross-examination, whether or
not it provided the afﬁdavit or is the party cross-examining?³
Paying conduct money to bring somebody to questioning (examinations for discovery) is
standard practice, and not something which should surprise any party. If a party wants conduct
money dispensed with, he or she should show the court good reason to do so.⁴
Lawyer’s Responsibilities
6.18(1) If a lawyer is served with a notice of appointment for questioning and an
allowance is also paid, the lawyer must,
(a) as soon as practicable, inform the person to be questioned about the
appointment, and
(b) use the allowance only for the purpose for which it is paid.
(2) If a person to be questioned does not attend the appointment for questioning,
the allowance must, unless the parties otherwise agree or the Court otherwise
orders, be repaid to the person who paid it by
(a) the lawyer, or
(b) if the lawyer paid the allowance to another person, that other person.⁵

Related Provisions
2.25 (duties of lawyer of record) ; 6.38 (order to attend) ; 10.52(3) (a) (iv) (contempt)

R.6.19(1) PART 6: RESOLVING ISSUES AND PRESERVING RIGHTS
Interpreter
6.19(1) If a person to be questioned will not be able to understand the questions
or be able to answer the questions without the aid of an interpreter, the person to
be questioned must give reasonable notice of that fact to the party who served
the notice of appointment for questioning, and the questioning party must then
notify every other party that an interpreter will be present.
(2) The questioning party must provide an interpreter
(a) who is impartial and competent, and
(b) who takes an oath to interpret the questions and answers correctly
and honestly.
(3) The cost of the interpreter must initially be borne by the questioning party.

Related Provisions
13.23 (understanding affidavits)

The history of legislation on use of language in the courts of Saskatchewan (and Alberta) is
found in the Mercure case.¹
Alberta had a constitutional power to amend (and did amend) s.110
of Canada’s old N. W. T. Act, which had given a right to English and French statutes in Alberta.²
Any business relationship between one of the parties and an interpreter would be a conﬂict
of interest.³
Where relevant, cross-examination on credibility can include knowledge of a language, and
for that purpose the witness can be required to answer questions in one language, without an
interpreter.⁴
It is curious that this Rule is not stated to be conﬁned to persons who speak a different
language, or are deaf, or cannot speak at all.
Form of Questioning and Transcript
6.20(1) A person questioned on an afﬁdavit under this Part or a person questioned
as a witness for the purpose of obtaining a transcript under this Part for use at a
hearing may also be questioned by any other party, and the person questioned
may then be questioned again by the questioning party on that person’s answers
to the questions of other parties.
(2) Questioning and questioning again under this rule by parties adverse in
interest may take the form of cross-examination.

6-108
R.6.20(6) PART 6: RESOLVING ISSUES AND PRESERVING RIGHTS
A. Nature of the Proceeding
The cross-examination is usually conducted in a lawyer’s ofﬁce, without presence of any judge
or court ofﬁcial, only a court reporter.¹
It can be electronic or remote.²
Despite physical appearances, in law cross-examination before trial is much like cross-
examination at a trial, and very different from a questioning under Part 5 (examination for
discovery).³
Most important, R.5.31 does not apply, and in theory every answer is automatically
evidence before the court.⁴
So no one later needs to “read in” an answer on a motion, and in
argument any party can rely on any answer.⁵
The justice or Applications Judge can read the whole
cross-examination transcript and act on it.⁶
Rule 13.18 (hearsay) does not apply.⁷
See R.8.10n. on the proper scope of cross-examination at trial.
Therefore, cross-examining on an afﬁdavit is as dangerous as cross-examining at trial. Counsel
runs big risks by asking a question if counsel is not sure (or cannot control) what answer will
result. It is also dangerous to cross-examine if the afﬁdavit by that witness is weak or has gaps,
for the answers or the ensuing re-examination by the other side may (deliberately or accidentally)
plug the gaps. So cross-examination ordinarily should be brief to be safe. There may often be no
need to cross-examine if the afﬁdavit (and any other evidence) leave a total absence of evidence
on a key fact.
B. Procedure
A person cross-examined on afﬁdavit, a witness who is not a party, has the right to bring his
or her own counsel?⁸
Where the combination of an unrepresented litigant and a restraining order made supervision
by a judge of cross-examination on afﬁdavit necessary, the court acted properly in setting in
advance a one-half day limit on the cross-examination.⁹
In another case, the court refused to hold
cross-examination before a court ofﬁcial, but allowed it to be held at the Law Courts, with a judge
on immediate call.¹⁰
Despite subrule (5), an Applications Judge says that the practice is not to ﬁle the transcript
unless it is needed for the motion.¹¹

A party wrongly refusing to answer many proper questions on cross-examination may have to
pay heavy costs at once.¹
On undertakings, see Part D below.
It takes rare and very strong circumstances to exclude a party from attending someone else’s
cross-examination on afﬁdavit, even if that party is also going to be cross-examined.²
C. Topics for Questions
Reading this Rule shows that the word "question" is heavily overworked in these Rules, both
as a noun and a verb, and has many meanings: different types of procedures, and individual
queries within one such procedure. And none of it a usage either traditional, nor used commonly
outside Alberta. That is very confusing.³
Counsel cross-examining on an afﬁdavit (or examining for discovery) may ask questions on
topics relevant to that counsel’s defence, without ﬁrst ﬁling any evidence to substantiate that
defence.⁴
The possible scope of questions in cross-examination on an afﬁdavit is very wide.⁵
At trial,
cross-examination is not conﬁned to the topics asked of that witness in chief, and can cover
anything relevant at trial (or to credibility).⁶
Similarly, cross-examination on afﬁdavit is not
conﬁned to the contents of the afﬁdavit, and covers anything relevant to the pending motion (or to
credibility).⁷
If the merits of the suit are relevant to the application (e. g. , an application for summary
judgment, security for costs, or opening up default), then the scope of cross-examination is
wide.⁸
Conversely, one cannot cross-examine on topics irrelevant to the pending application, and
relevant only to other parts of the lawsuit, even by calling them “credibility”.⁹
One cannot conduct
a general cross-examination on matters outside the pending application and going instead to
the deponent’s (afﬁant’s) ofﬁce and general plans.¹⁰
Considering whether questions ﬁt within

R.6.20(6) PART 6: RESOLVING ISSUES AND PRESERVING RIGHTS
the foregoing categories, is a more apt test than asking whether they are a cloak for disclosure
(examination for discovery).¹
One can also cross-examine on anything in the afﬁdavit, even if it is irrelevant.²
It does not lie
in the afﬁant’s mouth, or his side’s counsel’s mouth, to say that.
Put into evidence (as an afﬁdavit exhibit) were merely the ﬁrst page and the last (signature)
page of an assignment agreement. The plaintiffs gave this only to show the fact of the agreement,
which assignment was not disputed. The purchase price of the assignment was not relevant. It
was asked for on cross-examination on afﬁdavit but denied.³
An afﬁant (deponent) can be cross-examined on information which he or she got in another
capacity.⁴
Some questions on cross-examination on afﬁdavit were improper because they were of the
“on what evidence do you rely” nature.⁵
Hypothetical questions are not improper in themselves, but cannot become speculative,
unrealistic or lack an air of reality.⁶
Nor go beyond the pleadings, nor ask something too broad or
vague, nor ask for comments on other persons’ actions or lack of action?
But examination cannot be carried on to an abusive or excessive extent,⁷
especially where
the burden of answering undertakings would be grossly disproportionate to the likely beneﬁt of
an answer. That is reinforced by Rr.1.2 and 1.4,⁸
and possibly Rr.5.3 and 5.19 by analogy under
R.1.7(2). There is no right to cross-examine on an afﬁdavit which is irrelevant to the disposition
of the application?⁹
An unsatisfactory attempt to oversimplify excessive cross-examinations down
to a ﬂat prohibition on repetition of a question (using or misciting authorities discussing different
topics) comes up occasionally.¹⁰

A non-party afﬁant usually need not undertake (when cross-examined) to produce more
information and documents.¹¹

PART 6: RESOLVING ISSUES AND PRESERVING RIGHTS R.6.21(1)
information beyond the ambit of the afﬁdavit.¹
The precise rules for having to give such undertaking
are discussed in R.6.7 n. C.3.
Early in cross-examination on afﬁdavit the witness refused to produce certain documents and
the questioning lawyer ended the examination unﬁnished. But that was improper; the counsel
should have asked any other possible questions, as the witness had come a long way, and a
second session wasted costs.²
Undertakings given on cross-examination on afﬁdavit are given to the court. As any party
could use the transcript had the deponent prepared himself and answered at once, therefore
any party can enforce the undertakings without need for any such prior agreement at the cross-
examination.³
There were inconsistent undertakings, on cross-examination answers, and written responses
to undertakings, with two years between one set and the other set. These “revisions” were minor,
the time taken normal, and every witness has an ongoing duty to correct errors in his or her
evidence (R.5.27). The errors were corrected quickly after discovery. Everyone makes some
errors; the witness had been trying to look at documents on several electronic devices. There was
no ground for solicitor-client costs. However, the errors and their last-minute correction put the
opponent to unnecessary legal costs and a second questioning on undertakings. So the party-
and-party costs of one questioning were payable forthwith and in any event.⁴
DIVISION 2
PRESERVING EVIDENCE AND OBTAINING EVIDENCE
OUTSIDE ALBERTA
Preserving Evidence for Future Use
6.21(1) The Court may order that a person be questioned, under oath,
(a) for the purpose of preserving evidence, or
(b) for any other purpose satisfactory to the Court.
[Footnote 11 from prior page]

Footnote
11 CRC-Evans Pipeline Int. , supra; Borgden (Rozak Est. ) v. Demas 2011 ABQB 239, 509 AR 337 (¶’s 33-44)
(reviewing authorities). He may have to if he previously reviewed that information, or if getting the information
would be very important and easy: Borgden v. Demas. Also see dicta in Cdn. W. Bank v. R. (M) 2003 ABQB
254, 46 CCLI(3d) 274. The court should be slow to order a witness being cross-examined to make further
inquiries and get further information. That assumes that (i) he has not referred to that information in his
afﬁdavit, (ii) it is not the only possible source for the statements he made in his afﬁdavit, and (iii) the question
is not extremely important in a situation where it would be easy for him to get the information: Dow Chem.

R.6.21(2) PART 6: RESOLVING ISSUES AND PRESERVING RIGHTS
(2) An order may be made under subrule (1) (a)
(a) if the person to be questioned is or might be unable to give evidence
before the Court because of accident, ill health or disability, or if there
is the likelihood that the person might die before being required to

(b) if the person to be questioned is within the Court’s jurisdiction when

Information Note
This rule and the following rule provide for obtaining what was formerly described as
commission evidence in Alberta (rule 6.21) and outside Alberta (rule 6.22)

Related Provisions
6.22 (obtaining evidence outside Alberta) ; 13.31 (video recordings of evidence)

Rules 6.21 and 6.22 are traditionally used to record evidence of witnesses who may not be able
to come to the trial because they are abroad, or are ill. However, nothing in the Rules expressly
conﬁnes this “commission” procedure to those two situations. For example, it could be used to
let an expert testify at the site of a hydro-electric dam, or it could be used to have some neutral
person referee a hostile and fractious questioning under Part 5 (questioning, i. e. , examination
for discovery), cross-examination on afﬁdavit, or R.6.8 (examination for a pending application).¹
The afﬁdavits given by the two sides (for summary judgment) clashed on the facts. The
defendants said that 3 of the witnesses were not connected to Canada in any way nor did they
ever come to Canada. An order was made to question two of them to preserve their evidence for
trial.²
It is fairly easy to get a commission to take evidence of a witness who is now too ill to come to
trial, or who may be too ill by the time of trial, e. g. , an elderly person now in good health.³
This is
called evidence de bene esse, because if the witness is well enough at the time of trial, he or she
may come and testify live instead of using the transcript.⁴
Obtaining Evidence Outside Alberta
6.22(1) On application, the Court may order the evidence of a person to be taken
outside Alberta for the purpose of one or more of the following:
(a) questioning under rule 5.17 [People who can be questioned];

PART 6: RESOLVING ISSUES AND PRESERVING RIGHTS R.6.22(2)

(c) an originating application;
(d) trial;
(e) any other purpose that the Court considers appropriate.
(2) In making its decision on the application, the Court must consider
(a) the convenience of the person to be questioned,
(b) whether the person is or might be unable to give evidence before the
Court because of accident, ill health or disability, or if there is the

when the person is required to give evidence,
(d) regarding the evidence to be given, the expense and inconvenience
of bringing the person to give evidence,
(e) whether the witness should give evidence in person, and
(f) any other sufﬁcient reason for granting or refusing the application.
(3) The Court may determine
(a) the date, time and place of the questioning,
(b) the minimum notice to be given to the person to be questioned of the
date, time and place of the questioning,
(c) the person before whom the questioning is to be conducted,
(d) the amount of the allowance to be paid to the person to be questioned,
and
(e) any other matter that needs to be resolved about the questioning.
(4) An order under this rule must be in Form 31, ﬁled and served, and may
(a) authorize the taking of evidence before a named person,
(b) give instructions to the person named to take evidence and to have
a transcript of the evidence prepared,
(c) order the production of records applicable to the questioning, and
(d) authorize a letter of request in Form 30 to be sent to the judicial
authority of the jurisdiction in which the person to be questioned is
located, requesting the necessary order or document to be issued
to require the person to be questioned to attend before the person
authorized to take evidence and, if necessary, to produce records.

Related Provisions
5.17 (examinations for discovery) ; 6.21 (preserving evidence) ; 13.31 (video recordings of

evidence)
Generally on obtaining evidence abroad, see Halsbury.¹

Footnote
1 Halsbury’s Laws of Canada Conﬂict of Laws vol. pp.423 ff. (2d reissue 2011).

6-114
R.6.22(4) PART 6: RESOLVING ISSUES AND PRESERVING RIGHTS
Commissions to take evidence of witnesses abroad are no longer that common, as travel from
distant lands now takes only a few days, not months. In Canada, the Interprovincial Subpoena
Act¹
and its equivalents make it easier to compel live attendance here of a Canadian witness.²
Occasionally the trial judge will be named as the commissioner to preside over the questioning if
a number of witnesses reside near each other abroad. Counsel should check local law with the
appropriate Canadian embassy before arranging a foreign commission: some countries view this
as an extremely sensitive issue, and a great infringement of their sovereignty.
Where evidence is to be taken on commission, then ordinarily a party cannot cross-examine
the witness whom he has called, unless he is able ﬁrst to establish that he or she is a hostile
witness. Alternatively, instead of commission evidence it might be possible to take the evidence
live by video conference during the trial.³
There are no ﬁrm rules on when to take a foreign witness’s evidence “on commission”, though
there is a heavy onus on the party seeking it.⁴
But one need not show that all other avenues have
been exhausted.⁵
However, there are some traditional criteria. Is the applicant in good faith? Does he or she
need the evidence, and will it be material?⁶
Is the witness unable to come here, or unwilling to
come and cannot be compelled to do so?⁷
Who seeks the commission: the defendant, or the
plaintiff who selected this forum to litigate?⁸
Is it more economical to take commission evidence?⁹
Will the evidence be controversial, so that credibility and live appearance are desirable?¹⁰
More
recent criteria also exist. The evidence sought must be material and not mere corroboration.
Particulars of the proposed evidence and what it will be are needed. But it can be hearsay and
the applicant need not exhaust all other avenues. Nor need the court grant the order even if those
tests are all met.¹¹
Hearsay may be used under R.13.18 to get the commission.¹²
Where the plaintiff’s psychologist in British Columbia refused to produce his ﬁle to either party,
or to come to Alberta, alleging that that would harm the plaintiff, he was ordered examined in
British Columbia, on his refusal, under R.6.22.¹³
The Judicature Act s.12 lets an Alberta court take judicial notice of the law of any Canadian
province or territory, without expert evidence.¹⁴

PART 6: RESOLVING ISSUES AND PRESERVING RIGHTS R.6.23(1)
Duties of Person Authorized to Take Evidence
6.23(1) A person authorized to take evidence under rule 6.22 [Obtaining evidence
outside Alberta] must, to the extent that it is possible to do so, conduct the
questioning in accordance with these rules, the law of evidence of Alberta, and
the terms of the authorization unless
(a) another form of questioning is required by the Court, or
(b) the law of the place where the questioning is conducted otherwise
requires.
(2) On or attached to the transcript, the person preparing the transcript must
(a) state the person’s name,
(b) specify the date and place where the transcript was prepared, and
(c) certify the transcript as complete and accurate.¹
(3) As soon as the transcript of the questioning is prepared and certiﬁed as
complete and accurate, the person authorized to take evidence must
(a) return the authorization, together with the original transcript and
exhibits, to the court clerk of the judicial centre in which the action
is located,
(b) keep a copy of the transcript and, where practicable, the exhibits,
and
(c) notify the parties who appeared at the questioning that the transcript
is complete and has been sent to the court clerk.²
This person used to be called the commissioner, and since the new Rules give no title,
probably still will be.

Related Provisions

6.24 If a judicial authority in another jurisdiction requests or authorizes a request
to be made to the Court for the Court’s assistance in obtaining evidence from
a person in Alberta for use in a court or other proceeding outside Alberta, the
Court may make any order that it considers appropriate, including any one or
more of the following:
(a) requiring a person to attend for questioning, under oath, which may
take the form of cross-examination;
(b) requiring a person to produce records;
(c) requiring a transcript of the questioning to be prepared;
(d) specifying the manner in which questioning is to be conducted.

R.6.24 PART 6: RESOLVING ISSUES AND PRESERVING RIGHTS

American and Canadian law differ greatly on how to handle the conﬂict between admissibility
of evidence and its use to prosecute the person testifying, thus raising a problem with U. S. letters
of request.¹
If a foreign court gives an order to take evidence on commission here for a foreign suit, and a
witness here will not cooperate, then the foreign court may issue letters of request. Then our court
may force the witness here to come before a commissioner here and testify.²
Agreed de bene evidence of a sick witness was ordered, and a date was set in advance (1/2
day) to decide any objections to questions. Disputed objections were to be listed and ﬁled in court
beforehand. Whether to let the witness testify at trial if he lived long enough, would be decided
closer to trial.³
The court enforced an American letter of request to gather evidence in Alberta. If the evidence
sought is generally relevant, it should not be parsed nicely to ensure that every bit will be ultimately
useful. The foreign court can best judge that, and judge prejudicial testimony. Precise topics were
set out here. Objections by the Alberta witnesses were to be judged by the Court of King's Bench,
and the length of examination was limited, or such objections could be raised with the Court of
King's Bench, and that was fair.⁴
Material was obtained from a U. S. military court on condition that it could be ﬁled in a Canadian
court only if sealed, and the Supreme Court of Canada agreed with the sealing.⁵
An American letter of request for very broad examination for discovery was enforced only
enough to allow questions to authenticate certain records already produced.⁶
It is relevant that the questioning may contemplate use of the answers for purposes other than
trial, but that is not conclusive.⁷

PART 6: RESOLVING ISSUES AND PRESERVING RIGHTS R.6.25(1)
DIVISION 3
PRESERVING AND PROTECTING PROPERTY OR ITS VALUE
AND INSPECTION OF PROPERTY
Preserving or Protecting Property or its Value
6.25(1) On application, the Court may make one or more of the following orders:
(a) an order for the preservation or custody of property that is in dispute
or that may be evidence in an action;
(b) an order that the amount in dispute or other amount be paid into
Court or that security be given to the Court or to a person named by
the Court, in a form and manner satisfactory to the Court, including
an amount for interest, costs and other expenses;
(c) an order for the sale of property and payment of the proceeds into
Court if the property is perishable, likely to deteriorate or likely to
lose its value, or for any other reason should be sold;
(d) if property is sought to be retained or attached under a lien or
otherwise as security for money, an order
(i) that the person otherwise entitled to possession of the
property be given possession,
(ii) that possession of the property be given to a party pending
the outcome of the action on payment of an amount into
Court or on security being given to the Court, or
(iii) that possession be given to a person named by the Court in
a form and manner satisfactory to the Court;
(e) an order to enter land or premises for the purpose of carrying out an
order under this rule.
(2) If the right of a party to a speciﬁc fund is in question, the Court may order
that the fund be paid into Court or that security be given for it to the Court or to a
person named by the Court in a form and manner and in an amount satisfactory
to the Court.¹

See also the Civil Enforcement Act.

6-118
R.6.25(2) PART 6: RESOLVING ISSUES AND PRESERVING RIGHTS
A. Preservation Generally
1. Introduction
There are textbooks about physical property.¹
Rules 6.25 to 6.27 really cover 4 very different situations:²
a. the lawsuit is about who gets possession or title to property, and in the meantime both
parties want it, or neither does, or it is in some danger from either of the parties or from
some other factor;
b. some evidence is in danger of disappearance or harm;
c. a party wants to inspect, test, or experiment with, some property (real or personal) for
purposes of the suit, e. g. the site of the accident, but the person (party or not) possessing
it will not consent; or
d. someone holds property claiming a lien on it, but the right to the lien or its amount is
disputed.
In any of these cases, the court can give any order necessary to safeguard the property or
evidence. In many cases, it can also replace the property with money, if that is safer and more
economical. It would not of course do that if the property were unique, or were important as
evidence.³
Rule 6.25(a) does not apply unless there is speciﬁc property in existence and the lawsuit
disputes who owns it?⁴
Presumably that assumes that cases b-d above do not apply.
Statute creates a separate ﬁfth situation where probably no right to property and no safety of
evidence is in issue. The Civil Enforcement Act gives a number of possible prejudgment remedies
to preserve property.⁵
A damage or debt claim is not proprietary.
An order to preserve a wasting or endangered asset, e. g. to sell it and put the proceeds in
court, is not a Mareva or attachment order, and different grounds are used to give or test such a
preservation order.⁶
Where the property to be preserved is the very subject of the suit, the higher
standard of proof for a Mareva injunction need not be met.⁷
Urgent orders for relief to prevent
dissipation of funds may not be the same in all cases. It may depend on whether the court gives
the order under the oppression sections of the Business Corporations Act, s.118, or under the

2. Mareva Injunctions and Preservation Orders
(a) Introduction
A very special kind of injunction restraining the defendant from disposing of his or her assets
before trial is called a Mareva injunction.⁴
It is designed to ensure that the defendant does not
hide or strip itself of exigible assets before judgment. In Alberta, Mareva injunctions are probably
now subsumed by the new broader attachment order under Alberta’s Civil Enforcement Act?⁵
It is
a poor practice to combine an attachment order under the Civil Enforcement Act, and a Mareva
injunction, in the same order, as it becomes then difﬁcult to see whether the governing principles
of the Act or law or equity, apply.⁶
Conﬂating the two is dangerous or wrong.⁷

R.6.25(2) PART 6: RESOLVING ISSUES AND PRESERVING RIGHTS
Applications Judges can give such orders.¹
(b) Tests
An important rule of law is that ordinarily the plaintiff gets execution only after ﬁnal judgment,
and gets no security over the defendant’s assets before then, even in a matrimonial property
suit, and cannot even ﬁle caveats.²
It takes special circumstances to give such security before
ﬁnal judgment, such as dissipating assets, moving them offshore, or defying court process. Mere
potential to consume or transfer assets is not enough.³
The Matrimonial Property Act and its
successor, the Family Property Act, do not change that. They allow pre-judgment remedies and
security only in narrow limited circumstances.⁴
When a Mareva injunction or similar remedy is sought, it must be supported by the applicant’s
proper evidence, and hearsay evidence should not be used unless ﬁrst-hand evidence is not
available. If the applicant’s staff or ofﬁcers were present, they should swear afﬁdavits.⁵
The
property owner has no onus of showing prejudice from such an order.⁶
Even more exceptional is
pre-judgment security against property of a non-party to the suit.⁷
Often an undertaking as to damages is needed.⁸
On a non-proprietary claim, the test for a Mareva injunction is tough.⁹
On the merits, most
cases say that a strong prima facie case is needed.¹⁰
For each of the tests or criteria, strong
evidence is needed, and any inference must be compelling.¹¹
Evidence of removal of and hiding assets¹²
outside the usual course of business is necessary,¹³
or at least evidence of dishonesty from which that can be inferred.¹⁴
Regularly paying proﬁts to a

PART 6: RESOLVING ISSUES AND PRESERVING RIGHTS R.6.25(2)
parent company is not enough.¹
Evidence of dissipation of assets may sufﬁce, though the tests at
common-law and under the Act may differ a little.²
But reviving a company struck off which has no
business except collecting the money in question, strongly suggests that the shell company will
disburse the funds at once.³
If a plaintiff seeking an attachment order before trial gives some evidence of the facts for its
cause of action, and is not cross-examined (or only brieﬂy and not shaken), and not faced with
any contrary evidence, it must have the arguable cause of action (e. g. strong prima facie case).
The chambers judge cannot assume that there will be contrary evidence at trial, still less strong
evidence, and must take the evidence of the plaintiff at face value.⁴
If an order against a number of individuals is sought, the evidence must be strong against
each of them.⁵
Giving such an attachment order is discretionary.⁶
It is not clear whether a pre-trial application for an order to preserve property must meet
the 3-part test for an interlocutory injunction.⁷
There is a dictum that apparently one can still be
given under common law without complying with s.17.⁸
But the tests are statutory, this is not an
injunction, and the common-law tests do not apply.⁹
The application is often made without notice, and then the applicant must observe utmost good
faith, and give full, fair, and candid disclosure, including adverse facts and potential defences.¹⁰

5 Tiger Calciuim v. Sazwan, supra (¶’s 117-20).
6 Lion’s Gate Homes v. Bahramloian 2007 ABQB 137; Henenghaixin Corp. v. Deng 2022 ABCA 271, Calg
2101 0082 AC (Aug 22). So satisfying all the statutory requirements does not compel issue of the order:
Michael J. Bondar Pro. Corp. v. Janstar Homes, supra.

order was upheld. In a ﬁght over proceeds of land, the Court would not order them paid into court, as that
would require an injunction, and money damages would sufﬁce, and there would be no irreparable harm. The
usual tripartite test: Monco Hldg. v. B. A. T. Dev. (#1) 2005 ABQB 696. Dicta that a strong prima facie case
is not needed: Ridge Dev. Corp. v. Crestwood Condos, supra. The tests for an interlocutory injunction
