6-122
R.6.25(2) PART 6: RESOLVING ISSUES AND PRESERVING RIGHTS
(c) Duration
An order like this should not be refused (if the evidence is all one way) on the theory that there
might be more evidence later.¹
Such an attachment order should not be left in place for a long time; the plaintiff getting the
order should move diligently to get to trial.²
A freezing injunction may later be set aside if little is
done in the meantime to prepare for trial,³
especially if there is no undertaking as to damages.⁴
Indeed if it is got ex parte (as is usually the case) the order should have an early expiry date;
leave to move to vary is not enough, and the onus is on the plaintiff seeking a renewal.⁵
A motion to set aside a Mareva injunction, especially after 6 years, will succeed if there is no
evidence (then or now) of dissipating or removing assets or irreparable harm, nor of balance of
convenience.⁶
If there were originally grounds for the injunction, but they have ceased to exist,
then the order should be set aside.⁷
(d) Application without Notice
On the scope of cross-examination on an afﬁdavit to set aside a Mareva injunction see the
MacDonald case.⁸
Where there is no basis for moving ex parte, and the respondent attends and seeks an
adjournment, he is entitled to his adjournment, and should not be subject to conditions such as
interim relief, especially absent any evidence of improper conduct by the respondent or danger
that assets would disappear.⁹
An ex parte attachment order freezing some assets of the defendant was got without proper
full disclosure; indeed the person obtaining it misled the court about the owner of the monies and
the purpose of various transfers. The judge cut back the scope of the order or replaced it with a
new one for a smaller sum.¹⁰
Such nondisclosure was a big factor in quashing such an order on
appeal.¹¹
A motion to review or to extend an attachment order got ex parte is treated as a de novo
application, with the onus on the plaintiff who got the order.¹²
Any proprietary claim by a non-party
should then be considered.¹³
An attachment order given without notice against a non-party was especially criticized.¹⁴

6-123
PART 6: RESOLVING ISSUES AND PRESERVING RIGHTS R.6.25(2)
(e) Scope, Terms and Conditions
Though a plaintiff established a prima facie case of duties and real risk of dissipation of funds,
to require offshore funds in a country with no legislation on enforcement of foreign judgments to
be repatriated to Canada would be extraordinary. Requiring that it be put in trust with a lawyer in
the jurisdiction where it was (Austria) was sufﬁcient.¹
The Civil Enforcement Act gives some usual conditions or protections to the person whose
assets are attached, and those should ordinarily be reﬂected in a Mareva injunction.²
No prejudgment remedy should tie up more assets than necessary, as s.17 of the Civil
Enforcement Act codiﬁes.³
Some funds can be released if the sum frozen is bigger than necessary,
and inconvenience to the defendant was not minimized.⁴
Sometimes the court will later release some of the frozen funds, e. g. for living expenses; the
tests are described. The present set of assets is more important than future assets. Whether
there is a valid prima facie proprietary claim is very important, and a phony one should not be
encouraged; a mere assertion is not enough.⁵
Some funds should not be frozen at the outset (for
living and legal expenses).⁶
The legislation on prejudgment attachment orders deliberately does not give legislative
guidelines for discovery to aid such an order, and it is not simply an examination in aid of execution,
as there is no judgment debtor.⁷
An undertaking as to damages should be part of an application for an injunction or an
accompanying afﬁdavit, not left until the day of the hearing of the application, as the plaintiff’s
solvency or the authority of the person signing the undertaking, could be an issue or require
cross-examination.⁸
A judge ordered that an existing undertaking as to damages given previously to secure an
attachment order, be fortiﬁed by paying money into court, but not for the full amount of the possible
costs, as that would insure one party against risk but not the other.⁹
After a limited freezing order on one bank account, it was learned that as soon as the
respondent learned such an order was sought, it had taken all the funds out of that bank account,
so the judge immediately ordered disclosure of all bank accounts, both present and recent, and
attached all of them.¹⁰

R.6.25(2) PART 6: RESOLVING ISSUES AND PRESERVING RIGHTS
In addition to security for costs, a judge also ordered security for the entire amount of the
judgment debt pending appeal. A litigant cannot use the court process to his advantage while
effectively disavowing in advance any judgment against him. Ordering security for the debt is not
to enhance the rights of the plaintiff, but to show that the court is not impotent “in the face of those
who choose to order their affairs so as to keep all their options for themselves. ” This is a bit like a
Mareva injunction, but it is after judgment.¹
(f) Miscellaneous
If a freezing order is terminated and then funds held are ordered paid out, but the termination
is upset on appeal, the order to pay out must be quashed and reheard in the Court of King's
Bench.²
Even if an interlocutory application for a Mareva injunction was withdrawn, that does not prove
that the part of the pleadings seeking a Mareva injunction was abandoned, or that it should be
summarily dismissed before trial; what the defendant did with his assets pending trial could create
additional grounds for such an order.³
On appeal from a freezing order, the standard of review is deferential.⁴
3. Preserving Speciﬁc Assets
An order to preserve speciﬁc property pending appeal requires some ownership on the part of
the plaintiff, not merely management fees, if the Crown (not the plaintiff) owns the chattels.⁵
One
should distinguish proprietary injunctions from Mareva and similar ones.⁶
On interim distribution of funds, see the Alexander case.⁷
Someone challenging a demolition order against his property used an originating application.
If that was not the correct procedure, then the court could simply request that the application be
continued as a statement of claim asking for an injunction.⁸
Money was admittedly paid in error, but there was a cross-claim, so a judge ordered the
money paid into court, pending trial.⁹
A receiver appointed in the wake of an alleged fraudulent Ponzi scheme has standing to apply
for an attachment order against those alleged to have received the missing money.¹⁰
On an interim distribution of matrimonial property, and a preservation order under R.6.25, see
the Holmes case.¹¹

6-125
PART 6: RESOLVING ISSUES AND PRESERVING RIGHTS R.6.25(2)
B. Anton Piller Orders
1. Introduction
In the last few generations, English courts expanded remedies of this sort into a new remedy,
the Anton Piller order.¹
An Anton Piller order is a sort of civil search warrant, usually got ex
parte.²
It authorizes the applicant’s solicitor, with assistants, to enter into the premises of the
respondent and there seize, carry off, and secure, evidence. Often the person empowered to
search will be an independent solicitor. After all, the urgency and lack of notice are to prevent
hiding or destroying evidence, not to speed up the plaintiff’s access. See subpart 3 below.
2. Tests
If the applicant shows³
a strong prima facie case,⁴
potential for serious harm to the applicant,
and very good evidence that the defendants would destroy evidence which they now possess,
the order may issue.⁵
The evidence for each branch of the test should be strong.⁶
Danger of
destroying evidence is hard to prove directly, but it can be inferred where the evidence has been
acquired in suspicious circumstances.⁷
3. Scope, Terms and Conditions
An Anton Piller order should not be any broader than necessary, and needs reasonable
limits as to time, people, and geography.⁸
There should be precise names and places, not generic
references, nor reference to “anyone having notice or served”. It should not cover all ﬁnancial
information, nor anything which relates to or may related to, the plaintiff, nor bind persons only
peripherally involved.⁹

Footnote
1 There is a considerable discussion of Anton Piller orders: Catalyst P’ners. v. Meridian Pkg.2007 ABCA
201, 417 AR 7. In England, a Practice Direction set the form for an Anton Piller order: see the Supreme Court
Practice (White Book) 2005 v.2 ¶ A1.17.002 and v.1 ¶’s 25.1.27 to 25.1.27.7. Note on the subject of Anton
Piller orders generally, C. P. E. , Chapter 29, Part C.11; Abrams and McGuinness, Canadian Civil Procedure
Law, 924 ff. (2d ed.2010) ; Drapeau, Anton Piller Orders and the Federal Court of Canada (Intellectual
Property Institute 2008); Crerar, Mareva and Anton Piller Preservation Orders in Canada: A Practical Guide
(Irwin 2017).
2 See R.6.4n. The tests are reviewed, in Ritter v. Hoag (#5) 2003 ABQB 611, 341 AR 376; Yaghi v. WMS
Gaming 2003 ABQB 680, [2004] 2 WWR 657. There is a heavy duty to disclose all relevant facts to the court:
Ritter v. Hoag (#5).
3 On use of previous court judgments as evidence of their ﬁndings or conclusions, to get an Anton Piller order,
see A. -G. B. C. v. Malik, infra.

5 See Sulphur Experts v. O’Connell, infra. ; Sasol Technology v. de Klerk, infra, and A. -G. B. C. v. Malik

proved through compelling inferences, e. g. by proving dishonesty: see A. -G. B. C. v. Malik, supra, and

6-126
R.6.25(2) PART 6: RESOLVING ISSUES AND PRESERVING RIGHTS
The applicant must give an undertaking as to damages. That undertaking must be at least as
broad as that for an interlocutory injunction.¹
Various other conditions are usually imposed, such
as times of day, who may attend, right to counsel, and so forth.²
But the Order should not allow
occupying waiting rooms, barring entry, police assistance, seizure of computers, unlocking safes,
giving up passwords, nor bar phoning out; and it should compensate non-parties for their time and
inconvenience.³
Nor should it allow searches or imaging of phones and personal computers of a
large number of people.⁴
An Anton Piller order should limit the use which the applicant plaintiff or its solicitors can make
of the documents seized. It was wrong to allow them to be used to pursue assets of the defendants
in unrelated matters. That is contrary to the usual "implied undertaking" in all discovery.⁵
Anything
broader than that should be called to the judge's attention and justiﬁed by special evidence and
argument.⁶
The Order should provide for custody and use of the matters seized, and the plaintiffs or
their solicitors should not automatically get or see them without further order or consent. And
commercial secrets should be safeguarded also, as should privileged materials.⁷
Generally
speaking, an Anton Piller order should not be made for search of the ofﬁces of a lawyer or an
accountant, even if those are also the defendant’s registered ofﬁce. It would take very special
evidence to show a real danger that a lawyer or an accountant would destroy documents, with or
without his client’s request.⁸
Execution of an Anton Piller order is not bad just because it was executed shortly before
5: 00 p. m. , and the person being searched could not immediately contact his or her own counsel.
Nor does use of an incompetent technician to search contents of a phone, if that was promptly
discovered and he was replaced by a competent technician.⁹
Such orders are often given against persons alleged to be knowingly distributing counterfeits
of copyright or trademarked goods, or against embezzlers,¹⁰
or persons stealing conﬁdential
information from employers.¹¹
They are to preserve evidence, not to prevent its dissemination.¹²

6-127
PART 6: RESOLVING ISSUES AND PRESERVING RIGHTS R.6.25(2)
An Anton Piller order has both evidence-preservation parts and injunction elements. Correct
granting of the order need not always mean it has to be preserved until trial. It should only continue
if the party is shown still to have relevant evidence in danger of loss or destruction. So it is better
to apply later (after execution) the usual 3-part test to the injunction provisions.¹
4. Procedure
Such orders are usually given ex parte (without notice),²
and a later review of the order on
notice (by one seeking to set aside the order) is de novo.³
On such a review, the court can look
at the evidence which was seized under the order.⁴
If an Anton Piller order got ex parte is challenged, the court will use a high standard to see
whether sufﬁcient disclosure was given to get the order.⁵
But original misstatements of immaterial
facts, or slight error in material facts, have no effect.⁶
On a motion to set the order aside, a big
factor is the procedural impact of the order, as its aim is to preserve evidence, especially where
there is danger that it will be destroyed.⁷
If an Anton Piller order is set aside,⁸
the court may order that all records thus obtained be
returned uncopied,⁹
and give higher costs for nondisclosure.¹⁰
If a chambers judge sets aside
such an order, and there was no actual search, an appeal from the original order may be moot.¹¹

6-128
R.6.26 PART 6: RESOLVING ISSUES AND PRESERVING RIGHTS
After an Anton Piller order is executed and then set aside on appeal, how the independent
supervising solicitor and the solicitor for the plaintiff were to get paid for this additional work, was
not the concern or responsibility of the defendants whose records had been seized. That and
other details could be sorted out by the case management judge.¹
For Anton Piller and interim injunction applications the court can choose a costs scale higher
than col.1. The conduct suggested for the Anton Piller order and injunction did exist, but on
examination was slight, justiﬁed or proper, harmless, and inadvertent. That counsel applying for
the two orders did not know all the facts is no excuse, especially on an ex parte application. His
client had the duty to disclose, and no excuse or explanation was offered. Solicitor-client costs
were therefore ordered, this applicant having known full well its duties from other earlier litigation.²
Where the case management judge ordered expert examination of a computer seized under
an Anton Piller order, the topics which he directed the expert to investigate limit the topics
relevant for the judge’s later decision.³
A non-party who deliberately broadcast a conﬁdential report given to the judge about execution
of an Anton Piller order, and refused to tell the judge where and how he got a copy, was guilty
of contempt.⁴
On spoliation (destroying evidence), see R.5.16n.⁵
Inspection or Examination of Property
6.26 On application, the Court may make one or more of the following orders:
(a) an order to inspect property, including an inspection by a judge
or jury, or both, at trial, if the inspection is advisable to decide a
question in dispute in an action, application or proceeding;
(b) an order to take samples, make observations or undertake
experiments for the purpose of obtaining information or evidence,
or both;
(c) an order to enter land or premises for the purpose of carrying out an
order under this rule.⁶

Related Provisions
5.6(2) (c) (stating time when records may be inspected) ; 5.13 (obtaining records from third

parties); 5.14 (examining records) ; 5.41 (medical examination) ; 6.40 (court experts) ; 13.30
(authenticated photographs of property)
The general topic of inspection of places and things is discussed in Harris’ text.⁷

On Anton Piller orders, see R.6.25n.
This Rule combines former Rules for pre-trial inspection of property, and inspection by the
court during trial.
The courts are liberal in giving orders for inspection of property.¹
But the court will not allow
dangerous or harmful tests or sampling. Or the court would at least ensure that all parties had
equal access to, and control over, destructive testing of evidence.²
Gauze found inside a post-
operative patient needed destructive testing to ﬁnd its origin, and as the parties could not agree
on who would run the tests, the court selected an expert under R.6.40.³
Paras. (b) and (c) of this Rule are independent. The plaintiff can have someone attend and
observe only, not to obtain test results or reports, nor interfere. It is at the plaintiff’s expense. A
previous inspection is no bar to this inspection.⁴
There is no bar to resorting to inspection under
R.6.26 even before the end of questioning (examinations for discovery).⁵
A search warrant in prescribed form by a competent authority gives a good defence (to a
peace ofﬁcer executing it) against civil or criminal action, even if it was bad in fact and (since)
quashed.⁶
Presumably the same is true of orders for testing. Given the advent of the Charter,
further research on that topic might be fruitful.
A trial judge took a long aerial view of a transmission line.⁷
A trial judge who takes a view can
use what he or she sees as evidence.⁸
In a paternity contest in court, an order that one party submit to DNA testing is a sort of
discovery and is not a “remedial order” subject to the 2-year deadline in the Limitations Act.⁹
Provisions in privacy legislation about conﬁdential documents’ disposition at the end of
proceedings, do not apply to courts or the contents of their ﬁle, because the entire Act is stated
not to apply to information in a court ﬁle, or a record of a justice or an applications judge or a
judicial administration record, etc.¹⁰
Notice Before Disposing of Anything Held by the Court
6.27(1) On application, the Court may direct that money or other personal
property held by the Court not be paid out or disposed of without notice being
served on the applicant.

DIVISION 4
RESTRICTION ON MEDIA REPORTING
AND PUBLIC ACCESS TO COURT PROCEEDINGS
Application of This Division
6.28 Unless an enactment otherwise provides or the Court otherwise orders, this
Division applies to an application for an order
(a) to ban publication of court proceedings,
(b) to seal or partially seal a court ﬁle,
(c) permitting a person to give evidence in a way that prevents that
person or another person from being identiﬁed,
(d) for a hearing from which the public is excluded, or
(e) for use of a pseudonym.

Related Provisions

1 Imp. Oil v. Info. & Privacy Comm’r. , R.6.26n.

6-131
PART 6: RESOLVING ISSUES AND PRESERVING RIGHTS R.6.29
Restricted Court Access Applications and Orders
6.29 An application under this Division is to be known as a restricted court
access application and an order made under this Division is to be known as a
restricted court access order.

A. Publication Bans
There are strict criteria for a court-ordered ban on publishing court proceedings: the ban
must be necessary to prevent a serious risk to the administration of justice, there must be no
reasonable alternative measure, and the beneﬁts of the ban must outweigh the bad effects on the
interests of the parties and the public, such as free expression, public trials, open judicial system
and efﬁcacy of administration of justice.¹
Stigmatized medical conditions, and sexual assaults, often get privacy orders.²
A statutory ban on publication of bail hearings is constitutional.³
There is prima facie Charter protection for journalists’ interviews, photographs, and ﬁlming.
However, Rules of Court can put on limits, in order to foster serenity of hearings, foster decorum,
and reduce nervousness and anxiety of witnesses or potential witnesses. Where a journalist makes
an audio recording of a hearing in court, the journalist may use that to increase the accuracy of
his or her report, but cannot use it in a way which would have an impact on the testimony itself.
Broadcasting such recordings in the name of freedom of the press would undermine the integrity
of the judicial process. The Rules of Court’s restrictions did more good than harm and were
upheld.⁴
It does not matter whether the person in question was the ﬁrst to raise the topic in their
pleadings, or was merely responding after others pleaded it.⁵
A ban on publication may not be necessary, despite presumptive harm from publicity, if there
is already an order to use initials, not full name, for the person concerned.⁶

There are tests for court-ordered bans on broadcasts or publication of ﬁction which might
prejudice a pending criminal jury trial, especially in light of the Charter rights of the accused and
of the media, which are equal in status. These tests may apply to orders banning publication of
evidence in a trial.¹
For a discussion of pretrial publicity where a pending criminal case might
be tried by a jury, see the Phillips case.²
A ban on publication of information, made to prevent
prejudice to an upcoming trial, should be lifted once the suit is stayed so that there will be no trial,
especially where the information would all have become public had there been a trial.³
On publishing court records or exhibits, see Part C below.
The law on publicity about events in closed courtrooms is reviewed, especially as it applies to
a search warrant against an innocent person and dangers to a fair trial. A total ban may not be
necessary, and edited production of information may be ordered.⁴
An injunction was upheld because interlocutory and the case for a breach of a non-publication
order was arguable, without ﬁnally deciding the point (on whether an electronic link to an old
posting is a new publication).⁵
An electronic link to an existing older publication is not a new publication of the older material,
and so does not violate an intervening injunction.⁶
Policy is discussed at length.⁷
B. Closed Courtroom
The Court of King's Bench website has a prominent link entitled “Public and Media Access”. It
leads to about 15 court documents from the “News and Announcements” part of the website, about
access to courtrooms and court ﬁles, and what cannot be reported or published. Under Public and
Media Access, the Court of King’s Bench website now has a link to the Court Information Access
Guide for Alberta which sets out situations when restrictions on access or publication may affect
the public and the media. There are other links available on the Public and Media Access page
as well.
The right of spectators and the media to attend a trial is protected by s.2(b) of the Charter.⁸
Courts should be open to the public. There may be a mistrial if the doors to the building or the
courtroom were inadvertently locked during a trial.⁹

Judges can cut back that right to attend and watch, if need is shown, by crafting terms and
conditions, having regard to all the competing interests.¹
In Donovan v. Sherman Est. ,²
the
Supreme Court made a number of statements about protecting privacy interests from disclosure
through court proceedings. The law was laid out and it was applied in C. B. C. v. R.³
In the Sherman
decision, this was all obiter, as the invasion there was held not to have serious enough likely
consequences to the holders of the privacy rights. But in the 2023 C. B. C. decision, it was clearly
ratio decidendi.⁴
The 2023 C. B. C. decision reafﬁrmed the standard test for a non-publication
order about court proceedings: serious risk to important public interest, necessity to prevent that
risk (no alternative measures would work), and more beneﬁts than detriments from proposed
ban. It would be very unusual to exclude the public from a jury trial on an important charge,
even if there have been past inadvertent breaches of publication bans.⁵
Legislation on privacy
for complainants triggers that, but s.1 of the Charter saves it. But the Trial Judge must apply
the legislation carefully, and look for alternatives to exclusion, and limit exclusion. Evidence is
necessary to get an order for exclusion.⁶

closed doors.⁷
The court may have inherent power to exclude from a divorce or access hearing
the children of the marriage?⁸
Closing the courtroom was not needed to protect sensitive health information; more speciﬁc
restrictions would sufﬁce, even though the other party was self-represented.⁹
C. Access to Court Files
1. Principles
The common-law rule is that all papers ﬁled in the Clerk’s ofﬁce are open to inspection by all
members of the public. That includes court pleadings, written arguments such as factums, and
ﬁled evidence (appeal records, transcripts, or exhibits which have been ﬁled at a trial).¹⁰
There
really seem to be only ﬁve exceptions to this right of inspection:
1. Reasonableness and convenience of hours or access to documents, when court
resources are limited or when court staff themselves need access to the documents.
2. There is no right of access to documents which have been marked for identiﬁcation only
and then ruled inadmissible and not admitted as exhibits.¹¹

3. The court has a limited power to keep certain evidence or papers conﬁdential where
necessary, i. e. there will be grave harm without such a sealing order,¹
and all competing

and there is no narrower restriction which will sufﬁce.³
One
may compare the general principles when the court may keep oral proceedings private.⁴
On the Supreme Court of Canada’s principles, see Part B above.
4. Where there is express legislation requiring that court ﬁles be sealed, e. g. in adoption
matters.⁵
5. Where (civil) litigation has ended without a judgment, e. g. by settlement and the parties
want their evidence back, as it still belongs to them, the privacy given by ﬁling in court is
temporary, and openness refers to Judges and court processes, not to litigants' affairs.⁶
Usually a ban on publication of evidence should only last one year, but a later application to
extend it (for ofﬁcer safety) can be made with more evidence.⁷
Conversely, a publication ban can
be set aside by a timely motion by the media or other affected person who never got notice of the
request for the ban, or where the circumstances have materially changed.⁸
If documents have to be ﬁled electronically with the court, whose system will seal only whole
documents, not partial documents, a sealing order does not itself bar use of the sealed document's
legitimate use in later ﬁlings, if the party wishing to use it already legitimately possesses copies of
those documents; but if they are privileged someone not owning the privilege cannot so use the
documents, because of the privilege (not because of the sealing order).⁹

PART 6: RESOLVING ISSUES AND PRESERVING RIGHTS R.6.29
2. Examples
Courts have protected secrets or privacy by replacing a bulky exhibit with photocopies of only
the relevant entries or pages,¹
sealing the court ﬁle,²
allowing a broadcast of a videotape exhibit
only with a face blurred,³
an anonymized style of cause to protect names,⁴
and by letting a lawyer
use a pseudonym for protection from abusive self-represented litigants.⁵
But there is no need for sealing or conﬁdentiality where publication contrary to earlier bans was
inadvertent,⁶
court decisions in question were worded to protect privacy,⁷
more recently the party
originally applying abandoned the proceedings,⁸
or the document or ﬁle in question discloses no
stolen information, even if the suit is about such a theft.⁹
Solicitor-client privilege is enough to found a sealing order, but the media should get notice;
the parties cannot waive the media's rights.¹⁰
After a judge found privilege and an appeal was
launched, the privileged documents were put into the public Extracts of Key Evidence and a
detailed appendix to a factum. Yet R.14.38 says that restricted access orders in the trial court
continue during the appeal.¹¹
The Court of Appeal’s new electronic ﬁling system permits sealing
an entire ﬁled document, but not mere passages. The Court of Appeal sealed big parts of its ﬁle.
Absent a claim of privilege, whether a record produced in discovery of records may be redacted
is a fact-speciﬁc inquiry? (Personal ﬁnancial records were in question. ) If the plaintiff can compare
and see what is redacted, he has an onus to show redaction is improper?¹²
Where an afﬁdavit in a lawsuit annexes documents got from the other side, despite R.5.33,
and despite great media interest, and despite a consent by both parties, and despite the fact that
the documents include personnel records, the court should be slow to impose a sealing order.¹³
Rules of Court about broadcasting recordings of proceedings were interpreted as conﬁned to
sounds heard during the hearing in court. They did not extend to a video recording in evidence, of
some previous event. Hearings and exhibits are distinct. An exhibit is not part of the proceedings,

and is always created before the proceedings. Access to exhibits is a corollary of the open court
principle. The Charter therefore gives presumptive rights to broadcast electronic exhibits, but the
court must weigh that against impacts on fairness of trial.¹
A recorded prior inconsistent statement
is not open to public view just because the trial judge marked it for identiﬁcation during cross-
examination of the person who had been recorded; most of the recording was not played in court.²
An infant's settlement contained a conﬁdentiality clause, but needed court approval. The law
favors settlements, and the court needed some ﬁnancial information, so the court did not close
the ﬁle or proceedings, but gave directions on how to reapply.³
A claimant cannot seek anonymity just to conceal the fact that she was previously involved
in a particular occupation (exotic danger). The open court principle requires transparency. (The
claimant took the option of abandoning her claim. )⁴
Loss of government contracts by being a witness in a suit against the government was held not
to be enough ground for use of a pseudonym, apart from need for clear evidence of the danger.⁵
Where the opposing party was self-represented, special restrictions were devised.⁶
D. Deliberative Secrecy
A tribunal has “deliberative” secrecy for the content of, and substance of, its decision making
and deliberations, but not for the formal process which it established to ensure consistency.⁷
Appeals and judicial review never get copies or descriptions of private notes or discussions, or
drafts, by the decision-maker below. This privilege also extends to the administrative aspects, or
at least those directly affecting adjudication, such as how members are assigned to particular
panels.⁸
Anything at the heart of or integral to the decision-making process is covered, including
notes which a tribunal member makes during a proceeding to assist himself or herself to reach a
decision and prepare reasons.⁹
Deliberative secrecy has been extended from courts to administrative decision-makers.¹⁰
But
tribunal deliberative secrecy is not so rigid as that for courts, and it might be lifted if a litigant
presented by evidence, valid reasons to believe that the process followed did not meet the rules
of natural justice, though mere speculation is not a valid reason.¹¹
E. Legislation
On notice to the media of such applications, see R.6.32.
Legislation barring publication of names does not bar use of real names in pleadings or
judgments. The presumption is in favour of open courts, and the mere desire for privacy is not

6.30 A person may ﬁle a restricted court access application only if the Court
has authority to make a restricted court access order under an enactment or at
common law. [Alta. Reg.124/10;194/20]

This Rule sounds harmless, but its usefulness is not obvious. Maybe it is directed at odd

6.33.

(a) ﬁle the application in Form 32, and
(b) unless the Court otherwise orders, serve every party and any other

An application to a trial judge to bar publication and to seal the ﬁle (in a criminal case) was
probably just handed to the trial judge by defence counsel. It certainly was not notiﬁed to any of
the media, despite repeated objections by Crown counsel, contravening the Rules.²
Getting an order to seal a court ﬁle without notifying the media is not always fatal; the judge
hearing the motion has the ultimate power over notice. Rule 6.28 is only a default mode if the
judge does not order otherwise. Sealing here was to preserve solicitor-client privilege, which is
very important and on the face of things, existed. The issue was about its waiver, not its existence.³

6.33 A restricted court access application must be heard and decided by

judge for the action, or
(c) if there is no judge or applications judge available to hear the
application as set out in clause (a) or (b), the Chief Justice or a judge
designated for the purpose by the Chief Justice.
[Alta. Reg.124/10;194/20;136/22]

6-142
R.6.37(8) PART 6: RESOLVING ISSUES AND PRESERVING RIGHTS
A. References
Generally on admissions, see the Ferguson book.¹
B. Effect of Admissions
Admissions must be about facts, not law. Counsel cannot bind the court to propositions of law.²
The court is not bound by an admission by a party on a question of law, particularly an admission
as to jurisdiction.³
Agreed facts are not merely evidence; they are incontrovertible, even by additional evidence,
even if there had been accident or inadvertence, or new evidence found.⁴
Most civil cases and R.
6.37(6) say that one can only withdraw one’s earlier admission with the leave of the court, and
that any prejudice occasioned must be compensated. Prejudice not compensable in costs may
bar withdrawal.⁵
There may be a distinction between formal and informal admissions.
But the court need not accept what is stated in a Response to a Notice to Admit Facts; that
would be unsworn evidence foisted on an unwilling opposing party.⁶
Admissions are a very important part of litigation, obviating evidence and speeding up trials.⁷
Admissions are an important aim of pre-trial case management and questioning (examinations for
discovery).⁸
Rule 6.37 is especially important if a witness is busy or far away.
An admission is not evidence against a co-party, even one alleged to have acted in concert.⁹
A statement of claim issued by accused’s lawyer after an accident was not admissible against
the accused in a criminal prosecution over the same accident, as unduly prejudicial. Though it is
a court document bearing the court's seal, that does not make it proof of the truth of its contents.

Sanderseon.
2 R. v. Gibson 2015 ABCA 41, 593 AR 380 (joint submission on sentencing). On admissions as to law not
binding the court, see also: G. C. v. T. F. (Vignaux-Fires v. Charron) [1987] 2 SCR 244, 257-58, 78 NR 214;
R. v. Sappier (Gray) 2006 SCC 54, [2006] 2 SCR 686.

6-143
PART 6: RESOLVING ISSUES AND PRESERVING RIGHTS R.6.37(8)
Nor was it (without more) an admission, especially as the signiﬁcance could not be foreseen at
the time of its issue.¹
Where a notice to admit was served and there was no reply, so that there is a deemed
admission, a party wishing to withdraw its deemed admissions is seeking an indulgence.²
The
issue is not whether the facts should have been admitted, but rather the failure to deal with the
question promptly. Particularly where there was no inadvertence or accident in failing to reply
earlier, the request to withdraw must get over several hurdles. First, a serious issue as to whether
the admission is true.³
Second, that withdrawal would not cause any substantial irreparable
prejudice to the party opposite.⁴
Third, if the failure to deny was a conscious decision, there must
be a substantial and exemplary costs penalty. Even if it is inadvertent, thrown-away costs must
be paid.⁵
The terms should be ﬂexible, imaginative, and truly compensatory, e. g. as to evidence
or damages. If the decision to admit was conscious and without reasonable excuse, it becomes
far harder to show a triable issue.⁶
But an admission at an earlier stage (deliberate or inadvertent)
may be easier to withdraw promptly?⁷
It is easier to give leave to withdraw an admission if it was deemed, not express (e. g. under a
notice to admit not replied to), delay is explained (absence and illness), there is no undue delay,
and no prejudice, the admissions involved law and expert opinions, not bare facts, and the points
are now explained.⁸
C. Notices to Admit
There is a general discussion of the authorities on notices to admit.⁹
A notice to admit is deemed to be agreed to, unless denied in writing within 20 days. That
time could be extended under R.13.5.¹⁰
A denial and an objection to a Notice to Admit, are very
different.¹¹

6-144
R.6.37(8) PART 6: RESOLVING ISSUES AND PRESERVING RIGHTS
A reply to a notice to admit is not sufﬁcient if it merely repeats the words of R.6.37(3) (b) (ii),
adding nothing else.¹
Rule 1.2 calls for openness, efﬁciency, and resolving the real issues. A
notice to admit must yield an answer fairly meeting the substance of the requested admission. A
judge after the trial must have enough information about an objection to the notice to admit.²
Prolixity does not render such a notice invalid.³
A notice to admit should not be used to
bypass the Rules on use of expert reports, to prove agreements between counsel, or to introduce
debatable matters of opinion?⁴
If a party does not expressly answer a notice to admit, but moves (applies) formally to set it
aside, there is no silence and so no deemed admission.⁵
A notice to admit given for an interlocutory proceeding is not necessarily bad.⁶
A motion to set aside a notice to admit must be decided under the new Rules, even if that
notice to admit was given before the new Rules came into force.⁷
Often an admission (deemed or actual) would materially advance the action (under R.4.33),
but not if the fact admitted was not in contention.⁸
And there is no advance if the recipient refuses
to admit.⁹
A notice to admit cannot be validly used after the trial begins.¹⁰
D. Costs for Not Admitting
Where a notice to admit authenticity and admissibility of documents at trial was denied, the
only practical way to prove those topics was by a very expensive commission hearing in Arizona,
and the party not admitting had to pay the costs of that, including travel expenses and the fees of
the commissioner.¹¹
There are usually costs penalties for failure to admit what one’s opponent then had to prove
(and did) at trial, and those usually go beyond ordinary party-party costs if one’s opponent wins
the trial.¹²
Rule 10.33(2) (b) on costs for refusing to make admissions is another important Rule
which often counsel unfortunately ignore, especially as the penalty is now not really in R.6.37.

Footnote
1 MacKenzie v. Gregory Est.2022 ABQB 521, 56 Alta LR(7th) 137.
2 MacKenzie v. Gregory, supra (¶’s 17 ff. ).
3 Ironstone v. Jansen, supra.
4 T. S. v. Stazenski (#1), supra (¶’s 11-14).
5 Andriuk v. Merrill Lynch, supra.
6 id. at ¶’s 18, 21-2, 24, 27.
7 Andriuk v. Merrill Lynch Can.2011 ABQB 59, 508 AR 114 (¶’s 9-11).
8 Davidchuk v. Wolansky (M) 2002 ABQB 409.
9 Krieter v. R. (Alta. ) (M) 2014 ABQB 349, 590 AR 109.
10 T. S. v. Stazenski (#1), supra. Quaere.
11 Crowe Mackay & Co. v. Ferry (#2) 2020 ABQB 280, JCE 1203 15920 (Apr 23) (¶’s 18-27).
12 Pettipas v. Klingbeil (#2) 2000 ABQB 988, 276 AR 24; Wainwright (Town) v. G-M Pearson Env’l. Mgmt.
(#2) 2008 ABQB 446, 447 AR 346, affd 2010 ABCA 23, 469 AR 383. Or the scale of costs otherwise payable
may be reduced: Edmonton (City) v. Lovat Tunnel Equip.2002 ABQB 1033, 328 AR 314. It is suggested
that one should not have to admit what one does not know, in Ironstone v. Jansen, supra. Quaere; this
largely destroys the usefulness of the procedure? A party will not be an eyewitness to many of the key facts.
It is common to accompany a notice with inadmissible evidence such as a photocopy of a paper, or a “will-
say” statement from a witness. If the recipient of the notice will not admit it, he (and not his opponent) should
run the risk and expense of bringing a witness from Timbuktu to prove the fact in question: Patterson v.
Hryciuk 2005 ABQB 136, 45 Alta LR(4th) 240 (¶’s 29, 31-2).

6-145
PART 6: RESOLVING ISSUES AND PRESERVING RIGHTS R.6.38(1)
But the court may refuse to impose any costs for failure to admit what the plaintiff proved later
at trial, if the notice came only 5 days before trial, and the topic was complex and technical.¹
But
if the Notice to Admit is so structured that the recipient is entitled to refuse to admit, then costs
need not be awarded.²
But even after a trial it is hard for the trial judge to know which costs should be attributed to
proving facts not admitted, especially if legal admissions were sought. The Court of Appeal has
shown the dangers of ﬁnding excuses for non-admission and letting belated admissions or proof
supposedly remove any harm from not admitting and so bar any extra costs. And R.6.37(2)
(b) (ii) can work only if the basis for the objection is disclosed. Only the party asked to admit
knows the basis for the objection. For example, is the objection irrelevance, impropriety, lack of
necessity, ambiguity, generality, legal questions, an opinion on a highly specialized subject, lack
of independent knowledge, etc? That must be made clear.³
In costs, only one fee is allowed for a notice to admit, even if it is served on more than one
party.⁴
The Rules do not conﬁne costs for notices to admit to the party serving the notice, and costs
may be allowed to the other (receiving) side, if it takes a fair amount of labour to answer an
involved notice to admit.⁵
Requiring Attendance for Questioning
6.38(1) On application, the Court may order a person to attend for questioning, at
a date, time and place speciﬁed by the Court, if the person
(a) is required to be questioned under these rules,
(b) was served with a notice of appointment for questioning in Form 29
under these rules,
(c) was provided with an allowance, determined in accordance with
Schedule B [Court Fees and Witness and Other Allowances], if so
required by these rules, and
(d) did not attend the appointment, attended the appointment but
refused to answer or fully answer proper questions or did not bring
a record required to be brought to the questioning.
(2) The Court may order the person to be questioned to bring records to the
questioning that the person could be required to produce at trial.⁶

A litigant with health issues did show that attending a questioning (examination for discovery)

greater harm, and delay the lawsuit or motion pending. So that litigant had to attend, but for only
two-hour sessions on three different days in one month.¹

RESOURCES TO ASSIST THE COURT

Court Experts
Appointment of Court Expert
6.40(1) The Court may appoint a person as a court expert to give evidence on a
matter.
(2) The court expert must give independent evidence to the Court.
(3) If possible, the parties must agree on the court expert to be appointed under
subrule (1).
(4) The appointment of a court expert does not affect the right of a party to call
the party’s own expert as a witness.⁴

See rules starting at rule 5.41 [Medical examinations] for the conduct of medical examinations

Related Provisions
5.34 (expert reports) ; 5.41 (medical examinations) ; 6.26 (examining property) ; 6.44(d)

(expert referee) ; 10.54 (medical examination following contempt)
It is a mystery why Alberta counsel and judges so rarely use this Rule. Many suits force a
technically untrained judge to choose between two conﬂicting largely incomprehensible experts
or live witnesses.¹
Citing this passage and a recent King's Bench decision, the Court of King's
Bench appointed a court expert to value one spouse’s income and the other spouse’s business.²
The family law Bar often get independent assessments for custody by an agreed psychologist;
presumably that is ordered under this Rule.³
That procedure is very helpful, even vital.
A chambers judge properly used R.6.40 to get an independent valuation of the land (as the
parties’ evidence conﬂicted. )⁴
A trial judge appointed an independent psychiatrist to help him
evaluate conﬂicting medical evidence.⁵
But appointing a court accounting expert was rejected in
favor of giving money to one party to hire her own.⁶
Where destructive testing was necessary and the parties could not agree on who or how, the
court appointed an expert to do the testing and report on its results.⁷
Counsel was personally ordered to pay costs of a court-appointed expert. The counsel
abandoned in argument the issue on which the trial judge felt he needed the expert.⁸
An 80-year-old driver sued after a collision, and the defendant wanted an “independent
medical examination”, not for damages, but for liability. The court skipped the question of whether
Rr.5.41, 5.42, 5.43 and 5.44 would allow that, and ordered it under Rr.6.40, 6.41, 6.42, 6.43 (on
a court expert).⁹
After an Anton Piller order, the case management judge ordered an expert examination (and
report) on a computer seized under the previous order.¹⁰

6.41(1) If the parties do not agree on the directions or instructions to be given or
questions to be put to a court expert, the Court may decide what directions or
instructions are to be given or questions are to be put to the court expert.²

not.
(3) The court expert’s report
(a) must be in writing, veriﬁed by afﬁdavit,
(b) must be served on the parties by the court clerk, and
(c) is admissible in evidence.³

Related Provisions

Application to Question Court Expert
6.42(1) Within 20 days after receipt of a copy of the court expert’s report, a party
may apply to the Court to question the court expert on the report.
(2) The Court may order the questioning of the court expert before or at a hearing
of an application or originating application or before or at trial.
(3) The questioning may take the form of cross-examination.⁴

Subdivision 2
Referees
Persons Who are Referees
6.44 The following are referees for the purposes of these rules:
(a) an applications judge;
(b) a court clerk;
(c) a person appointed as a referee by the Lieutenant Governor in
Council;
(d) a person appointed as a referee by the Court with the agreement of
all parties.¹
[Alta. Reg.124/10;136/22]

Related Provisions
6.40 (court experts) ; 6.47 (receivers) ; 6.9 (jurisdiction of [Applications Judges] ?Masters?)

Rule 6.44(a) duplicates the Court of King's Bench Act, s.11. There may be many reasons to
prefer hearing an issue by a referee rather than a judge. One reason may be to secure someone
who is available quickly, or someone who does not go out on circuit and so will be available in one
city from time to time.
Another reason may be to get someone with special expertise.²
For example, in the 1980’s
one Master in Toronto used to try all the builders’ lien cases. R.6.44(d) lets the parties agree on
anyone, such as an accountant or an engineer. This is an alternative to using a court expert under
R.6.40.
Someone seeking an accounting should ordinarily get production of his or her opponent’s
books of account from the outset of the lawsuit, and not have to wait for a judgment for accounting,
especially if the books would also be relevant to damages?³

The court may appoint an investigator of a condominium corporation (under the Condominium

Where a liquidator was also named by the court as investigator, conﬂicts of interest as liquidator

industry billing practices is needed, and evidence that disbursements are actually incurred and
not notional overhead charges.⁴
Both the Court of Appeal and the Court of King's Bench sometimes appoint a “special
commissioner” to hear evidence about and report on, some factual issue arising on an appeal,
e. g. whether trial counsel was competent. That person (sometimes a retired judge), is much like
a referee.

Related Provisions
7.1 (ordering trial of issues) ; 7.3(3) (b) (reference after summary judgment)

A. Introduction
The Rules speak of questions, inquiries, and taking accounts. But the concept and procedure
is basically the same:
1. The court issues an interlocutory order naming someone as a referee.²
2. That referee then holds a hearing and hears evidence. It is much like a trial, though it may
be less formal, and the procedure may vary somewhat. For example, all the evidence
from both sides on one topic or item may be heard before going onto the next topic
or item. Rule 6.45(2) (c) lets the referee take a view. There should be a record of the
evidence and proceedings before the referee.³
This is often done in one of the ordinary
courtrooms.
3. Then after the hearing, the referee makes a report to the court (R.6.46) recommending
what fact ﬁndings should be made, and maybe what legal results should ﬂow from them.
4. Any party can then move under R.6.46 to have the court adopt the report and make it the
court’s judgment, or to vary the report, or to reject the report.
An order for a reference to a Master to report on damages caused by an interlocutory injunction
ordered that there be oral evidence like that at a trial, with prior discovery of documents and
expert statements, and a week was reserved.⁴
When (after giving summary judgment) the court refers damage assessment to a referee, it
keeps control and can later determine the basis for assessing the damages.⁵
This procedure has been used only sporadically in Alberta in recent years, but is very
common in some jurisdictions. In the 1980s, Toronto Masters used to hear many long references.⁶
References are particularly suitable where a lot of vouchers or other documents have to be sifted,
or accounting has to be performed.
It is doubtful that the Alberta Rules put any restriction on what topics may be referred to
a referee, and a host of Rules authorize it for speciﬁc topics as well. However, s.96 of the
Constitution Act 1867 has been interpreted as preventing the court from referring all of a lawsuit

solicitor-client cost: ibid.
6 Examples of such references which have been ordered are found in the C. P. E. , Chapter 49, Parts A-G.

R.6.46(1) PART 6: RESOLVING ISSUES AND PRESERVING RIGHTS
to a referee who is not federally appointed, or from giving the referee the power to decide ﬁnally
without conﬁrmation by the court.¹
B. Procedure
An accounting is not a separate cause of action, but is merely a way to quantify proprietary
relief, and if that proprietary relief has already been given by an earlier judge, cause of action
estoppel does not prevent a new claim to get that accounting, particularly when the earlier
judgment declared the person from whom the accounting is sought, to be a trustee for the plaintiff.²
A dissenting judge (citing law) felt that a pleading calling for an order directing the opposite
party to account, contemplated a 2-step procedure, so that reaching a ﬁnal dollar amount at
trial was unfair. But the majority held that it was fair here, because a pretrial correspondence,
the claimant’s opening statement at trial, and written argument after close of evidence, all gave
enough warning to lead the evidence, at trial.³
Where a reference to ﬁx damages is ordered on appeal, the costs may be conditional on
whether the damages exceed the amount paid into court by the defendant.⁴
There were difﬁculties in wording a formal judgment, including the trial judge’s decision on the
main points, so then there was an agreed reference to the experts to settle amounts and details.⁵
C. Miscellaneous Ofﬁcials
The Criminal Code s.490(15) lets the court allow “a person who has an interest in what is
detained” to examine goods seized under a search warrant. The Minister qualiﬁed. Privilege could
be a bar. The judge would appoint a quasi-referee to examine the documents (or the parties could
select someone).⁶
Where the court has appointed an inspector under the Business Corporations Act, it can
receive opinion evidence from him without anyone’s complying with R.5.35.⁷
There is a discussion of a “claims ofﬁcer” in a receivership, and the procedure for him and
appeals/conﬁrmation of his decisions. This is an ofﬁce created by a judge’s orders, not by
legislation.⁸

referred to the referee, and a copy of the report must be ﬁled and served on the
parties to the question or matter.
(2) After the referee’s report has been served, a party may apply to the Court for
an order
(a) adopting the referee’s report in whole or in part;

Related Provisions
7.1 (ordering trials of issues)

Where a referee was to take accounts and answer questions based on the existing evidence,
not taking any more evidence, it is no objection that the referee had the mechanical work done by
his clerk (his son), if the referee examined all evidence and made all the decisions.²
A plaintiff suing
for an account is entitled to a judgment for a sworn account with vouchers from the defendant,
even if the defendant has given an unsworn one.³
When there is an interlocutory order for an account of mesne proﬁts, or damages (in a speciﬁc
performance action), it can go ahead even after ﬁnal judgment for possession, if only under Rr.
9.12 or 9.14?⁴
Apparently the court will accord the referee’s fact ﬁndings the deference usually given by
appeal courts to trial judges’ fact ﬁndings.⁵
A report of a court-appointed hearing ofﬁcer gets some
deference on appeal.⁶
One could appeal to the Supreme Court of Canada from an Exchequer
Court judge’s order conﬁrming a report of a referee, even if there was no timely appeal from the
referee’s report.⁷
In a bankruptcy, a trustee's report should be accepted at face value.⁸
Such an ofﬁcer of the
court should be helpful to the court, not adversarial, and present facts impartially.⁹
A Master sitting as a referee gave a surprisingly narrow scope for production of records, but
the exact procedural context is not clear.¹⁰

6-154
R.6.47 PART 6: RESOLVING ISSUES AND PRESERVING RIGHTS
DIVISION 7
COURT-APPOINTED RECEIVER
Court-appointed Receiver
6.47 If a Court appoints a receiver other than under an enactment, the Court may,
in addition to a procedural order,
(a) prescribe the compensation payable to the receiver and who is to
pay it;
(b) require the receiver to provide security;
(c) require the receiver to ﬁle ﬁnancial accounts and reports with the
court clerk at the times and subject to the scrutiny ordered by the
Court;
(d) order payment to or disallow all or part of a payment to the receiver;
(e) order a hearing to be held with respect to any matter for which the
receiver was appointed or is responsible;
(f) make any other order or direction that the circumstances require.¹

Related Provisions
6.4 (ex parte applications; injunctions) ; 6.44 (referees) ; 7.1 (ordering trials of issues)

A. Introduction
See also the Civil Enforcement Regulation.²
Receivers³
used to be appointed in 3 different situations, which must not be confused:
(i) by way of equitable execution (to enforce a judgment) under former R.466; but that
has now been replaced by broader uniﬁed powers of execution under Alberta's Civil
Enforcement Act.⁴
(ii) under an express power to appoint given to a mortgagee in a debenture, as a means of
enforcing the security given over a business (private appointment under an instrument) ;⁵
but that is really outside the scope of books on civil procedure.
(iii) by the court, during a lawsuit, in order to preserve property or hold the status quo and
prevent part or all of the subject matter of the suit from disappearing before trial; this still
goes on.

Footnote
1 Quite similar to previous 1968 Rr.463, 464. They came from 1914 Rr.662 am. , 663, 664. And came via 1944
C. Rr.546, 547, 548. Cf.1883 O.50 rr.16, 18, 21. Cf. C. O. , O. XXIV rr. l, 2.
2 Alta. Reg.276/95, s.32 (receivers).
3 Note on the subject of receivers generally, C. P. E. , Chapter 55; Abrams and McGuinness, Canadian Civil
Procedure Law, pp.947 ff. (2d ed.2010), Bennett, Bennett on Receiverships (4th ed.2021) ; Van Kessel,
Interim Receivers and Monitors (LexisNexis 2006) ; Halsbury’s Laws of Canada - Receivers and Other Court
Ofﬁcers (LexisNexis Electronic Publication 2021).
4 But cf. dicta in Michel v. Lafrentz (#4) (CA 1999) 232 AR 82, 85, leave den (SCC 2000) 253 NR 199.
5 A privately-appointed receiver owes a duty only to the company, not to its creditors, though a derivative
action could be brought against it. Unless the creditors raise a wrong done to them as individuals, their suit
against it should be struck out: Hongkong Bank of Can. v. Fife (CA 9/26 Jan) 1998 ABCA 25.

6-155
PART 6: RESOLVING ISSUES AND PRESERVING RIGHTS R.6.47
Only situation (iii) is discussed in the following commentary.
B. Preserving Property
Getting a receivership order under s.13(2) of the Judicature Act is difﬁcult, but where the
applicant plainly is a major stakeholder, it may be done.¹
Here a law ﬁrm was insolvent (by one
test), and the Law Society sought the order to protect solicitor-client privilege, so a receiver
was proper. Otherwise a creditor of the law ﬁrm would endanger the rights of clients of the ﬁrm.
Solicitor-client privilege is very important, and cannot be breached by the rights of a secured
creditor.²
The usual three-part test for an interlocutory injunction was applied here by analogy
and all three parts were found satisﬁed. The receiver would have ample power to conduct interim
negotiations and payments.³
No other alternative remedy was viable.
Courts appoint receivers as an equitable remedy. The criteria for appointing a receiver are
cited.⁴
Here there was no risk to the creditor, the assets required little management, and did
not risk waste. The debtor consented to a receiver, but a receiver was not necessary; a consent
foreclosure would sufﬁce. So a receiver was denied.
There may be no rigid tests for when a receiver may be appointed during litigation?⁵
Maybe the
criteria for an interlocutory injunction (applied also to stays of execution pending appeal) form a
useful analogy.⁶
The court must balance the rights of the parties, and the party seeking a receiver
has the onus, and there are no presumptions, when the application is under the Judicature Act.⁷
Some authorities list 16 relevant criteria to weigh.⁸
But it must be a suit over property, not a mere debt action. There may be enough property
interest where the plaintiff claims that a mortgage or assignment of rents has given it a charge over
income. Indeed the Law of Property Act⁹
gives extra rights to get a receiver in those situations. A
receiver will often be appointed to collect rents or other periodical payments.¹⁰

Footnote
1 Law Socy. v. Higgerty, infra.
2 Law Socy. v. Higgerty, infra (¶’s 27 ff).
3 Law Socy. v. Higgerty 2023 ABKB 499, JCC 2301 03188 (Aug 31) (¶’s 27-78).
4 2531005 Alta. v. Katharine Ents.2023 ABKB 718, JCC 2301 15049 (Dec 14) (¶’s 19-21).
5 But the question is not entirely discretionary, and there must be some principle on which the receiver can
be appointed: Murphy Oil Co. v. Predator Corp.2002 ABQB 403, 316 AR 1. Tests for interlocutory relief
by receiver are given (much like those for an interlocutory injunction) : Kumra v. Luthra 2010 ABQB 772,
JDE 9703 10692, [2010] AR Uned 965 (Dec 3). The right to get a receiver does not depend on being a
“contingent creditor”. Where the applicant is a major stakeholder with respect to the operations and ﬁnancial

is a more important interest: Alta. Health Serv. v. Networc Health 2010 ABQB 373, [2010] 11 WWR 730, 28

9 RSA 2000 c. L-7 s.45. Before the predecessor to this legislation, only an equitable mortgagee could get

6-156
R.6.47 PART 6: RESOLVING ISSUES AND PRESERVING RIGHTS
A test much like the usual 3-part test for interlocutory injunctions was used to keep a receiver
in place pending appeal.¹
The test is balancing all the interests, and undue hardship (without a
receiver) is not needed; but a receiver is not lightly granted.²
If owners quarrel over a deteriorating and largely unused building, and will not cooperate,
nor repair or refurbish, a receiver is proper.³
Insolvency is not the only time when the court can
appoint a receiver under the Judicature Act; it was apparently done when a company was just a
fraudulent scheme to sell worthless shares to the public.⁴
Where there are parallel suits in Alberta
and another province, but land in Alberta has to be dealt with, a simple stay of the Alberta suit is
not possible, and a receiver-manager of the Alberta land may be necessary.⁵
An order to appoint a liquidator should not be delayed without a reason, even if there is no
evidence that the delay would cause great harm.⁶
C. Removing a Receiver
The test for removing an existing receiver is tougher than that for appointing one, particularly
where he has been acting for some time in a complex matter. A case required a large experienced
liquidator or receiver, there are not many of those in Canada, and all seem to be afﬁliated with
accounting ﬁrms. The standard of review on appeal is tough.⁷
It may be proper to question whether a receivership order was granted too soon, and lacked
any effort to keep the business going, without a real chance to pay, in a separate suit, so as
not to tie up the original receivership action for years. But the latter claim (not keeping alive) is
a collateral attack on the receivership order or the order approving the auction sale of assets,
unless fraud is pled. And if the damages could not possibly reach the amount owing, the action
is frivolous.⁸
D. Powers and Immunities of the Receiver
1. Power
A court-appointed receiver has whatever powers, over whatever property, that the court gives
it.⁹
In drafting such an order, it is thus critical to look at a good precedent, for the powers are often
quite extensive. If the court empowers the receiver to run a business, the ofﬁcial is often called a
receiver-manager.

Footnote
1 Nosratieh (Lindsey Est. ) v. Strategic Metals Corp.2009 ABCA 14 (company a possible sham to defraud
people and then hide the spoils).
2 BG Int. v. Cdn. Super. Enr.2009 ABCA 127, 457 AR 38 (receiver’s certiﬁcates only way to secure the only
party willing to advance cash to preserve the asset for beneﬁt of all parties).
3 Strategic Finl. Corp. v.1402801 Alta. , supra.

other company, the receivership may be amended to cover the other company also: Romspen Inv. Corp. v.

6-157
PART 6: RESOLVING ISSUES AND PRESERVING RIGHTS R.6.47
The order should not purport to alter priorities, nor otherwise decide the lawsuit.¹
A court-
appointed receiver should not assign the company into bankruptcy, unless there is clear proof that
investigation shows the company is insolvent, and that no creditor can or will petition.²
A receiver has status to apply for an attachment order (Mareva injunction) to preserve the
company’s assets.³
Or to sue to enforce trusts of which the company was trustee and get the
funds back from another trustee.⁴
The court had appointed a receiver and manager, and he was given permission to sue and
then put the lawsuit up for sale, indeed auctioning it off to those parties directly interested.⁵
A court
can authorize its ofﬁcer to undertake a suit, and to ﬁnance the suit from the estate.⁶
Whether or not
it is usually improper to start a lawsuit with no intention of taking it to trial, it is proper for a receiver
to start one in the expectation that one of the other parties will take it over, particularly if that has
since happened; the action should not be struck out on that ground alone.⁷
If the receiver sells the
business (which is very common), it is not champerty also to sell a lawsuit related to the business
which the company had started, where the same person buys both.⁸
The powers of a national receiver under the Bankruptcy and Insolvency Act do not clash with
provincial moratorium legislation on farms, and both Acts can operate together.⁹
2. Liability and Immunities
A court-appointed receiver is the agent of the court, not of the company or any of the other
parties to the suit, and as such probably has some inherent or implied immunities.¹⁰
But the order
of appointment often goes much further and gives broad immunities. Some orders bar any kind
of suit or exercise of rights by any person against the business in receivership; that seems very
dubious in principle, especially when those persons are not parties and had no notice of the
application.¹¹
A clause in a receivership order reducing the rights of non-parties will not bind them
because they got no notice; it is a poor practice to put in orders clauses purporting to bind absent
persons.¹²
Despite urgency and short time limits in insolvency proceedings, a Receiver still has basic
obligations to the parties and to the court, including secured claimants, guarantors, creditors,
contingent creditors, and shareholders. The receiver is a ﬁduciary to all interested.¹³

SCR 475, 5 NR 515. A trust condition in a receivership was enforceable, and the judge supervising had no
power to dispense with it: Locke Stock & Barrel Co. v. Bearcat Expl.2006 ABCA 368.

6-158
R.6.47 PART 6: RESOLVING ISSUES AND PRESERVING RIGHTS
The receiver owes a duty of care, and is liable for negligence.¹
It also has a duty to act honestly.²
Some sort of a prima facie case must be made out in order to get leave to sue a court-appointed
receiver, and the test is tougher if ground for the suit could have been raised earlier, e. g. in an
application to discharge the receiver as having fulﬁlled his mandate.³
The test for leave to sue
is not onerous, and the court does not ﬁnally assess the merits. But if the order appointing the
receiver said that the receiver would be liable only for gross negligence, that standard governs?⁴
Though a receiver is a court ofﬁcer, it is personally liable on contracts which it makes as a
receiver, unless the contract clearly provides otherwise.⁵
A professional receiver will therefore often
refuse to accept an appointment unless some solvent party guarantees its bills, and indemniﬁes
it against liability.
It would be unduly prejudicial to a court-appointed receiver later to give retroactive leave to
sue the receiver, after the receiver (to the plaintiff’s knowledge) sought and got court approval of
its actions, the plaintiff’s claims were asserted and rejected, and (most important) the funds were
disbursed.⁶
Usually leave is needed to sue a court-appointed receiver, but the test is easy: evidence of
some foundation for the claim, and that it is not merely frivolous or vexatious.⁷
Where res judicata
and limitation periods would likely defeat a proposed suit against a court-appointed receiver, and
success of the suit would make little difference given large secured claims, leave nunc pro tunc
to sue the receiver was denied, and so the statements of claim against it were struck out.⁸
Often the court order requires that the court approve certain steps by the receiver, such
as sales of property or compromises of lawsuits. And if it is doubtful whether the receiver has
power to take a certain step, court approval is wise. Furthermore, even where the receiver has an
undoubted power to make a decision, it can likely shield itself from a claim for negligence by ﬁrst
seeking advice and directions from the court. A court may decline to give advice and directions to
a receiver who merely wants legal advice, and should not bless an action if the right is likely to be
claimed in another jurisdiction whose courts may well not recognize our court’s order.⁹
Summary
decision of a request by a receiver for advice and directions is permissible, especially if no party
then objected to the procedure.¹⁰
3. Miscellaneous
A receiver must adduce evidence for every application which the receiver brings.¹¹
Court orders approving partial sales by a receiver create res judicata.¹²
The courts generally defer to a receiver’s expertise, and will not narrowly second-guess its
recommendations, e. g. to sell, and at what price.¹³
A receiver need not call for binding offers or

The court asked to approve a sale should consider
several things: whether enough has been done to get the best price, interests of all parties,

there was no initial misconduct.²

An earlier order in a receivership matter, establishing super-priorities, can be revisited or

On reports by trustees in bankruptcy, see R.6.46 n.
E. Company Continues
What is the basic nature of a court-appointed receiver-manager? As long as there is not
a liquidation, the company’s persona continues legally intact, as do its contracts. The receiver
merely controls the company and manages its business as an ofﬁcer of the court.⁵
The board of
directors can still function, but not get paid, even for helping the receiver.⁶
The debtor company
has no status to attack an order ending leases which the receiver has got.⁷
The receiver is the agent of the court, not of the company.⁸
On suits to which the company in receivership is a party, and who can run them, and the style
of cause, see the C. P. E.⁹

F. Fees, Procedure and Accounts
The criteria for a bankruptcy trustee's fees are reviewed, in the W. Logic case. Could this
apply to a receiver or liquidator?¹
Courts should take a careful look at fees for chartered accounting or insolvency ﬁrms appointed
by the court as monitors of some company, e. g. under the Companies’ Creditors Arrangement Act.
The approach should be like that in bankruptcy. Evidence of industry billing practices is needed,
and disbursements must be actually incurred and not notional overhead charges.²
It takes exceptional circumstances for the court to order cross-examination on the report to the
court by a court-appointed receiver; a desire to ﬁsh for misconduct is not enough.³
A receiver may ask the court for advice and directions about settlements or administration. But
if the request is really to adjudicate a dispute between the receiver and someone else, the court
may turn the application into a substantive one, since the receiver takes an adversarial position.
An application for advice and directions is not to be resorted to as a way to use the court as a
legal adviser.⁴
A judge gave an order that a receiver could distribute part of the money in hand, leaving
behind a big enough holdback for any possible claims not yet resolved.⁵
Rule 10.9 only covers agreements between a lawyer and his or her client, not agreements
between two non-lawyers. The court has a discretion over costs even when there is a contract
on the subject, and can disallow an award based on the contract, e. g. for unnecessary steps or
extraordinary measures.⁶
The court usually provides for payment of the fees and disbursements of its receiver out of
the property which the receiver holds or handles,⁷
and provides that the receiver will pass its
accounts. Propriety of an expenditure by a receiver is best decided on passing its accounts.⁸
It

A court-appointed Receiver retained an auctioneer, then later had to cancel the sale. That

DIVISION 8
REPLEVIN
Application of this Division
6.48 This Division applies to an application in an action
(a) for the recovery of personal property in which the applicant claims
that the property was unlawfully taken or is unlawfully detained, and
(b) in which the applicant seeks to repossess the personal property in
issue immediately, pending determination of the action described in
clause (a).⁵

Related Provisions
6.25 (preservation order) ; 6.56 (interpleader)

There was a common-law cause of action called replevin, but that is irrelevant here.⁶
Replevin
is now an interlocutory remedy, allowing a plaintiff to get possession of a chattel before trial or
[Footnote 8 continued from prior page]
evidence: MacDonald v. Taubner Est.2006 ABQB 138, 22 ETR(3d) 148. When the court appoints a judicial
trustee and sets his budget, it can later increase the budget for his legal expenses, if they are for the fund’s
beneﬁt and not for the trustee’s: A. T. A. v. Calg. Bd. of Educ. (#4) 2006 ABQB 817, 411 AR 320 (Nov 10) (¶’s
31-4, 43-5).

Footnote
1 Vague complaints by the debtor about the size of the fees lacked speciﬁcity and given the history, were of
dubious motivation: Servus C. U. v. Trimove 2015 ABQB 745, 2015 CarswellAlta 2169 (Nov 25).
2 Pacer Constr. Hldg. Corp. v. Pacer Promec Enr. Corp.2016 ABQB 697, JCC 1501 02652 (Dec 9).
3 Royal Bank v. Crowfoot Land &c.2021 ABQB 258, JCC 2001 084667 (Apr 6) (¶’s 29-36).

R.6.49(1) PART 6: RESOLVING ISSUES AND PRESERVING RIGHTS
ﬁnal judgment.¹
Where one has an action in detinue, then replevin which is related to the cause of
action may have a contemporary interlocutory remedy.
The remedy is only available where the plaintiff gives evidence that the chattel was unlawfully
taken from the plaintiff, or is unlawfully detained from him or her, and that the plaintiff is the true
owner or is entitled to legal possession. The courts have interpreted that fairly broadly in recent
years.²
Application for Replevin Order
6.49(1) A party may apply to the Court for a replevin order without serving notice
of the application on any other party unless the Court otherwise orders.
(2) The application for a replevin order must include in the application an
undertaking
(a) to conclude the action for recovery of the personal property without
delay,
(b) to return the personal property to the respondent if ordered to do so,
and
(c) to pay damages, costs and expenses sustained by the respondent
as a result of the replevin order if the applicant is not successful in
the action for recovery of the personal property and the Court so
orders.
(3) The application for a replevin order must be supported by an afﬁdavit that
(a) sets out the facts respecting the wrongful taking or detention of the
personal property,
(b) contains a clear and speciﬁc description of the personal property
and its value, and
(c) describes the applicant’s ownership or entitlement to lawful
possession of the personal property.³

Related Provisions
6.4 (ex parte applications) ; 6.4n. (injunctions and undertakings)

Replevin can always be given by a justice or Applications Judge, who may give it ex parte, or
direct that notice be given. Usually the plaintiff is required to give appropriate security before he
or she gets possession. The respondent to a replevin application can be ordered to post a bond
as a condition of denying replevin.⁴
There is a discussion of the degree of proof about the merits which is required, but the exact
test is not altogether clear.⁵

Footnote
1 Note on the subject of replevin generally, C. P. E. , Chapter 57.
2 See the C. P. E. , Chapter 53, Parts C and D.
3 Quite similar to previous 1968 R.428. It came from 1914 R.468; came via 1944 C. R.502. Cf. Judic. Ord. r.

6-163
PART 6: RESOLVING ISSUES AND PRESERVING RIGHTS R.6.50(1)
Replevin Order
6.50(1) A replevin order must
(a) include a clear and speciﬁc description of the personal property to
be repossessed,
(b) impose on the applicant the following duties:
(i) to conclude the action for recovery of the personal property
without delay, and
(ii) to return the personal property to the respondent if ordered
to do so,
(c) include a requirement to pay damages, costs and expenses sustained
by the respondent as a result of the replevin order if the applicant
is not successful in the action for recovery of the personal property
and if the Court so orders, and
(d) require the applicant to provide, to the person from whom the personal
property is to be repossessed, security in a form satisfactory to
the Court, which may include, without limitation, a bond, a letter of
undertaking or payment into Court.
(2) A replevin order may also include either or both of the following:
(a) an order to a civil enforcement agency to make a report on its
enforcement or attempted enforcement of the replevin order;
(b) the value of the personal property.
(3) If the replevin order is made without notice to the respondent, the Court must
specify a date, not more than 20 days after the date the order is granted, on
which the order will expire.
(4) The applicant must ﬁle an afﬁdavit or other evidence that the order requiring
security has been complied with.
(5) The replevin order is not effective until the afﬁdavit or evidence of the security
has been ﬁled and the afﬁdavit or evidence has been served on
(a) the respondent, and
(b) a civil enforcement agency.¹

Related Provisions
6.4 (ex parte applications)

Enforcement of Replevin Order
6.51(1) Only a civil enforcement agency has authority to enforce a replevin order.

(3) Unless the Court otherwise orders, if an order for possession of the personal
property to be given to the applicant is not made within one month after the
civil enforcement agency serves notice of taking possession of it, the civil
enforcement agency must return the personal property to the respondent.¹

10.52 (contempt)

Respondent May Apply for Remedy
6.52 If the respondent did not receive notice of the application for a replevin order,
or in any other circumstance with the Courtâ€™s prior permission, the respondent
may apply to the Court for an order
(a) to discharge or amend the replevin order,
(b) to stay the application for a replevin order,
(c) to return, keep safe or sell the personal property or any part of it, or
(d) for any other remedy relating to the personal property.²

Related Provisions
6.3 (applications) ; 6.25 (preservation order) ; 6.56 (interpleader) ; 9.15 (setting aside orders)

Expiry of Replevin Order
6.53 A replevin order
(a) obtained without notice to the respondent, expires in accordance
with its terms unless it is extended by the Court, or
(b) obtained after notice of the application for the replevin order was
served on the respondent, expires on the earlier of
(i) the dismissal of the action, and
(ii) 2 months after the date on which judgment in favour of the
applicant is entered.

Related Provisions
9.21 (extension of order) ; 13.5 (variation of time)

1 Quite similar to previous 1968 R.434. It came from 1914 R.474; via 1944 C. R.508. Cf. Ont.1897 Rr.365,
1076.
2 Quite similar to previous 1968 R.431. It came from 1914 R.470; via 1944 C. R.504. Cf. Judic. Ord. s.429(2),
Ont.1897 Rr.361, 1070.

6-165
PART 6: RESOLVING ISSUES AND PRESERVING RIGHTS R.6.54
DIVISION 9
INTERPLEADER
Deﬁnitions
6.54 In this Division,
(a) “applicant” means an originating applicant or applicant for an
interpleader order, as the context requires;
(b) “application for an interpleader order” means an application ﬁled
under rule 6.56 [Application for interpleader order], whether an
originating application or an application;
(c) “claimant” means a person who ﬁles or is expected to ﬁle an adverse
claim against personal property;
(d) “instructing creditor” has the same meaning as it has in the Civil
Enforcement Act;
(e) “personal property” includes a debt;
(f) “related writ” has the same meaning as it has in the Civil Enforcement
Act;
(g) “writ proceedings” has the same meaning as it has in the Civil
Enforcement Act.¹

Nature of Application for Interpleader Order
6.55(1) An application for an interpleader order must be ﬁled
(a) as an originating application if the applicant is not a party to an
action respecting the personal property that is the subject of the
application, or
(b) as an application if the applicant is a party to an action respecting
the personal property.
(2) An application for an interpleader order or an application to determine the
rights of the various claimants may be ﬁled under rule 6.56 [Application for
interpleader order] and an order may be made even if the respondent to the
application has provided security or an indemnity to the applicant.²

Footnote
1 Quite similar to previous R.442, which was ﬁrst enacted by Alta. Reg.277/95, to conform to the Civil
Enforcement Act.
2 Quite similar to previous 1968 R.443(7). It came from 1914 R.493, except that reference to a lien was new;
